Two tests added where the entry is present and merely out of reach, which is the only shape that distinguishes a refusal from a fallback; seven mutants now die. **Speech proposes and never performs** (§39): a closed verb set, a described proposal, confirmation before anything happens — because a spoken sentence is ambiguous by nature and has no natural confirmation step, so a misheard word must cost a declined proposal rather than a destroyed analysis. **And the recogniser is behind a seam for a reason that is not testing.** Chrome's `SpeechRecognition` streams microphone audio to Google. It is one line of code and it would break the first claim this project makes about itself — a researcher thinking aloud about unpublished data would be uploading it, with nothing in the interface to say so. Hand tracking was built the hard way for this reason and speech does not get an exception. A remote source must name who receives the audio or it is refused outright, and the default source is one that produces nothing and says why. Typed input is the same path, so the capability is reachable without a microphone at all. **No recogniser is connected yet** — that is the next piece, and it is a choice between a local model and a stated boundary rather than a default. |
| T048 | Closing the loop, and two bugs that only appear when it is closed | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Every piece of speech-plus-gesture was tested and the pieces did not add up to a feature: **the ink layer never told the timeline anything**, so every reference resolved to null while all 29 voice tests passed. `InkLayer` now opens a timeline entry at pen-down — not at commit, because by the time a stroke commits the word has already been said — and `/air-ink` closes it with whatever the loop caught. A command box on the page runs the whole path. **Verified live in a browser**: with nothing drawn it refuses, showing "Nothing was indicated around the time \"these\" was said", which is the behaviour that matters most and the one a fallback would have hidden. **Two bugs, both found by mutation rather than by writing tests.** Deleting the cleanup on `strokeCancelled` changed nothing, and chasing why showed the branch was **unreachable**: `strokeCancelled` is emitted from PEN_DOWN, a state reached *before* `penDown` is emitted, so no entry existed to abandon. The reachable leak had no handling at all — a stroke that begins drawing and then records fewer than two points (tracking lost, or every later frame rejected as a spike) is discarded by `commit`, so `onStroke` never fires and the entry opened at pen-down is never closed. An open entry is deliberately never forgotten, because a hand may rest mid-stroke, so **one such stroke would sit open for the rest of the session and capture every word spoken afterwards** under the "during" rule, binding each to an empty referent while reporting success. **And the console noise turned out to be a real defect.** Chrome refuses `navigator.vibrate` until the document has had a user activation, and refuses it by *logging an error and returning false* rather than by throwing — so the `try`/`catch` around it caught nothing and the page emitted one error per hover detent. That is not tidiness: the diagnostic story for hand tracking is that a researcher opens the console and reports what they see, and a page crying wolf several times a second makes a real error impossible to find. Gated on `userActivation.hasBeenActive`, with absence of the property treated as permission rather than refusal, since the engines lacking it never gated in the first place. |
| T049 | Two clocks, and the lag that was never measured | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Asked to make speech and gesture *sync*, which turned out to be the right thing to ask. **They were on different clocks, and the whole feature silently did nothing.** Hand frames are stamped with `performance.now()` — about 10,000, milliseconds since page load — and the command box stamped an utterance with `Date.now()`, about 1.76e12. Every word was therefore 55 years after every gesture, no reference could ever bind, and there was no error, no log and nothing on screen that looked wrong. The end-to-end test written an hour earlier passed throughout, because synthetic timestamps are consistent with themselves. `lib/spatial/clock.ts` now owns the one clock, and the argument for it is not precedent: **`Date.now()` can move backwards** — NTP corrections, daylight saving, a laptop waking with a stale clock — and a fusion timeline on a clock that jumps backwards binds a word to a gesture that had not happened yet, roughly once a fortnight, unreproducibly. `ReferenceTimeline` now throws on a cross-clock timestamp naming the fix, rather than resolving nothing for ever. **`tests/clock-discipline.test.ts` failed its own first test**, which is the part worth recording: it scanned the libraries and not `app/`, so the guard written for this bug would have watched this exact bug go past. `app/` is where two subsystems meet and therefore where clocks get mixed. Comments and string literals are excluded, so the error message telling a developer not to use `Date.now()` does not flag itself. **And lag is now a number instead of an assurance** (§149). `LatencyRecorder` reports p50, p95, p99 and the fraction over budget across a bounded window — three numbers rather than one because lag is felt as the moments it stops keeping up, and 49 frames at 20ms with one at 400ms averages to 27ms and feels broken. A negative sample is discarded rather than averaged in, since that is the one direction a latency figure must never be wrong in. Wired into the session after all per-frame work, read on `/gesture-check` at 2Hz, and honest about being a floor: it cannot see camera exposure or the compositor and says so. §149 forbids claiming zero milliseconds and this does not. |
| T050 | Measuring before optimising, and declining one requirement on purpose | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Reviewed the performance block — §50–§53, §149, §218, §219 — **160 → 154** unreviewed. **§53 is the real gap and it is now measured rather than asserted.** Nothing is off the UI thread: inference, the gesture machine and painting all share it, so whatever `detectForVideo` costs is time React and the canvas painter do not have, thirty times a second — and that surfaces as dropped frames *elsewhere on the page*, which is exactly why it cannot be read off the end-to-end figure. `/gesture-check` now reports hand-detection latency separately against an **8ms budget, half a 60Hz frame**, beside the 60ms end-to-end one. The two are budgeted apart so they can disagree: a tracker can sit comfortably inside 60ms end-to-end while eating most of every frame the renderer needed. Migrating inference to a worker is a real change with real risk, and it should be justified by a number rather than by the specification listing workers — so the number comes first. **§51 is declined, not missed.** It asks for interpolation between gesture states. Rendering is already independent of the tracker — rAF behind a dirty flag — but interpolating *adds* latency by definition, since it smooths by drawing a lagged position, and this subsystem has just been tuned in the opposite direction. The One Euro filter already smooths the input; interpolating on top would smooth it twice and trade away the responsiveness that was the whole complaint. Recorded as `partial` with the reasoning, because a future reader will otherwise implement it as an oversight. **§50 likewise**: no WebGL or WebGPU, and `scene3d.ts` has carried the argument since it was written — a scene graph is around 600KB for what is, at this scale, a 4x4 matrix and a sort. §218 came back genuinely `built`: inference runs inside the rAF tick on the freshest frame and the rate governor drops rather than queues, so no backlog can form because nothing is enqueued. |
| T051 | Undo, for the one action that actually destroyed work | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §42, §96 and §179. `lib/ink/history.ts`; 14 tests. The specification calls undo mandatory and gives the reason rather than leaving it as convention — spatial interaction raises the rate at which somebody does something they did not mean, because a pinch is a decision made by a hand that is also being used to gesture while talking, with a camera guessing at the boundary. **T046 recorded this as `not-built` and declined to build a camera-history stack**, on the grounds that rotating is reversible by rotating back and the destructive actions §42 worries about do not exist yet. That was half right: *Clear* exists, discards every annotation at once, and did so permanently. Forty marks made over twenty minutes gone to one misplaced press, with the interface offering nothing. **An undone clear hands back the same stroke objects**, asserted by identity in the tests, not strokes rebuilt from a description — a history that re-derives what it discarded will eventually re-derive it differently, and the researcher gets back something that *resembles* their annotation, which is worse than losing it because they will not notice. Order is preserved for the same reason a restored stroke goes back at its index rather than on the end: it would otherwise paint over marks that were above it. **The controls say what they would do** — "Undo clearing 12 strokes", not "Undo" — since after a few minutes of drawing the bare verb is not a decision anybody can make (§96). A pinch too short to be a mark is deliberately absent from the history, or the first press of undo would appear to do nothing and the real mistake would need two. Five mutants killed, including rebuilding strokes on restore and appending rather than splicing. `undo()` now returns whether anything was taken back rather than the stroke removed, because "the stroke that was removed" is not a meaningful answer to undoing a clear of twelve. **Still `partial`**: movement, filters, transformations and model changes are uncovered, and those actions do not exist in the gesture layer yet. |
| T052 | An annotation that knows it no longer corresponds | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §143 says never to treat every stroke merely as screen pixels, and every stroke here was screen pixels. The consequence was live in shipped behaviour: **circle a cluster, rotate the chart, and the ink stays while the marks move underneath** — a loop that meant "these four" comes to mean nothing, silently, while still looking like a deliberate annotation. **The obvious fix is wrong for this chart.** §143's data-space example is two-dimensional — X = 42–57, Y = 10–18 — and converts cleanly. A screen loop over a *rotatable 3D* scene has no data-space equivalent at all: depth is ambiguous from a single projection, so there is no region of data the researcher can be said to have circled independently of where they were standing. Inventing one would have produced a confident, wrong region — the failure this codebase spends its time removing. Anchoring the loop to the marks it caught and redrawing a hull would violate §174 by changing what they drew. So the annotation **carries the view it was made in** and can say when that is no longer the view. `viewState()` and `restoreViewState()` join the seam, deliberately **opaque** — a map has a centre and a zoom, a timeline has a range, and a seam that assumed three Euler angles would stop being usable the first time a chart was not a 3D scatter. `sameView` scales its tolerance to magnitude, because a zoom of 4 cannot be judged by the same absolute slack as a pitch of 0.02, and treats a missing snapshot as *not* the same view — never drawn is not evidence that nothing moved. `restoreViewState` ignores a snapshot it does not recognise rather than half-applying it, since a partial restore puts the scene somewhere the researcher has never been. The strokes table shows *Still the same view?* and offers to go back. Two mutants killed; the conformance suite grew to 35 and now covers the pair on both charts. **Still `partial`**: object, data, world and surface spaces are unimplemented, and data space is well-posed for the 2D charts — that is the next one worth doing. |
| T053 | Two bugs in yesterday's undo, both in the layer above it | 1 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Undo shipped correct and the things drawing from it were not, which is where this class of bug usually lives. **Changing the stabilisation level destroyed the undo history.** The layer rebuilds the recorder when the level changes and carried the strokes across but not the past, so a fresh `InkHistory` replaced the old one — and a researcher who adjusted a slider silently lost the ability to recover a clear. Nothing about that is visible: undo simply stops being offered, and the obvious reading is that there was nothing to undo. `adoptFrom` now moves strokes and both stacks in one call, so it cannot be half-done; redo moves too, because carrying only the undo stack would leave somebody who had just undone something unable to redo it for a reason they could not connect to the slider they moved. The strokes are carried unchanged and never re-interpreted — re-stabilising a finished mark would change what the researcher drew after the fact (§174). **And undoing a clear brought the strokes back without their rows.** The page appended readings on draw and emptied them on clear, and a list maintained that way cannot follow an operation that reverses either one: the canvas showed three marks and the table showed none. **The first fix was wrong and the second is the interesting one** — filtering the rows down to the strokes that exist follows an undo correctly and loses on redo, because the row is thrown away rather than hidden. Readings are now never discarded; the table renders the ones whose stroke is currently present. Five tests, expressed against the recorder because that is the contract a page has to follow. Separately confirmed the `navigator.vibrate` gate from T048 is working: `hasBeenActive` is false and the console errors are stale buffer entries, unchanged in count across navigations. |
| T054 | The second chart was never connected to anything | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Reported as gestures not working on the second graph, and that is exactly what it was. **`SpatialSession` takes one controller, and `/gesture-check` has two figures.** `surfaceRef` was created, passed to the chart, and given to nothing that produces gestures — the README's own "written by one part of the system and read by none" defect, in the one place where it does not look like a missing wire but like broken tracking. A researcher waving at a chart that does not respond concludes the camera is at fault. `lib/spatial/targeting.ts` decides which figure a frame is addressed to. **Point to choose, then lock until release** (§189): once a pinch closes the target is fixed however far the hand travels and whatever it crosses, because the alternative is a gesture whose meaning changes underneath the person making it — the scene stops responding mid-drag and something else starts turning. The lock is kept even when the hand leaves the figure entirely, which is the whole point of it, and released when the held figure leaves the page so a gesture cannot be stuck holding something unmounted. **A hand over nothing addresses nothing.** Deliberately not "the nearest chart": a hand resting beside the figures, or moving while the researcher talks, would otherwise quietly steer whichever was closest, which is Rule 2's failure and the reason a gesture layer gets switched off for good. **No change to the session at all** — it already resolves its controller *after* publishing the frame to the observer, so choosing in `SpatialControl.onFrame` is decided in time and the resolver just returns it. The lock uses the same pinch hysteresis the machine does, so it holds for exactly as long as the machine considers the hand closed. **Measured on the real page**, which corrected an assumption: bounds are viewport-relative, so with the saddle scrolled into view the cloud sits at y = -753 and is unreachable, and the saddle is. You address what is on screen — correct, non-obvious, and now pinned by three tests so it is not read as the bug this fixes. Three mutants killed. The integration test failed first for a real reason: 400px-wide fixtures with a pointer at x = 500 landed outside both and fell through to the default. |
| T055 | One pen across the page, not one pen per figure | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Air Ink had the same single-figure limit the gesture layer had in T054: `InkLayer` was sized to one chart's box, so it could only be drawn on inside that chart and strokes were recorded in that chart's pixels. A second figure would have needed a second layer, a second recorder and **a second undo history** — so a mark could not cross from one figure to the other and undo would not span them. `fullViewport` makes the layer cover the window, strokes are recorded in viewport coordinates, and the figure a stroke belongs to is whichever the hand was addressing — which §189's lock makes exactly the right question to ask at pen-up, since the target is fixed for the whole stroke. **The conversion is a subtraction, and only because both sides are in the same frame.** A chart hit-tests in its own coordinates, so the polygon is moved through its `bounds()` before it is asked anything. Getting that wrong is silent in the worst available way: the count comes back, it is plausible, and it describes observations from the other chart. Three tests cover it, including one asserting the raw viewport polygon finds *nothing* — so the conversion is shown to be doing work rather than assumed to be. `/air-ink` now carries a saddle beside the cloud, deliberately a different kind of figure: two identical clouds would make a mis-resolution invisible. Measured live — the layer covers 744x757 against a 744x757 window with correct 2x backing. |
| T056 | Erasing, and the sampling trap for the third time | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §175 method A, §176 and §178. `lib/ink/erase.ts`; 18 tests. **The eraser is measured against its path, not against its samples**, and that is the same defect this codebase met in T038's region selection and again in the ink recorder: a hand moving quickly leaves the eraser's centre in a few widely spaced places, and testing only those points leaves untouched flecks wherever the hand outran the sampling — a wipe that visibly removes most of a line and strews the rest. Distance is to the eraser's *segments*. Pinned by its own assertion as well as by the outcome, so the mechanism is tested rather than inferred: a point midway between two samples 300px apart is touched, and is more than five radii from either. **Erasing the middle splits the stroke** (§178) — a researcher rubbing out the centre of a line expects two lines, not the whole line gone and not a line with an invisible hole that still answers selections along its length. Each fragment is a *subsequence* of the points the hand made, never resampled or refitted, because §174 applies to the remains of a stroke as much as to the whole; each carries `derivedFrom` through repeated splits, since a fragment with no ancestry is indistinguishable from something drawn separately. A run of one point is dropped: it cannot be drawn as a line and would strew invisible dots that still answer selections. **Nothing mutates**, so undo hands back the original object rather than a reconstruction — asserted by identity. **§176 is a mode and the lock lives in the recorder**, not on a button: people wave their hands while they talk, and a second entry point must not be able to route around the check. Four mutants killed. The clock-discipline guard caught `erase.ts` stamping `createdAt` from the wall clock and required an explicit justification rather than passing silently — which is what it is for. **Not built**: §177's resistance and haptic while crossing ink, and §175's other three methods. |
| T057 | The eraser reaches a hand, and a bug in how it recorded itself | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | T056 built erasing and wired it to nothing — a feature that is built, tested and unreachable, which is the defect this project names most often. A wipe now takes ink away **as the hand moves** rather than at pen-up, because a wipe whose effect arrives on release gives the researcher nothing to aim with, and the whole pass is **one entry in the history**: a wipe is one thing somebody did, and undoing it a frame at a time would mean fourteen presses to take back one movement. **A real bug, caught by a test that expected the opposite.** The history recorded "erasing across 0 strokes" for a wipe over empty canvas, because `eraseAlong` builds a fresh array every call and the canvas was being reassigned every frame — so the identity check at pen-up saw a change that had not happened. Assigning only when something was actually taken fixes it and removes a pointless array per frame. **The `rub` operation was also simplified while it was still cheap to.** It held the strokes that changed and spliced fragments back at remembered indices; a pass can remove strokes, split one into two and another into three, and reversing that by index is arithmetic with several quiet ways to be wrong. It now holds the whole canvas either side — two lists of references, exactly reversible — because an undo that *nearly* restores a figure is one nobody notices is wrong. Its description still counts what the pass touched rather than the canvas, or "erasing across 12 strokes" would misdescribe a wipe that took one. **§177 in the form that is worth having**: a haptic tick at the moment the eraser meets ink, on the transition only — a tick per frame along a long line is a buzz rather than a boundary, and the moment of contact is the thing that makes a virtual mark feel like something that was there. Three mutants killed. |
| T058 | Reading a mark as a shape, and refusing to when it is not one | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §181 and the half of §174 that had never been exercised. A circle drawn in mid-air is a wobbly polygon and looks careless in a published figure, so offering a clean one is worth doing — what is not worth doing is any version that loses the original. Line, circle, ellipse and rectangle, read **after** the stroke finishes as §181 requires: morphing while the hand is still moving takes the drawing away from the person doing it, who aims, sees it jump, corrects, and sees it jump again. **The failure worth guarding is not failing to recognise a circle — it is recognising everything**, and the first implementation did exactly that. Scoring by *mean* residual, a loop with seven deliberate lobes scored **0.93** and was offered as a circle, because a seventh of the points being 35% out is small once averaged against the six sevenths that fit. That is the precise case §174 exists to protect: a researcher who drew an irregular boundary meant that boundary. Averaging is simply the wrong summary for "does this look like a circle to a person" — nobody looks at the mean deviation, they look at the parts that stick out. Scored on the **90th percentile** instead: sensitive to a systematic departure, still tolerant of a tracker spike, which the maximum would not be. A test asserts the threshold is doing work by showing the same boundary *is* accepted when it is lowered — a threshold nothing ever fails is not a threshold. **Rectangles keep their rotation**, aligned to the drawing rather than the screen: snapping a sketch upright would move the annotation off what it was drawn around. Deliberately geometry rather than a model, so a mistake can be reasoned about rather than only observed. Three mutants killed, including mean-versus-percentile. Two fixture faults found on the way, both of which blamed the fit for the test — measuring from the centre the fixture was built around rather than the fitted one, then averaging a closed path's points without dropping the repeated closing point, which pulls the centroid off by r/n. **Not built**: arrow, polygon and bracket, and nothing is applied yet — the offer has no control. |
| T059 | §181 finished rather than left partial | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Asked not to leave things partial, so §181 was completed: arrow, polygon and bracket joined line, circle, ellipse and rectangle, and the offer is now reachable and reversible on `/air-ink`. **A closed mark is walked cyclically, and that was a real bug rather than a refinement.** A hand-drawn triangle came back with two corners and was offered as a *circle* — the corner sitting on the seam, where the stroke ends where it began, has no neighbours on one side, and a walk that stops a window short of each end never sees it. Every closed polygon has a corner at its seam roughly a third of the time. The clustering wraps too, or that corner is counted twice and a triangle becomes a quadrilateral. **The mutation for this survived twice, which was the useful part.** Clamping the indices instead of wrapping fabricates a turn at the final point — comparing a real heading against a zero-length step — so a square still came back with four corners, one of them an artefact, and both the triangle and the seam test passed for the wrong reason. The property that separates them is that **a circle has no corners**: on a smooth loop the artefact has nothing to hide behind. **Four near-right corners is a rectangle, not "a 4-sided shape"** — true and useless; the specific name is the informative one and carries a rotation and side lengths with it. An arrow is defined by the *proportion* of barb to shaft, because a line with the small hook a hand leaves when it stops would otherwise be offered as an arrow every time. A bracket requires both ends to leave the spine on the same side, or any mark that turned twice would be one. **Accepting an offer changes the drawn copy and never `originalPoints`** — asserted by identity — and records that the shape was accepted rather than drawn, so a figure can tell the two apart. Four mutants killed. A fixture fault worth noting: a loop of equal radius in *normalised* units is a visibly squashed ellipse on a 720x520 canvas, so the recogniser was right and the test was wrong. |
| T060 | The lasso, finished in one go | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §180, complete: recogniser to control to page, nothing left partial. **A lasso is a question, not an annotation.** Drawing a loop with the pen leaves a mark *and* reports what was inside it; a lasso is drawn to ask "which of these", so the boundary is handed over on the frame it closes and removed in the same breath. Leaving it would turn every selection into a scribble somebody has to clean up. It is also taken out of the undo history, or the first press of undo after a selection would restore a boundary the researcher had already finished with. It *is* drawn while it is being made, because §180 asks for persistent feedback and a boundary you cannot see is one you cannot close accurately. Resolution reuses `selectWithinStroke` and the same conversion into the addressed figure's frame — it is the same question as a drawn region, so it must not be a second implementation of the answer. **`InkTool` lost `highlighter` on the way.** It was declared in the union and implemented nowhere: a member that reads as a capability and is only a plan, which is the same defect as a README claiming a test count that nothing verifies. The type now says what the product does. Three mutants killed, including the one that lets a lasso become a mark. Verified live: three tools on the page, switching correctly, each with its own instruction. |
| T061 | A ruler for a hand that cannot hold one | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §182, complete in one pass. **A hand in mid-air cannot draw a straight line, and smoothing cannot make it able to** — smoothing removes tremor, and the problem is not tremor: an arm rotates about a shoulder while the researcher believes they are moving it sideways, so the line bows. Acceptable in an annotation, wrong in an axis marker or a trend line, and the only recourse was to draw it again and keep whichever attempt bowed least. **Applied while drawing, which is the opposite of §181 and deliberately so.** A shape offer must wait until the stroke is finished, because morphing a mark under a moving hand takes the drawing away from the person doing it. A straightedge is not an interpretation of what somebody drew — it is a tool they picked up *before* they started, and a ruler that only straightened the line after the pen was lifted would be useless. The same distinction decides where the result lands: a shape offer changes the drawn copy and leaves `originalPoints` alone (§174), while a constrained line **is** what was drawn, so it goes into the record — otherwise a lasso or a measurement would resolve against the bowed path the hand took rather than the line on screen. **Magnetic means near, not nearest.** A hard snap-to-nearest would drag every deliberately oblique line onto 45 degrees and the researcher would be fighting the tool; the tolerance is about twelve degrees, wide enough to aim at without precision and narrow enough that thirty degrees is understood as thirty. **The heading waits, and then reaches backwards.** The first samples of a stroke are a hand accelerating from rest and their direction is mostly noise, so locking to them would give a perfectly straight line pointing somewhere nobody intended — worse than bowing, because it looks deliberate. But leaving those points free puts a visible kink in the first few pixels of every line, so once the direction is known they are pulled onto it, once. Found by a test asserting collinearity that failed by 0.06px. Three mutants killed. |
| T062 | Layers, and the one requirement in them that is not organisational | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §201 and §202, both complete. Layers are usually a convenience; one line of §201 is not — **"AI-generated drawings must be distinguishable from researcher-created drawings"** — and that is why this is a module rather than a `visible` flag on a stroke. A figure carrying a *suggested* trend line makes a different claim from one carrying a trend line somebody committed to, and the difference has to survive being screenshotted, printed in greyscale, and read a year later by somebody who was not there. It cannot depend on the reader remembering which layer was on. **So origin is authoritative and enforced at render time, not expressed through style.** A caller can set an assistant stroke's colour, width and dash to match a researcher's exactly — by copying a style, by a future control offering "dash" as an option, or deliberately — and the mark is still drawn dashed, because the renderer reads `origin` rather than trusting what it was handed. The mutation that removes the override kills three tests. Dashed specifically because it survives what a colour or a weight does not: greyscale, a small screenshot, a colour-blind reader. "It looked slightly fainter" is not a distinction anybody can rely on. A researcher's *own* dash is deliberately a different pattern, so choosing one does not make their mark read as the assistant's. **A stroke with no known layer is drawn rather than swallowed** — a mark on a missing group is still a mark somebody made, and hiding it would lose work silently, which is worse than showing it unattributed. Hiding is not erasing, and a test switches a layer off and on to say so. §202 kept to four colours rather than a picker, because it warns against a floating palette for every change. |
| T063 | The hand had no presence on the page | 3 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Asked to make it immersive, which turned out not to mean adding tools. **Nothing on screen acknowledged the researcher's hand until ink appeared** — so when a pinch failed there was no way to tell whether the camera could not see the hand, could see it and disagreed about what a pinch is, or the hand was not over a figure. Three different faults, one symptom: nothing happened. That is very likely part of what was behind several rounds of "it is not working". §142 states the requirement as a sentence rather than a feature — *the user must never wonder "am I drawing yet"* — and the useful reading of it is **continuous rather than confirmatory**. A cursor that appears when drawing starts confirms success and says nothing about failure. An arc that fills as the fingers close turns "it didn't work" into "I can see I am at two thirds", which somebody can act on without being told what hysteresis is. Measured against the *same* span-relative thresholds the machine uses, because a readout that said "nearly there" while the machine had already acted would teach the researcher to distrust the one thing telling them what is happening. **`contact` and `active` are separate phases**: the fingers have met, and the system has agreed. The gap between them is the two-frame rule, and showing it is what stops somebody concluding that a pinch which *was* registered a moment later did not register at all. The cursor sits at the pinch centroid, not the fingertip — 65px apart (T044) — or it would teach the researcher to aim wrongly. Nothing goes through React; the hand moves thirty times a second. Four mutants killed. **A generic `magnetTarget` was written for §191 and then deleted**: every chart already does nearest-within-a-radius in `nearest`, because only a chart knows where its own marks are, and a second implementation working from a copy of the projection is exactly how picking and painting drift apart. A tested but unused helper is the defect this codebase keeps naming, so it went rather than waiting for a caller. What the cursor owes §191 is the other half — it is drawn where the hand is and never at what it would select, because a pointer that leapt onto the nearest mark would feel possessed. |
| T064 | An immersion audit, and the four silences it found | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Asked for a granular audit of everything built rather than another feature. Went through the spatial surface asking one question — *where does the illusion of handling your research break* — and checked each answer against the code rather than against impressions. Hover highlighting turned out to be genuinely present (§15). Four things were not, and every one of them was a **silence**: something the system knew and never said. **§189's lock was invisible**, which is the serious one — it is the most important guarantee in the spatial layer, and with two figures on a page a researcher pinches and cannot tell which they have taken until it moves. If it is the wrong one, they find out by turning a figure they did not mean to. The addressed figure now carries a dashed edge and the held one a solid edge: deliberately different pictures rather than the same one at two opacities, because "the one I would grab" and "the one I have" are different facts. Drawn as an edge only — a tint over a figure competes with the data it is pointing at. **The eraser had no size.** A tool whose extent cannot be seen takes more than intended about half the time, and on an eraser that costs an annotation; it now shows its true radius, faintly, and nothing extra when the reach is smaller than the cursor itself, which would be noise rather than information. **A lasso ended in silence** — the boundary vanishes the instant it closes, which is right, but it left the most consequential gesture in the subsystem as the only one with no detent. **Nothing said which layer a mark would land on**, so a researcher who switched and forgot would draw into a group they might then hide, and conclude the ink had vanished rather than that it was somewhere they were not looking. Three mutants killed, including the one that locks onto nothing. |
| T065 | An introduction that watches, and therefore proves | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §97, complete. The specification is unusually clear about what not to build — no thirty-minute tutorial, progressive rather than up-front, a first session of exactly point, pinch, move, release — and silent on the thing that decides whether it is worth anything: **how a step completes**. **Each step advances on evidence from the hand, never on a button.** A slideshow saying "now pinch" with a Next teaches nothing and confirms nothing; a researcher can click through all four without the tracking having seen them once. Waiting until the pinch actually registers makes finishing the sequence *proof* that gestures work on this machine, with this camera, in this light, for this hand — which matters more here than it would elsewhere, because every report of "it's not working" so far has been a symptom shared by three unrelated faults. Somebody stuck on *pinch* while *point* completed knows the camera can see them and the threshold is the problem. That is a bug report instead of a shrug. **A hand that arrives already pinched does not satisfy "point"**, or the sequence would skip the next step without teaching it and tell the researcher they had learnt something they never did. **Losing the hand pauses rather than restarts**: resetting because a hand left the frame for a moment would punish exactly the person this exists for — the one whose camera or lighting is marginal. Three steady frames, for the same reason the pen needs two: one frame of anything is a hand passing through. **Never a gate** (§13). Steps skip individually as well as together, because being held at the one thing your hand is bad at is what makes somebody abandon the feature, and nothing is blocked while it runs. Remembered afterwards, defaulting to *not done* so a preferences blob written before this existed shows the introduction rather than silently skipping it. Three mutants killed. |
| T066 | The cue that was computed and never shown | 1 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §98, complete — and found the same way the audit found the last four: by checking whether something the system knows is ever said. The cursor had been computing `intent` on every frame since T063 and **never drawing it**. Written by one part of the system and read by none, in code two rounds old. **§98 contains a tension worth resolving rather than splitting.** It asks for a cue when somebody points at something and, in the same breath, says not to clutter the interface permanently. A label pinned to the cursor is the version that loses both ways: it follows the hand across the figure, sits on the data being read, and after ten minutes it is furniture nobody sees — so it is simultaneously clutter and not a cue. The resolution is that a cue is worth something at exactly two moments. **When the answer changes**: the hand has arrived somewhere new, or the tool has, and what a pinch would do is a different answer than it was. **When the hand goes still**: somebody holding their hand over something without acting is deliberating, and a hand in motion has already decided. It fades rather than blinking out, because a label that vanishes draws more attention leaving than arriving. The policy is a pure function so it could be argued with in tests rather than watched. Three mutants killed. Two fixture faults, both blaming the implementation for the test: a "moving" hand written as still-since-zero, which reads as maximally still and made every case return 1; and a recording context that answered `measureText` with undefined, so `.width` threw and the label was skipped entirely — the test read as though the feature were missing when it was the double that was. |

So a researcher who turned the camera on, raised the rotation sensitivity, and then finished pointing and pinching would find the sensitivity back where it started, with nothing connecting the two events — the kind of fault somebody reports as "it keeps forgetting my settings" and nobody can reproduce. Now merged through a ref, so a change is always against what is stored rather than against a snapshot. Two tests: one that the later write keeps the earlier change, and one asserting the *old* behaviour loses it, so the mechanism is pinned rather than the outcome. **A scan for the defect class that has appeared three times** — something computed, or exported, with no reader — turned up three over-exports rather than dead code. Narrowed anyway, and one mattered: `scene3d` states that `RADIANS_PER_PIXEL` is the one place the constant exists, and an export is an invitation to a second place that stays self-consistent and drifts from the projection, which is exactly how rotation was broken from its first commit. `OneEuroFilter` went the same way: every caller wants the point form, and exposing the scalar invites two independently configured filters on x and y that come apart on a diagonal. **Zero errors and zero warnings** across tsc, eslint, 857 web tests, 1129 backend tests, a production build, four routes, and `doctor`. |
| T068 | A ruler whose main feature is a refusal | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | §183 and §184, both complete, plus §25 assessed as already built (a short click on a lazily created `AudioContext`, off by default). §183 draws "42 mm" under a line between two points; §184 says what to do when that number is not available — *never infer it, say so clearly* — and on these charts the second sentence governs. **The axes are scaled independently**, so a centimetre along one is a different amount of a different quantity from a centimetre along another: there is no length of the line joining two observations. Not hard to compute — **it does not exist**. So the difference is reported along each axis in that axis's own units and the single number is refused, said with every measurement rather than once in a footnote, because the person reading it months later is exactly the one without the surrounding conversation. **Measured between observations rather than screen points**, which is the other half: a point on screen over a rotatable scene is a *ray*, since depth is ambiguous from one projection, so "the place I indicated" only has coordinates when it is a mark that exists. **A bug found by clicking rather than by reasoning.** Selecting one mark and selecting it again reported "0 x, 0 y, 0 z" — a confident answer to a question nobody asked, which a researcher glancing at it would read as a finding (two observations that coincide) rather than as a mis-click. Refused now, **by identity rather than by coordinates**, because two *different* observations at the same place is a genuine and interesting zero and refusing that would hide the finding this exists to surface. Two mutants killed. |
| T069 | Two integration failures found by asking where the pieces meet | 2 | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `feat/wave-2-interpretation` | Asked to keep everything in sync and failing nowhere, so the seams were interrogated rather than the modules. The researcher would have held a reference to a region they never indicated, and nothing about it would look wrong. Separated into `committed` and `erased`; the layer now repaints on an erase explicitly rather than by accident. The empty-canvas half of this was already safe, because T048's leak fix covers it — worth knowing rather than assuming, and the test that proved it also hid the dangerous half until a second case reached it. **A typed sentence could not reach the gesture it was about.** Words were dated from *submission*, so drawing a loop and taking ten seconds to type put every word outside the backward window: somebody referring to the circle they had just drawn was told nothing was indicated. Dated from the first keystroke now. **The first fix was also wrong and a test caught it**: spreading the words across the typing interval put "these" 4.3s after the gesture, still outside — and the spread models something that cannot happen, because speech words are spread out by a person gesturing *while speaking*, and **nobody can gesture while typing**. Every word of a typed sentence refers to the same moment, so it is now a short utterance dated from when composition began. The consequence is recorded rather than hidden: "compare this with this", one word per cluster, is genuinely unavailable by keyboard and is a thing speech will do that typing cannot. Node is 204 MB with npm 10.9.8. Fetch 8.5 s and ~21 s; 0.17 s once present. **Driven end to end rather than asserted.** A fresh `git worktree` with no `.venv` bootstrapped using the fetched interpreter: nine packages, 39 migrations against the bundled PostgreSQL, exit 0. `pyvenv.cfg` records `home = …/python-3.12.14/bin`, so it was the fetched interpreter and not the system's. Tightened to assert the reason rather than the exception. **Fixed in passing, found by this work:** `dev` chose node by version and then resolved `npm` with `shutil.which`, which searches *this* process's PATH rather than the chosen node's directory — pairing a new node with an old npm on any machine carrying both. `node_exe()` already existed for exactly this and was already used for `npx`; `dev` was the one call site that did not. **Three caveats, all real.** (1) The wrong-version re-exec is unit-tested but **never exercised against a real non-3.12 interpreter** — this machine has only 3.12. (2) The Linux pins are `gnu`, so **musl distributions such as Alpine are not covered**; stated in the module rather than left to be discovered. (3) Node's *download* branch was exercised directly (`python scripts/runtimes.py node`) but **not inside bootstrap**, because the test machine already had Node 24 and the skip branch correctly fired. A new `.gitattributes` pins both, and that was **verified through an actual checkout** rather than assumed — repo blob stores LF, `git worktree add` yields CRLF. It tries `py -3` before `python`, because `python` on a machine with no Python is the Microsoft Store stub, which prints an advert and exits 9009. The Linux `.desktop` entry's `Exec` is **quoted**: unquoted, a clone under `~/My Research/` splits into two arguments and the entry launches nothing, silently. **26 tests** in `tests/test_launchers.py`; both shell launchers are **driven for real** on their no-Python branch, the one path reachable here without starting the whole stack. **Six mutants killed** — batch reverted to LF, non-ASCII in the batch, `start` never installing, `start` launching after a failed install, the desktop entry hiding the terminal, and the Linux launcher forking to find the repo. | T072 | Base install plus feature packs on demand | dist | done | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/dist-feature-packs` | **Done.** Two halves: feature packs, and the base composition the row asked about. **Feature packs.** `throughline_domain/extras.py` is the single registry of all nine optional capabilities across three distributions. `/api/system/capabilities` reports every one, each stating what is **withheld** without it and its approximate size; `POST /api/system/packs/NAME/install` turns one on from the screen that reported it missing, guarded by an allowlist of nine literals so a path parameter can never reach pip, and returning 202 because a request held open for torch times out. pip exiting 0 is not believed on its own. And the interface stops being a Node process: it is exported to static files the API serves itself. **Measured both ways rather than argued**: the exported bundle is **36 MB** against 812 MB of `node_modules` plus a 204 MB Node runtime. The app was already suited to it — every route prerendered static, no route handlers, no middleware, no dynamic segments. **Why the API serves it rather than a second server**: the session cookie is `httpOnly` and `SameSite=strict`, so a cross-origin call drops it **silently**. The `rewrites()` proxy was the only thing making two ports look like one origin, and it is exactly what static export cannot have. It now builds an interface, skips when one exists, and is non-fatal when it cannot — the API answers 503 naming the command that fixes it. **50 backend tests and 7 web tests across the two halves; twelve mutants killed**, including both allowlist guards, the path-traversal guard, the symlink case, and the one that stops pip's exit code being taken as proof. There is a test on route *registration order*, because a route added below the catch-all would be unreachable while every unit test of it passed. | T073 | Check-for-updates button | dist | done | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/dist-updates` | **Done.** `manage.py update` applies one; a **Check for updates** button in Settings checks and then names the command. **Never from inside the running app** — applying replaces the code the API process is executing, so it cannot swap itself out from underneath a request, and a button that appeared to do it would have to lie about when it had finished. **The order is the design, chosen around the fact that the database is the only copy of the research.** Refuse on a dirty checkout, because updating fast-forwards the tree and uncommitted work is exactly what must not vanish. Back up before anything, database and object store in one archive since either without the other is useless. Fast-forward only — a merge could conflict, and a half-updated checkout is worse than an old one. Reinstall, rebuild the interface, migrate, in that order, because a migration may need code that arrived in the update. On any failure after the code moved, put the code back and reinstall from it. **It deliberately does not restore the database.** Migrations are forward-only and each runs in its own transaction, so a failure leaves the schema at the last one that succeeded rather than half-applied, and old code generally tolerates a newer schema. Restoring would discard everything done since the backup — the researcher's decision, not the updater's. The command is printed instead. **Two questions that look like one.** `version.py` answers *what am I* with **no network at all**, and reports where the answer came from: a stamped release, a git checkout, or nothing. A modified checkout says so, because it names something nobody else can obtain — and `0.1.0` from a pyproject that has said `0.1.0` since the first commit is worse than useless, because it looks like an answer. `updates.py` answers *is there anything newer* and reaches the network to do it. **The property most of the tests are about: being unable to check is not being up to date.** They render identically and only one is true. An installation follows `main` until somebody tags and follows tags after — one mechanism, which is the progression this row described. **19 tests, driven against real git repositories in a tmpdir rather than mocks** — the logic is almost entirely about what git says, so a mocked git would be testing the mock, and the failure modes that matter (no remote, no tags, dirty, not a checkout) are trivial to produce for real. **Five mutants killed**, including the one that reports an unreachable remote as up to date. The dirty-tree refusal was driven on this checkout for real and exits 1, before the backup and before the merge; `--check` exits 0, because being unable to ask is not a failure of the asking. Full suite **1392 passed, 13 skipped, 0 failed**; web **1282 across 88 files**. Linux only, not dispatched — D034. **Driven end to end after the fact, and it found two real bugs.** A stand-in installation was cloned at an older commit, bootstrapped with its own database, and updated to a pushed tag. The check switched channel from `main` to the tag automatically, reported `behind: 1`, and the apply backed up, fast-forwarded, reinstalled, migrated and reported the new version **by tag name** — which is the "a version with a name" this row asked for, demonstrated rather than described. The marker commit arrived in the working tree and a re-check then said up to date. **The first attempt failed, and correctly.** The backup could not run against a stopped installation, so the update refused and changed nothing — the safety property working exactly as designed, and the route by which **D045** was found: `backup.sh` and `restore.sh` were both pointed at the wrong socket and both required a running server, so neither half of the safety net worked, and nothing tested either. Fixed and now covered by `tests/test_backup_script.py`. **D046** came from the same session. Also fixed here: `update` did not honour `THROUGHLINE_SKIP_NODE` although `bootstrap` did, so a deliberately headless install would have acquired a Node dependency at its first update. |

| T081 | The launcher becomes the thing you download, not a file inside what you already have | dist | done | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/launcher-installs` | **Done.** Each launcher is now a file you can hand somebody. Run it and it opens Throughline, installing it first if that machine has not got it yet. Three cases, cheapest first, network only in the last: **inside a checkout** (running from a clone keeps working, and uses *that* clone rather than another copy in the home directory); **`~/throughline-os`** or `THROUGHLINE_INSTALL_DIR`, the common case on a downloaded launcher's second run, two `stat` calls and no network; **nothing yet**, hand over to `install.sh`, which stays the one implementation of the install sequence. `install.sh` now ends at `start` rather than `bootstrap` — `start` already means "set up if needed, then run", so stopping at bootstrap left a finished install and nothing on screen, the same failure the launchers had one level up. `.command` and `.sh` are one script with different headers, and a test asserts they have not drifted below the resolution block: three launchers disagreeing about where an installation lives would each get a different answer to "is it installed?", which is worse than any one of them being wrong. **8 new tests, four driving the real script against a stub installation rather than reading it.** Two existing tests were asserting the old *shape* — a `cd` line mentioning `BASH_SOURCE`, the literal string `manage.py start` — and now assert the property, which is why they broke when the shape changed and the behaviour did not. **Verified live**: a launcher alone in a downloads folder started a real installation and opened a browser on it, 200 and a real page. Full suite **1420 passed, 13 skipped, 0 failed**. **Still blocked, and it is the half that matters for a stranger:** the repository is private, so the advertised `curl` line 404s and a clone prompts for credentials nobody outside has. Both were tested as an unauthenticated stranger. The launcher is now the right artifact; there is nowhere to put it. D050. |
| T082 | A release artifact: one tarball, a checksum and a manifest | dist | done | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/release-build` | **Done.** `manage.py release` writes a tarball, its SHA-256 and a `latest.json` manifest into `dist/`. **One artifact serves every platform, and that fell out of decisions already made** rather than being a goal: the interface is a static export with no native binaries (T072), `runtimes.py` fetches the right CPython and Node per machine at install time, and pip builds the virtualenv there. So one build, and no macOS at its 10x multiplier. **19 MB compressed, 311 files** — it carries `apps/web/out` and not `apps/web`, which is the difference between that and the 850 MB the build dependencies weigh. **Two refusals, both because the failure is otherwise silent.** A dirty checkout is refused: a release built from uncommitted changes is indistinguishable from one built from a commit until somebody tries to reproduce it and cannot. A missing interface is refused by name, because shipping without it is a Python package whose every page answers 503. **The archive is an allowlist of declared roots, not a walk with exclusions** — a stray `secrets.env` at the top level cannot travel by accident, which is the failure mode a denylist has, where safety depends on having thought of the thing in advance. The package list is passed in rather than copied, because `bootstrap.sh` once installed four of nine and nothing failed on the machine that wrote it. **Verified end to end, not asserted**: built a real release, unpacked it clean, and confirmed it carries the interface, a VERSION file, no `.git` and no `node_modules` — and that `version.current()` on the unpacked copy reports `source: "release"` rather than `checkout`, which is the whole point of the stamp. `updates.check()` there says it cannot check, honestly, which is T084's job. **15 tests, five mutants killed.** One mutant showed three of my own exclusion tests were passing for the wrong reason — `node_modules` and `.venv` are never *reached* rather than excluded, so deleting the exclusion logic left them green. The mechanism now has its own test and the misleading one says which of its cases prove what. Full suite **1435 passed, 13 skipped, 0 failed**. **The checksum is not the security story**: whoever can replace the tarball can replace the digest beside it. Until T083 signs the manifest, a release is only as trustworthy as the server it came from. |
| T083 | Sign the manifest, so the download server is not the whole trust story | dist | done | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/release-signing` | **Done, with one limit documented rather than hidden.** The manifest is signed with ed25519, and because the manifest names the tarball's SHA-256 the signature covers the archive transitively — one small signature rather than streaming 19 MB through a verifier. The public half ships **inside every tarball**, which is the entire point: it arrives with the software rather than from the server being verified, the same reasoning that puts `runtimes.py`'s CPython digests in this repository. **A first install cannot check a signature, and that is stated everywhere it matters** — in `signing.py`, `keys/README.md` and the README. Verification has to happen before anything is installed and the library that checks a signature arrives *in* the download, so first install trusts HTTPS plus the digest in the manifest; every update afterwards verifies properly, because the virtualenv exists by then. That is the common path over a product's life. Two alternatives were rejected: a pure-Python verifier is writing our own crypto, and shelling out to `openssl` degrades to no verification at all on Windows — the platform least able to notice. **A verification that silently does nothing is worse than none, because it is believed.** **Unsigned is legitimate but never quiet**: printed every build. Asked to sign with a key that is not there, the build refuses rather than falling back — a release published believing it is protected is worse than one that never claimed to be. The private key is printed and never written, and `.gitignore` refuses `*.pem`, because a signing key a script saves is one that gets committed eventually. **Verified end to end**, not asserted: generated a throwaway key, built a real signed release, verified the manifest against the shipped public key, and confirmed a swapped tarball digest is refused. The throwaway public key was then removed — a committed key whose private half sat in a temp directory would be worse than none. `keys/` ships a README and no key; the first one is generated by a person. **30 tests, four mutants killed**, including a bad signature passing silently and the signature field being left inside its own signed payload. `verify()` raises rather than returning False, because `if verify(...)` on a None-returning function succeeds for every input — a check with no consequence, which is a read with no writer wearing different clothes. Full suite **1452 passed, 13 skipped, 0 failed**. **Adds `cryptography` as a base dependency** rather than an extra: a verification somebody has to install first is one nobody performs. **Untested off Linux** — a new dependency is precisely what CLAUDE.md says to dispatch for, and D034 still blocks it. Also fixed here: the missing-path error named `build-interface` for everything absent, including the release key, sending the reader at a command that could not help — D041 and D043's defect for the third time. |
| T084 | Updates for installs that have no `.git` | dist | done | Claude (Fenil) | `feat/release-updates` | **Done.** A released copy fetches the signed manifest over HTTPS and verifies it against the public key it shipped with. HTTPS authenticates the server; the signature authenticates whoever made the release — the same fact right up until the server is compromised, which is the day it matters. **Three states kept apart**, because collapsing any two tells the researcher something false: a newer release exists, this is current, or the question could not be answered. An unreachable server is the third and never the second. Two refusals carry the value: a manifest signed by another key is refused — the case HTTPS cannot see, a server offering a perfectly valid document this product did not produce — and an installation with **no** shipped public key refuses to check rather than believing what arrives. **The virtualenv is never moved, and that shaped the whole design.** It sits at `<root>/.venv` with the workspace packages installed *editable* into it, pointing back at `<root>/packages/...`. A virtualenv is not relocatable, so renaming the installation directory would leave every editable install addressing somewhere that no longer exists. The source is replaced in place instead. **Rollback is therefore a move, not a re-download**: the replaced tree goes to `.rollback/` and comes back if pip or a migration fails, so the previous version is *literally still on disk* — what T073 asked for, where `git reset --hard` could only reconstruct it. The tarball URL is **derived from the manifest's own location** rather than read out of it: a signed manifest naming an arbitrary host would be a redirect the signature endorses. **14 tests, none touching the network** — manifests are served from `file://`, exercising the same fetch, verification and refusals. **Three mutants killed**: the signature never verified, checking with no key to verify against, and an unreachable server reported as up to date. The directory swap is tested in-process, because that is the part which, done wrong, destroys an installation rather than failing to update one. A T073 test that asserted this limitation now asserts the behaviour replacing it. **Not yet driven end to end.** The check path and the swap are tested; a full download-verify-swap against a real bootstrapped tarball install has not been run, and that is the obvious thing for a verifier to do. It also cannot be fully exercised until there is somewhere to host a release — D050. |

Since T072 the whole app is a static export, so the same build deploys publicly with the workspace routes excluded. Carries the download link. Cloudflare Pages plus R2 is the fit: no egress fees on an artifact of tens of megabytes. **Correction: the page is already built.** It is `frontend/` — a WebGL2 ray-traced black hole with scroll choreography, story beats, and a per-platform **Download** menu under the mark. I scoped this row as "add a download button to `apps/web/app/page.tsx`" and that was wrong: `frontend/` is **deliberately untracked** via `.git/info/exclude`, so every git-based search returned nothing and I concluded no download affordance existed. Its own README says the exclusion is so design iteration never dirties the shared tree, and that porting it in should be recorded as a ledger task — this row. **So the remaining work is a port, not a design.** Bring `frontend/` into the repo (delete the exclude entry), build it as a public export alongside or instead of `apps/web/app/page.tsx`, and deploy it. **And repoint its download links, which currently 404 for exactly the person the page is for.** Measured unauthenticated: `github.com/.../archive/refs/heads/main.zip` → **404**, `raw.githubusercontent.com/.../scripts/install.sh` → **404**. Both point into the private repository. They should point at the signed release artifact from T082/T083, which is what D050 is about — so this row is blocked on the same hosting decision. **Worth knowing:** being untracked means the page exists only on this machine and is not backed up by git. A `git clean -fdx` would delete it. |

| T087 | The stranger's install path stops going through a private repository | dist | done | Claude (Fenil) | `main` | **D050 said the host name was the last thing; it was not.** With the name chosen, `install.sh` still ran `git clone` against the private repo, so a stranger who downloaded it from the release host got *could not read Username* at the last step — the second of D050's two measured failures, which naming a host does nothing about. `Throughline.bat` was worse: it cloned the private repo directly, and the launchers' `INSTALLER_URL` still pointed at a `raw.githubusercontent.com` path that 404s. **All three now install the published release.** The sequence lives once, in the new `scripts/install.py` — resolve the manifest, derive the archive URL from *where the manifest was found* rather than from a field in it, download, check the digest, reject any entry that would write outside the destination, confirm it looks like Throughline, and only then move it into place. Both doors fetch that file rather than reimplementing it; expressing download-verify-unpack in POSIX shell **and** batch would have been a third and fourth copy of an install sequence, which is the drift this repository has already paid for twice. A git checkout is now the developer path, taken only when one is already present or `THROUGHLINE_REPO` is set on purpose. Evidence: `tests/test_install_script.py` — 12 tests, **mutation-checked**: never comparing the digest fails 1, dropping the traversal check 1, dropping the shape check 1, and reading the archive URL out of the manifest instead of deriving it fails 4. `tests/test_landing_downloads.py` +3, also mutation-checked against five separate drifts. The release now publishes `install.py` beside the launchers, so a release without it fails the build rather than at a stranger's last step. **1481 Python tests pass locally.** **Driven end to end on two platforms**, against the real signed release served over HTTP: on Linux the advertised line was fetched with `curl` and run, and it downloaded, verified the digest, unpacked and handed over to `manage.py`, which recovered from a system Python with no `venv` exactly as D046 promised and reached *Ready* — the install tree carries `VERSION` and `keys/release.pub` and has **no `.git`**, so it is a release install rather than a clone. On **Windows**, via D051's interop route, `py -3` reached the server, fetched `install.py` with the same `urljoin` one-liner `Throughline.bat` uses, and installed it with the checksum verified — the first time the Windows install path has been exercised without credentials. **Not dispatched to CI, so macOS and the Docker build have not seen this.** |

| T091 | The shell's edges belong to the researcher | shell | done | Claude | — | **Done.** `react-resizable-panels` v4 for the rail and the inspector, which were fixed at 232px and 360px with no control anywhere to change them — a long research question in the rail, or a dense evidence panel in the inspector, had nowhere to go. v4 takes **pixel** sizes, so the shipped widths carry over exactly: a researcher who never touches a divider cannot tell anything changed. `.shell` drops from a three-column grid to two rows, and the columns become a `Group`. **The §117 breakpoint had to move from CSS into JS**: below 1101px the inspector is dropped rather than crushed, and a flex panel hidden with `display: none` still holds its share of the width, so the panel is not rendered at all — with its divider, because a divider that resizes nothing is a control that lies. `useSyncExternalStore` over `matchMedia` rather than `useEffect`, so the first client paint is already right instead of flashing the desktop layout. The media query is deleted rather than rewritten: the threshold now lives in exactly one place. **The width is stored per browser, not per project** — a panel width is a fact about this person's screen, not about the project they happened to be in when they dragged it, and keying it by project would mean switching projects silently rearranges the screen. Fixed. **Verified in the browser, and the verification found a bug the suite could not** — see D128. Measured live at 1400px: rail **232px** and inspector **360px** — though that exactness was itself the symptom of **D134**, since the two dividers were taking no width at all. With them at 9px each the panels sit within a few pixels of the shipped values, which is the honest version; two labelled `separator`s, both tab stops; arrow keys resize and clamp at **380** and **180**, the declared max and min; no double border where `.rail`'s used to be; no horizontal overflow. **2409 web tests** pass. |
| T092 | The command bar is a real modal | shell | done | Claude | — | **Done, and smaller than it looked.** The palette's keyboard handling was already correct — arrow keys with wrapping, the Ctrl-N/Ctrl-P bindings it advertises, `aria-activedescendant` so focus can stay in the input while the selection moves, `scrollIntoView({block:"nearest"})`. None of that changed. What was wrong was the modal itself: `role="dialog"` and `aria-modal="true"` asserted on a plain `<div>`. `aria-modal` is a promise that everything outside is inert, and nothing kept it — no Tab trap, so focus walked out of the palette into a page the user could no longer see; no focus restore, so dismissing it left focus on `<body>`; a background that stayed scrollable. Now a native `<dialog showModal()>`, which is where the trap, the inert background, the top-layer stacking and Escape come from — the argument `ConfirmDialog` already makes in this codebase, so the two modal surfaces share one mechanism. `role` and `aria-modal` are dropped rather than kept: a modal `<dialog>` carries both implicitly, and restating them by hand is how they drift. Escape routes through the `cancel` event to `onClose` rather than letting the browser close the element, which would leave React's `open` still true and the parent believing it was showing. `.palette-backdrop` is gone in favour of `::backdrop`. **No dependency added** — this is the case where native beats a library, for the same reason `ConfirmDialog` was the wrong place to start with Radix. Closes **D123**. Verified in the browser: `dialog.matches(':modal')` is true — so `showModal()` really ran, which is what supplies the trap, the inert background and the top layer — the `::backdrop` paints the same `rgba(8,10,13,0.42)` the old overlay did, focus sits on the input, arrows move the selection, and `cancel` closes it with the background usable again. | **The component had no tests at all** — 283 lines of the one surface that exists to be used without a mouse. 11 written; run against the previous version **4 fail and 7 pass**, which is the honest shape of it: the four are the modal mechanism, and the seven passing are the keyboard behaviour that was already right. **2382 web tests** pass. |
