Three new tests in `test_landing_downloads.py`; **1932 backend tests** pass. The local tap was removed afterwards. **Then rehearsed for real**, on a throwaway clone at HEAD with a throwaway tag: `manage.py release` builds 325 files into a **19 MB** tarball — inside the host's 25 MB cap — writes the checksum, the launchers and the formula, and the formula it writes now passes `brew style` with `brew info` reporting version `0.1.0-test` from URL `throughline-v0.1.0-test.tar.gz`. The tarball contains `scripts/manage.py`, `VERSION` and the exported interface under a single top-level directory, which is what the formula's shim expects, and `manage.py --help` prints `bootstrap` and exits 0, which is what its `test do` block asserts. **Two prerequisites surfaced by rehearsing rather than reasoning**: the release needs the interface exported first (`build-interface`, 51 MB into `apps/web/out`, gitignored), and the dirty-tree guard is real — it refused the clone until the change was committed. **The remaining work is publication and is not mine to do** — see D058. |
| T098 | A release cannot break the dev server on its way out | dist | done | Claude | — | **Done.** `build-interface` now refuses while something is answering the web dev port, and says how to proceed. The two cannot share a checkout: `NEXT_DIST_DIR=out` sends the *export* to `apps/web/out` and that part works, but Next still writes its build manifests through `.next`, which is where `next dev` keeps its own — so the dev server starts reading files the build has replaced and every route answers 500 with `ENOENT: .next/static/development/_buildManifest.js.tmp.*`. **This matters for shipping, not just for development**: `manage.py release` calls `build_interface` when no export exists, so cutting a release with the dev server up breaks it as a side effect. A socket probe rather than a process list, because `pgrep` is not everywhere and the question is whether the port is served. `--force` for anyone who knows. Closes **D133**. | Reproduced deliberately twice: with a healthy dev server, `build-interface` took `/workspace` and `/gesture-check` from 200 to 500 and put twenty errors in its log, and the build reported success throughout. 5 tests, mutation-tested — removing the guard trips a deliberate `pytest.fail` planted in `_node_on_path`, so the test cannot pass by asserting nothing. **1937 backend tests** pass. |
| T099 | What a `brew install` actually produces, run end to end | dist | done | Claude | — | **Done — verification, not new code.** The Homebrew route was built (T097) and validated as *text*; what had never happened is anyone installing from the artefact and starting it. Rehearsed the whole thing: built a release from a clean clone, unpacked the tarball the way Homebrew unpacks it (`--strip-components=1` into a `libexec`), wrote the shim exactly as the formula writes it, and ran what a researcher would run. **It works.** The tarball carries `scripts`, `packages`, `services`, `apps`, `keys`, `launchers`, `README.md` and `VERSION`, and carries no `.git`, no `node_modules` and no `tests` — which is what `release.py` says it excludes and now what it demonstrably excludes. The formula's `test do` block passes. `bootstrap` installs all nine workspace packages, applies every migration, boots the bundled PostgreSQL and reports `Ready`. `doctor` then passes **8 of 8 and exits 0**. | Two of my own readings were wrong before they were right, and both are worth recording: `doctor` appeared to exit 0 while reporting failures — that was `$?` after a pipe returning `head`'s status, not the script's; and `bootstrap`'s silence looked like a missing progress report until I checked `start`, which is what the formula tells people to run and which prints "First run — setting this up… Leave this window open." Neither was a defect. Ran under `THROUGHLINE_HOME` pointed at a temp directory, and verified afterwards that the rehearsal built its own 42 MB `pgdata` there and left `~/.throughline-os` untouched — D116's trap, avoided deliberately. The 814 MB rehearsal was removed. |
| T100 | The shell dividers are wide enough to grab | shell | done | Claude | — | **Done.** Both dividers computed to **zero width**, so the resize feature was keyboard-only and invisible. `react-resizable-panels` writes `flex: 0 0 auto` as an inline style on every separator, and an inline declaration beats a stylesheet whatever its specificity — so `.shell-divider { flex: 0 0 9px }` was discarded in full. With `flex-basis: auto` the used basis is the width, so the rule sets `width` instead and stops fighting a declaration it cannot win against. Closes **D134**. | Found by sweeping for elements that are focusable and invisible — the class `.pm-del` belonged to (D120) — rather than by looking at the panels again. Verified in the browser after the fix: both dividers 9px, the hairline painted, `cursor: col-resize`, and a synthesized pointer drag moving the rail 229 → 302 against a 72px gesture. 4 tests, mutation-tested; the mutation also caught a fault in the test's own example, which was passing a whole rule where the matcher expects a rule body. **2416 web tests** pass. |
| T101 | Text meets the contrast it needs, in both themes | shell | done | Claude | — | **Done.** Measured every rendered text node against its real background, in both themes, across nine screens — 15 distinct failures on the Overview screen alone before the sweep. `--ink-faint` was **2.36:1** in dark and 3.29:1 in light against the 4.5:1 normal text needs, and it carries real content: rail group labels, the context note, step descriptions, captions. Lifted to the first compliant step of the existing scale in each theme — n-500 in light (5.36:1), n-600 in dark (6.74:1) — because the scale flips and the same name is a different colour. `--caution` and `--positive` never got dark variants although their sibling `--warn` did, and `--caution` is the *same* colour as light `--warn`, so it now shares the dark one. `--accent` was chosen against a white page and never flipped: 3.44:1 as text on a dark ground, and the stylesheet uses it as text in **45 places** against 10 as background, so the failing role was the common one. Closes **D135**, **D136**, **D137**, **D138**, **D139**. | Ends at **zero failures across all 21 sections in both themes**, measured rather than inspected. Every value taken from the palette rather than invented — and twice the accessible value was already in the file, offered only through `prefers-contrast: more`. 2 new guards, mutation-tested. **2418 web tests** pass. |
| T102 | The pages outside the workspace get the same treatment | shell | done | Claude | — | **Done.** The contrast and layout sweeps had covered the 21 workspace sections and none of the four standalone pages. `/air-ink` turned out to use **43 inline colour literals and not one design token** — greys like `#333`, `#555`, `#777` and pale blues like `#f4f7fd` chosen against a white page, rendering on the dark one. Measured: 1.55:1, 2.01:1, 2.35:1, 2.63:1. Every literal is now a token, and the drawing colours are untouched because they come from `INK_COLOURS` rather than from the markup. Two more buttons on `/charts-3d` and `/gesture-check` were falling back to the browser's grey default. Closes **D140**, **D141**. | `/air-ink` ends **clean in both themes**; `/charts-3d` and `/gesture-check` clean. **2418 web tests** pass. One reported problem was investigated and dismissed: `/air-ink` showed 249px of horizontal overflow, which was the browser pane being 497px wide — below any width the product supports. At 1280 it is −15. |
| T103 | Correction has to discriminate, not merely refuse | interpretation | done | Claude | — | **Done — a gap found by asking a strategic question empirically rather than arguing it.** The claim under test was that the rigour engine is domain-neutral enough to be repositioned as evaluation infrastructure for ML and autonomy. Rather than assert it, the real `enquiry` and `exploration` modules were fed a model-selection sweep: 200 checkpoints against an incumbent, all drawn from the null. Ten looked "significant" at p<0.05, the best at **p = 0.00054** — a number that would pass review almost anywhere — and **none survived correction**. Repeated with five genuine effects planted among the 200: fourteen looked significant, **all five real ones survived and none of the nine spurious ones did**. The engine needed no modification; the only concession was passing `verb="discovery"`, because the ledger's vocabulary is research-bound (`paper_reconciliation`, `image_similarity`) even though its arithmetic is not. **That exercise exposed a real gap in the suite**, which is what this task fixes: nothing asserted that a strong result survives a *large* family. | `test_a_strong_result_still_survives_a_large_family`, and the mutation that justifies it: a correction dividing the FDR by the family size — right at a family of one, too harsh at two hundred — fails **only this test** while the other 21 in the file pass. An earlier draft of its docstring claimed no existing test would catch a correction that rejected everything; mutation showed that was false (the neighbouring test's first assertion catches it) and the claim was corrected rather than left standing. **1938 backend tests** pass. |
| T104 | Where a vocabulary is written twice, the copies agree | interpretation | done | Claude | — | **Done, and wider than it started.** `exploration.VERBS` and `exploration_tests_verb_check` are the same list in two languages with nothing holding them together — the shape this codebase already names as *"five places named the release host and none of them agreed until a test held them together."* Sweeping for the pattern found **eleven enumerated columns in the schema and three with a Python twin**: `verb`/`VERBS`, `predicted_direction`/`DIRECTIONS`, and `kind`/`marks.KINDS`. All three agreed; none was guarded. The two drifts fail differently, so both are caught: a value added to Python alone passes the module's own check — the one that reads as authoritative — then dies in the INSERT on a constraint the caller never saw; a value added to the schema alone is refused for something the store would hold. Constraints are read from `pg_constraint` rather than the migrations, because `0026` created the verb constraint with six verbs and `0040` rebuilt it with seven — the file that creates a constraint is not the file that defines it. Closes **D142**. | Six tests. Mutation-tested on all three pairs: shrinking `DIRECTIONS` or growing `KINDS` each fails exactly one. A completeness test fails when the schema grows an enumeration the list has not been told about — verified by removing one entry, which reports `['variable_aliases_status_check']` by name. Pairs are declared rather than discovered by content, because a matcher that guesses finds no pair after a rename and passes in silence, which is this file's own failure mode wearing a different hat. **1944 backend tests** pass. |
| T105 | One formula, and the right one | dist | done | Claude | — | **Done.** Two Homebrew formulas existed: the one `release.py` generates, and a hand-written `packaging/homebrew/Formula/throughline.rb`. Two sources of truth for the install path is the defect T104 had just been fixing twice, in its most consequential instance. The hand-written one looked more thorough and was **wrong on the point that mattered**: `depends_on "postgresql@16"`, with caveats telling people to `brew services start postgresql@16`. `db.py` opens by saying *"a desktop researcher must never be asked to install one"* and uses `pgserver`, which ships its own server as a pip wheel — so that formula installed something the product never talks to and asked for a step that does nothing. It also proposed running `bootstrap` during `brew install`, which Homebrew sandboxes without network. **What it got right is now folded in**: `depends_on "python@3.12"` and a shim that uses it, because `runtimes.py` says *"the documented install has always asked for Python 3.12 exactly"* and `/usr/bin/env python3` is whatever happens to be on PATH. Its observation that there is no LICENSE file is kept as a comment; `brew audit` flagging that is the correct outcome. Closes **D143**. | `brew style` clean against the real Homebrew 6.0.18 after one further fix it caught — `Formula[...].opt_bin` is deprecated in favour of `formula_opt_bin(...)`, which the hand-written version would also have failed. Guarded by a test, mutation-tested both ways: restoring the PostgreSQL dependency fails it, and so does removing the Python pin. **1945 backend tests** pass. |
| T106 | A report says how much looking stands behind its q | communication | done | Claude | — | **Done — the rigour now reaches the artefact a researcher hands to somebody else.** `resolve_block` already argued the point in its own docstring: a corrected q *"is a property of that test within its multiple-testing family, and the same run in a family of five and a family of five hundred yields different q."* The ledger argued it too. And the drafted report quoted q and said nothing about the family, so a reviewer could not tell a first look from a two-hundredth — the most important number in the document resting on trust, in a product whose whole argument is that nothing should. A third reference source, `family_of_connection_id`, walks connection → discovery run → line of enquiry → ledger, and the draft states the family it was corrected in and how many survived. Closes **D144**. | 7 tests. `draft_from_connection` had **no direct test at all** before this — only the API route called it, and the file's own comment records a bug that survived because of it. Mutation-tested: removing the sentence fails 3, resolving the family from the wrong place fails 1. **The product's own guard caught my first draft**: `add_block` rejected "survive at a 5% false-discovery rate" with `LiteralNumberRejected`, on a literal I had not thought of as a statistic — and it was right twice, since the rate is a property of the run rather than a constant. **1952 backend tests** pass. |
| T107 | A globe is drawn as a globe | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done.** The catalogue's own measurement opened this: 253 entries resolving to 15 distinct pictures, with three globes among them drawn by the `surface` renderer as height fields on a flat grid. Most of those collisions are legitimate — a paraboloid and a saddle genuinely are one renderer — but a globe drawn as a plane is the wrong shape, not undifferentiated styling. New `globe` primitive, with the geometry in `lib/charts3d/globe.ts` as arithmetic that can be tested without a canvas: great-circle densification, back-face culling by the outward normal, rings kept separate. Culling is checked against the real 110m topology as well as hand-built polygons, on the property that opposite views of a sphere partition it — a country hidden from here is visible from directly behind. That test failed first and was right to: with a pitched camera, yaw + π is *not* the opposite view, and a band around the far pole hides from both. Reachable from `/charts-3d` for the reason that page exists, and drawn with **no data at all** — `GENERATORS.places` stays `null` because a value invented for a real country reads as a fact about the world. Two defects found by opening it in a browser after the suite was green (D146, D147). Verified live: an 843×843 sphere filling 92% of the frame, 34 countries hidden facing Eurasia against 136 facing the Pacific, deterministic across reloads, Home restoring exactly. |
| T108 | The address bar says where you are | shell | done | Claude | — | **Done, with one part unverified and said so.** Found by asking what navigation actually costs a researcher rather than whether it looked tidy: every screen in the product was `/workspace`. `lib/section-url.ts` holds the meaning of the address as pure functions, so what a URL denotes is testable without a DOM — an unknown or hostile `?section=` falls back to Overview rather than throwing, because a bookmark outlives a rename and an error page blames the reader for something that is not theirs; the default section is written as a bare path so the front door is not a URL that looks like a redirect; and other parameters survive a navigation. `SECTION_IDS` duplicates the `Section` union deliberately — a type is gone at runtime and a query string is typed by a stranger — and a test holds the two copies together on `test_vocabularies_agree`'s reasoning, mutation-tested in both directions. A first measurement of route-level navigation was **wrong and worth recording**: a grep for `href="/x"` reported `/air-ink` and `/case-compare` as orphans, when `Shell.tsx` links them as `href: "/x"` object properties — and `pages-are-reachable.test.ts` already walks the graph from the landing page properly. |
| T109 | Whether the spatial charts are actually spatial | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done — the honest answer to "are they immersive" turned out to be an accessibility finding.** Measured rather than judged: of seven rotatable canvases, six had no key handler, no `tabIndex` and (five) no label, and the depth cues per renderer were counted one by one. §10 is *DO NOT FORCE 3D*, so immersion as spectacle is against the product's law — but depth **cues** are not decoration, they are what makes a third dimension legible rather than misleading, and that is what was missing. Two guards added and both mutation-tested. The keyboard guard **failed its first mutation**: it looked for the string `useSpatialKeys` anywhere in the file, so deleting `{...spatialKeys}` from the canvas left every test green with the chart back to mouse-only. It now asks whether the props reach an element. |
| T110 | What the network layout costs, and what it claimed | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done — and the headline is a limit, not a win.** Followed the gap named at the end of T109: depth cues earn their keep at 500 nodes, and nothing had measured 500 nodes. Barnes-Hut over an octree, reused across passes, chosen above a measured crossover of 800 and the all-pairs loop kept below it. At 5000 nodes 12.2s becomes 6.1s; at 2000, 1.9s becomes 1.4s. **Both are still a frozen interface, and the docstring now says so in those words** rather than leaving the impression the problem is solved — the remaining work is getting the layout off the main thread, which is what the false claim had been hiding. Three defects found (D152-D154), two of them in my own new code, and one near-miss worth recording: a browser check reported the network canvas painting **zero pixels**, which was a measurement artefact from a tab being torn down mid-call, not a defect — re-measured at 8,690 pixels against 8,748 before the change. |
| T111 | The catalogue may not misdescribe what this product can do | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done — every defect here was found by a person reading the rendered list, with 2,475 tests green.** Three false statements, all of the same kind: the catalogue is the product's claim about itself, and the existing guards only checked it against *itself*. The new guard checks negative claims against `package.json` and asserts no entry named "globe" is filed under a primitive that draws flat grids. One reported problem was **withdrawn after reading the code**: the tail entries that look like duplicates ("Multivariable function plot" beside "Multivariable function") are deliberate, and the note says so — "named as the brief names it" — so the catalogue matches the requirements vocabulary rather than having grown redundant rows. |
| T112 | Audit every claim the catalogue makes, one at a time | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done — a granular sweep of all 253 entries after three defects in a row were found by a reader rather than by the suite.** Two checks came back clean and are worth recording as such: the data-shape/primitive cross-tab is coherent (`geometry`→`mesh` 33/33, `graph`→`network` 35/35, `places`→`globe` 4/4, and the three outliers are all correct — a streamline through a field *is* a line), and the tail entries that read as duplicates are deliberate. The real finding was structural: the catalogue's strongest negative claim was made 24 times with no reason attached, so nothing could check it — and 20 of the 24 turned out to be wrong once each was written down. Four survive. **Three existing tests pinned the old classification and each was read before being changed**; every one of their guarantees is intact, and only the admission each entry makes has changed. |
| T113 | Verify the claim the last reclassification rested on | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done, and the reading it was checking turned out to be right for the wrong reason.** T112 moved 15 entries out of `needs-library` on the strength of *reading* that the mesh path sets no scalar range; that gap was named in the handover as needing a real file. Building one found two defects the suite could not: `.vtp` never opened at all, and once it did, the field was drawn — badly — rather than not drawn. So the classification stands and its stated reason is now accurate, which it was not before. Two counts were also checked and came back **clean**: the specialist mount says "21" and there are exactly 21 entries carrying a viewer, and STL and PLY genuinely do take `parse`. |
| T114 | Check the viewers against the libraries, not against their doubles | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done, and it is mostly a clean result.** D161 was invisible because the test double was healthier than the real library — a stand-in reader with a `parse` the real `XMLPolyDataReader` has never had — so every library call in all four specialist viewers was resolved against the installed packages by hand. **Only vtk.js was wrong**; Mol*, niivue and deck.gl all resolve, including the whole `builders` chain and `deck.finalize`. Worth recording that **three of my own path guesses were wrong during the audit** — `IO/Geometry/OBJReader` (it is `IO/Misc`), Mol*'s `builder/` (not `builders/`), and `parseAsArrayBuffer` looking absent from `XMLPolyDataReader` because it is declared on the base `XMLReader`. Two of those produced a confident "NOT FOUND" against working code, which is what the new guard exists to stop being a matter of care. |
| T115 | Draw the field, not just the shape | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done — five entries moved from "renderer not written" to drawable, by writing the renderer.** They were one missing capability wearing five names. The build is small and the care is in what it refuses: a constant field is left uncoloured, because every colour in the bar would stand for one value and the range is zero-width; a file with no array says so rather than showing a silent shape; and the legend and the ramp are the same object, so the bar cannot misreport the picture. Verified with a `.vtp` built for the purpose — a radial stress field, 0 at the centre to 240 MPa at the corners — which had previously rendered as a flat blue sheet with one hot spot and now renders as the gradient it is. **Six entries remain deliberately undrawable** and each says why: mode shapes need stepping, deformation needs a warp, an exploded assembly needs a part transform, a digital twin needs a live binding, and two simulations need a physics engine. |
| T116 | Stop the networks all drawing the same graph | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done — and the diagnosis I started with was wrong, which is worth recording.** I measured "216 drawable → 13 distinct pictures" and concluded that named surfaces like Paraboloid and Torus were rendering as generic shapes. Reading the code showed `SHAPES` already existed and already covered all twenty of the named objects I was about to write, with the same formulas; my count had ignored it entirely. Re-measured with the codebase's own `sharesPictureWith`, the real figure was **23 unique pictures**, and the real gap was the networks. Now 31. The generic entries — "3D network", "Concept graph" — deliberately keep sharing: a graph is what they are, and giving each a random layout would make 35 pictures and 35 claims, none of them true. |
| T117 | Read the page as a reader, not as its author | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done — four defects, all found by reading the rendered page, with 2,504 tests green.** Two are arithmetic the suite could not see because every individual number was right and only the word joining them was wrong. Two of the four fixes were **initially unguarded**: reverting the caption fix passed all 35 conformance tests and reverting the filter fix passed all 2,506, so both got tests before this was called done. The pattern across the last six sessions is now unmistakable — the defects live in what the product says about itself, and internal consistency has never once caught one. |
| T118 | Close the gaps I had listed as open | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done — three of five, and the first one moved because a claim of mine was wrong.** I had recorded the workspace navigation as unverifiable because "this machine has no PostgreSQL". ADR-0001 says the product ships Postgres as a Python wheel (`pgserver`) precisely so a researcher never installs one; `./scripts/dev.sh` brings up Postgres, migrations, the API on 8080, a worker and the web app in one command, and it did. The remaining blocker is a sign-in gate, which is the researcher's to pass, not mine. |
| T119 | Close the last of the open gaps | charts | done | Claude | — | **Done — and the one I had called impossible was verified live.** The workspace navigation was recorded as unverifiable for want of PostgreSQL; ADR-0001 ships it as a Python wheel and `./scripts/dev.sh` brought up the database, migrations, API, worker and web app in one command. With a researcher signed in: a rail click moves the address bar (`?section=reports` → `figures` → `notebook`), **Back steps one section at a time** rather than leaving the product, the heading follows the URL, a deep link to `?section=connections` opens Connections, and a reload keeps it. Four defects were found *while* closing the gap, three of them in the new code and two of them invisible to a green suite. |
| T120 | Check the ledger against the product | interpretation | done | Claude | — | **Done, and the first thing it found was that I had duplicated an existing guard.** I wrote a new ledger test before looking for one; `test_requirements_ledger.py` already had twelve checks, including the file-existence check I had just written and an unreviewed-count ratchet. My file was deleted and only the genuinely new check contributed — the same correction this session made once before. Two clean results worth recording: **all 83 built/partial rows name paths that exist**, so the ledger has never overstated, and the drift is one-directional. A caution on the headline number: 140 unreviewed includes phases, worked examples and north-star prose that can never be built, so it is not a backlog of 140 things. |
| T121 | Give three unassessed sections a verdict | interpretation | done | Claude | — | **Done — read against the specification rather than pattern-matched, which changed two of the three answers.** §62 STATISTICS SYSTEM is `partial`: descriptive statistics, three correlations, t-test, Mann-Whitney, Kruskal-Wallis, ANOVA, chi-square and linear regression are registered and conformance-checked against reference implementations — **logistic regression and mixed models are absent**, and the `"mixed"` a grep finds in `claim_test.py` is a verdict label (`Family.QUALIFIED`), not a model. §73 CITATIONS is `partial`: citation objects go *past* what the section asks, since entailment is checked per claim rather than asserted by the author — but **bibliography export does not exist** and the section names it. §84 AI DATA BOUNDARIES is `partial`: three of four modes, "make external transmission understandable" met by `capability()` reporting `local=False`, **organisation-level controls absent**. Each names a test that evidences the claim rather than merely existing — the ledger guard checks the path, not the relevance, so that part is still judgement. `MAX_UNREVIEWED` ratchets 137 → 134. |
| T122 | Get the references back out | communication | done | Claude | — | **Done — the gap I recorded last turn, built.** Two properties carry it and both are mutation-tested. **Nothing is invented**: a dropped PDF often has a title and no author, and `Anonymous, n.d.` renders as a real reference in a manuscript where an empty field is visible — missing fields are omitted and reported in `missing_fields`, and an entry with no journal is `@misc` rather than an `@article` with a dangling comma. **A key is stable and unique**: it is what a researcher types into their document, so the same project exports the same bytes, and two papers that would collide get suffixes rather than BibTeX silently keeping one. A citation pointing at an analysis run is counted rather than dropped, because "the number came from run 41" is a real citation and not a bibliography entry. The project's own house-style guard caught `citation(s)` in my empty-file comment and sent me to `throughline_schemas.words.counted`. |
| T123 | Ask whether the interface has requirements behind it | interpretation | done | Claude | — | **Done — the inverse of the ledger audit, and it found an overclaim of mine.** The ledger asks "does this requirement have code"; nobody had asked "does this code have a requirement". Of 24 navigation entries, ten map to a numbered section; four (Variables, Findings, Analyses, Notebook) are named in §5 only as *object types*, not as places; two (Compare, Embedding space) appear only inside worked examples; and one — Find data — is not in the specification at all. **Three further claims in the first version of this row were wrong and are corrected here**: Overview is specified (*"Overview mode — see the architecture of the research project"*), Sources is specified as a capability (15 mentions of source, 18 of import, plus upload and ingest), and Patterns likewise (23 mentions of cluster). I had probed for the wrong phrases — `project overview` rather than `overview mode` — which is the same title-matching error as D183, made twice in one analysis. Two of my keyword matches were **wrong and nearly reported**: §37 DEICTIC REFERENCES is about understanding the phrase "Compare these" as language, and §98 DISCOVERABILITY is about hover cues, neither being a workspace section. Most striking: the specification never says "multiple comparisons", "false discovery" or "p-hacking" — the correction engine this project has spent most of its effort on is **unrequested**, while §54's canvas arrangement, which is requested, is not built. |

## Discovered

Found while doing something else. Recorded here rather than fixed silently, so
the cost of each is visible before anybody spends it.

| ID | Finding | Severity | Status | Owner | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D001 | A validated spec naming the normalised column name failed in the sandbox. Discovery emits exactly that spelling, so **every auto-discovered analysis failed on any file whose headers had spaces or capitals** — i.e. most real research exports. Invisible because every test dataset was already snake_case. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | `tests/test_human_headers.py` — 7 tests; the discovery test failed before the fix and passes after |
| D002 | `apps/api/.../app.py` defined 55 of its 99 routes more than once — **76 dead definitions**. FastAPI serves the first, so any edit landing in a later copy was a silent no-op. `/api/dataset-versions/{version_id}/density` was defined nine times. Wave 0 found one instance and treated it as a one-off; it was systemic. | high | **verified** | Sarthak | **Already fixed on `origin/main` (`8df65a6`) by Sarthak — do not spend time on it.** Verified independently by Fenil's session on 2026-08-14: the same AST count against `origin/main` gives 101 decorators, 101 distinct, 0 duplicated, 0 dead. My branch still shows 175/99/55/76 because it predates that merge. |
| D003 | `Matrix.tsx` and `SetRegions.tsx` held raw NUL bytes, which made git and grep treat them as binary and silently skip them. **They were not a defect** — the NUL was a deliberate key separator, chosen because it is the one character that cannot appear in a label. Removing it was tried and had to be reverted: it made `"ab"+"c"` collide with `"a"+"bc"` and turned `split(NUL)` into `split("")`. Now written as ``: identical behaviour, plain-ASCII source. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `apps/web/tests/chart-tables.test.tsx` — two tests that fail when the separator is removed. `tsc` and all 79 web tests passed while it *was* removed, which is why the tests exist. |
| D004 | `Cartesian.tsx:137` carried the comment "the figure describes itself, and a table follows" while no table followed, in any of the thirteen primitives. | low | done | Claude (Fenil) | Closed by T004 |
| D005 | `KnowledgeGraph.tsx` claimed "5,000 nodes at 60fps with a quadtree for hit-testing". **Both halves were wrong.** There is no quadtree — hit-testing is a linear scan (which measures 0.43ms and costs nothing, so the index is unnecessary rather than missing). 60fps holds only after the layout settles: one force tick at 5,000 nodes measures ~155ms against a 16.67ms budget, and ~300 ticks are needed, so that graph spends ~46s laying out at ~6fps. `d3-quadtree` is a declared dependency nothing imports. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `apps/web/tests/graph-performance.test.ts`; comment rewritten with the numbers. Real graphs are far smaller and settle in under a second — a ceiling nobody has hit, not a bug being lived with. |

It is present locally only because Wave 0 installed it by hand. On a fresh checkout that test skips, and CI's allowlist accepts three reasons, none of them this one — so the build fails on a library nothing declares, which is the exact class of defect that CI file was written to catch. Never seen because CI has never run on a Wave 0 or Wave 1 branch: it triggers on push-to-main and pull_request only, and no PR has been opened. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | `scripts/manage.py` now installs it with the other test-only dependencies. Verified locally by running CI's own allowlist grep against a `-rs` run: 625 passed, 9 skipped, all nine reasons allowlisted. |
| D008 | **Fixed.** The self-hosted fonts introduced layout shift: CLS **0.083** on the landing page over Slow 4G, against **0.000** with the font requests blocked. The shifting element is `div.l-layer`, a decorative `aria-hidden` parallax layer whose `top:-22%/height:144%` geometry tracks its container, so it moves when text reflows. Within Google's "good" (≤0.1) but 83% of the budget, and it was zero before the fonts. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | **Re-measured first, with per-element attribution, and it was worse than recorded: CLS 0.1044 at 1280x900 over Slow 4G — over Google's 0.1 "good" threshold — with 100% of it attributed to one element, `div.l-layer.l-depth-2.l-halo`, against 0.0000 with fonts blocked.** That layer is a decorative `aria-hidden` band 1.44 screens tall whose `top:-22%` / `height:144%` resolve against the hero, so the 107px reflow moved and resized it. **Fixed by sizing the hero's layers from the viewport instead** (`top:-22vh; height:200vh`) — `vh` does not reflow, and `.l-scene` clips overflow so over-covering is free. **CLS 0.1044 → 0.0558**, and `.l-layer` no longer appears as a shift source at all; mobile (390x844) is unchanged at 0.0287, where the hero is exactly one viewport and the layer never shifted. Neither the metric-matched fallback nor `font-display: optional` was needed, so the serif still paints on a first slow visit. **`max(144vh, 144%)` was written first and was wrong**: at 1280x900 the hero is 1115px so `144%` is 1606px, `max()` picks the percentage, and the height tracks the reflow exactly as before — a fallback that reintroduces the dependency is not a fallback. Coverage verified at 390/1024/1280 wide: the band overhangs the hero by 390-658px everywhere. Harness: `scripts/measure-cls.mjs`. **Caveat: the remaining 0.0558 is genuine content reflow** — the lede, CTA row and hero graph moving as the headline rewraps. Removing it needs `font-display: optional` or reserved space, which is a design trade, not a bug. |

A trial merge gives exactly **two conflicts, one hunk each** (`app.py`, `globals.css`) — small, but resolving `app.py` by taking main's side wholesale **silently makes the binned figure unreachable again**, which is the precise defect Wave 0 existed to fix. `hexbin` appears 0 times in main's `app.py` and `"cells"` twice, against 1 and 9 on the Wave 1 branch. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | `origin/main` (`8df65a6`) merged into the branch. Verified by route-set comparison against both parents: 101 distinct, 0 duplicates, no route lost from either side, file parses. 625 Python and 100 web tests pass, typecheck clean. |

| D028 | ~~**Throughline has no 3D visualization**~~ **— I was wrong, and the correction matters more than the original claim.**, which decides the order the Spatial Interaction work can be built in. I checked for Three.js, WebGL and Plotly and grepped `KnowledgeGraph.tsx`, and never looked at the chart primitives, where the 3D actually was. The conclusion I drew from that gap ("no 3D exists, so gesture work is premature") was wrong; what survives is the narrower and still-true half — Three.js is deliberately absent, because `Volume` reasons that a WebGL scene graph is ~600KB for a 4x4 matrix and a sort, on a product premised on installing locally. **The prerequisite was already met.** T015 wires the gesture layer to it. |
| D029 | **Spike rejection was operating on smoothed coordinates**, which made it dead code for the case it exists to catch. `rotate()` filtered the palm position and then compared *filtered* points against the spike threshold — but One Euro damps a single large jump so thoroughly that the jump never exceeded it. A tracker switching to another person's hand (§31) would have produced a smooth, confident, entirely fictional rotation. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by a mutation, not by review: deleting the guard changed nothing, which is what exposed it. Spikes are now judged on raw landmarks *before* smoothing, and a rejected frame is never fed to the filter — leaving the fiction in the filter's history would make the following frames drift toward a position the hand was never in. `::discards a tracking spike rather than rotating by it`, which asserts the frame is silent rather than merely small. |
| D010 | `render_artifact.render` writes every render of a format to one filename — `storage_root()/artifacts/{artifact_id}/{artifact_id}.{suffix}` — while inserting a **new** `artifact_renders` row each time. The rows record `byte_size` and `resolved_hash` for bytes that no longer exist, and any route serving a render by id hands back different content than the row describes. Found while building T007, which has to treat superseded rows as history rather than as retrievable copies for exactly this reason. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Filename now carries the **render id**, so every export is its own file and each row describes bytes that are actually present. `tests/test_communication.py::test_two_renders_of_one_format_are_two_files` and `::test_every_render_row_describes_the_bytes_that_are_actually_there`, both mutation-checked against the old filename. **The second test passed with the bug fully present on the first attempt** — two renders of unchanged content are byte-identical, so the stale row's size matched the file that replaced it; it now edits the document between renders. Cost: renders are no longer overwritten, so exports accumulate on disk. That is the right trade, because `artifact_staleness` exists to answer what a given export said. |

| D048 | **The web suite can fail one test in a way that leaves no name behind.** On `feat/specialist-viz`, one full run of the 1430-test suite reported `1 failed` and the four runs immediately after were green — the failing run's output was not captured, so which test flaked is unknown, which is itself the defect worth recording: a suite that is green four times out of five is not green, and D037 already showed this suite can be order-dependent (vitest schedules files in parallel, so ordering varies run to run). Until it recurs under logging, every "N passed" claim on this branch names how many consecutive runs produced it. | low | open | — | **Seen again on 2026-08-30, and this time it left names.** A full web run reported 2 failed / 2339 passed: `chart-conformance.test.tsx > 'Volume' honours the spatial contract > rotates by an amount somebody can see` and `embeddingspace.test.tsx > asking about what was selected > sends the selected point as coordinates, not as a summary`. Both passed in isolation immediately afterwards, and two consecutive full runs went 2341/2341. Both touch `Volume` and its spatial controller, which is the first common thread this defect has had — the earlier sighting recorded nothing. Not chased further here; recorded so the next occurrence has something to match against. **Root cause found, and it reduces to a two-second reproduction:** `pytest tests/test_observability.py tests/test_workflow.py tests/test_worker_durability.py` fails five tests without the fix and passes 44 with it — the same five the shuffle seeds found, in 2s rather than 13 minutes. Three tests in `test_observability.py` must call `cur.connection.commit()`, because `observability.health()` opens its own connection and cannot see an open transaction; the commit defeats the rollback that isolates every other test. What survives is a `system.echo` run in state `running` with an expired lease and **`project_id` NULL** — the worst shape available: `claim_next` treats an expired lease as a worker that died and hands the run over, and because the column is nullable, the tidy-up every other file does by deleting its user cascades to nothing. The next file to call `claim_next` gets that row instead of its own, and it is older, so `ORDER BY created_at` prefers it. It stayed hidden because in declaration order a file in between drains the queue with a real worker; shuffle and nothing does. **Fixed at both ends** — the three tests now clean up what they commit, and `empty_queue` states the precondition the readers were assuming. Either fix alone is sufficient, verified separately; both are kept because the first removes today's contamination and the second stops the next one. **The same instrument then found order dependence in the *backend* suite, which is a separate defect and a worse one.** pytest honours the order of file arguments, so shuffling that list needs no plugin. Two seeds, both green in declaration order: seed 1 failed `test_worker_durability.py::test_worker_completes_a_queued_run`; seed 2 failed four tests in `test_workflow.py` — `test_claim_is_exclusive`, `test_expired_lease_is_reclaimed_after_a_worker_dies`, `test_heartbeat_only_extends_your_own_lease`, `test_retries_are_bounded_then_the_run_fails`. One cause: `claim_next` and `Worker.run_once` take the next claimable run in the *whole table*, and each of these asserts that what came back is the run it just enqueued. That is only true while nothing else is queued, and nothing made it true — it held by accident of declaration order. Fixed with an `empty_queue` fixture that states the precondition and guarantees it; both seeds pass with it. **The fixture is the right fix even at zero leaks**: owning the queue is a precondition these tests need, not a property of the system, and the next test that legitimately leaves a run queued would break them again. **Which file leaks is still unidentified** — every file that commits a project also deletes its user, and `workflow_runs.project_id` cascades from `projects`, so the obvious route is closed; a leak simulated by hand proves nothing because the session fixture empties every table before the first test. **Hunted under logging, not reproduced — and the hunt found a different, real one.** Six consecutive default `npx vitest run` invocations: green, 2010/2010 each. Shuffling *file* order (`--sequence.shuffle.files`) across four seeds: green. So the original sighting is still unidentified and this stays open. **What did reproduce** is within-*file* order dependence, which a default run never varies and so is not the same defect: `--sequence.shuffle.tests --sequence.seed=3` failed three tests in `tests/claimtest.test.tsx`, deterministically, on that file alone. Cause: the stored-claims block set `api.get` with `mockResolvedValue` — persistent, not `Once` — and reset only in its own `beforeEach`. Reorder the blocks and that mock survives into "located claims", whose tests then take the *read the record* path instead of the *re-read the paper* path, render the stored claim, and never post at all. Those are two different acts and the block exists to tell them apart, so it was silently exercising the wrong one. Fixed with a file-level `beforeEach` reset; verified green across twelve seeds, and removing the reset makes seed 3 fail again. Full suite green across eight `--sequence.shuffle` seeds afterwards. **Recipe for the next sighting**, since it is now known to work: `npx vitest run --sequence.shuffle --sequence.seed=N` bisects order dependence far faster than repetition does. Observed once on 2026-08-25 (first full run after the T076–T080 build; 94 files, 1429/1430). Not reproduced in four immediately consecutive `npx vitest run` invocations. No identity captured — the run was not logged. If seen again: `npx vitest run 2>&1 \| tee` first, then bisect with `--sequence.shuffle`. |
| D049 | **The launchers started Throughline without opening it, and told installed copies their interface was missing.** Two faults in the same path, both found by asking what actually happens when somebody double-clicks. First: `start` brought the stack up and printed a URL. For the person T071 names — *"somebody who has never opened a terminal"* — that is a window of log output and nothing visibly happening; they must notice an address in the text and know to paste it into a browser, which is the step the launcher existed to remove. Second, and worse: `dev()` printed *"Web interface unavailable — Node 20+ is not installed"* whenever Node was absent, but since **T072** the API serves the exported interface itself — so it claimed an absence on exactly the installation where the interface *is* available, at the API's own port. §123 in reverse: an unavailability that is not real is as wrong as a capability that is not. **Both fixed.** `start` opens a browser once the app answers rather than when the port accepts — the socket listens seconds before anything is served, and a browser opened on the accept shows a connection error. `dev` deliberately does not open one, which is the difference between the two commands. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `tests/test_launcher_reporting.py` and `tests/test_launchers.py` — 7 tests. **Three mutants killed**: the headless opt-out removed, `dev` opening by default, and opening after nothing ever answered. The "start opens one" assertion is behavioural rather than a source grep and fails when `start` stops passing it. `THROUGHLINE_NO_BROWSER=1` declines it, for a machine with no browser. **Renumbered from D048.** Another session took that number for a different finding at 17:29 while this one was being written, and both reached `main` — the exact collision the ledger protocol warns about, and the second time it has happened here (D010/D011 were the first). Theirs was on `main` first, so this one moved, per the rule. Commit `ac237eb`'s message still says D048 and cannot be corrected without rewriting pushed history; this row is the correction. |
| D050 | **The advertised install line does not work for anybody outside the repository.** `README.md` and `scripts/install.sh` both publish `curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/.../scripts/install.sh` — and the repository is private, so that URL returns **404** before a byte runs. Even handed the script, its `git clone` of `https://github.com/SarthakPattnaik1/throughline-os.git` fails with *could not read Username*. Both tested as an unauthenticated stranger, the second with credential helpers explicitly disabled, because git had silently used a cached token on the first attempt and made it look like it worked. **T073 inherits it**: `updates.check()` runs `git fetch origin`, so an installed copy can only check for updates if its clone still has working credentials — reported honestly as *not knowing* rather than as up to date, but it cannot update. The reasoning behind `curl \| sh` is untouched (quarantine is set by the downloading browser, so a terminal line carries no Gatekeeper or SmartScreen warning); what is missing is that nothing can be downloaded at all. **Not a defect in the launchers** — T081 made them the right artifact — but there is nowhere to host one. | high | open | — | Measured: `curl` of the raw URL returns HTTP 404; `git -c credential.helper= ls-remote` returns `fatal: could not read Username for 'https://github.com'`. `gh repo view` reports `isPrivate: true`. The decision is a product one and not mine: tagged **release assets** carrying the prebuilt 35 MB interface is the option that fits a private product and is where T073 was already heading; making the repository public would also un-meter Actions and resolve D034, but that is a different product. **State of play, 2026-08-26 — everything below is done except one decision.** The release *signing key now exists*: `keys/release.pub` is committed (`22bea19`) and the private half is saved outside the repo at `~/throughline-release-key.pem`, verified as a matching pair by signing a manifest and refusing a tampered one. **The only thing blocking distribution is choosing the host name.** It must be wired into two places that have to agree — `TL_RELEASES` in `frontend/assemble.mjs` (the landing page's download links) and `RELEASE_URL` in `throughline_domain/updates.py` (where an installed copy checks). Both currently hold the placeholder `releases.throughline.tools`. If they ever drift, downloads keep working while updates silently find nothing — with no error, because a copy that cannot reach its server reports *could not check* rather than *up to date*. **Decided:** a Cloudflare Pages project is enough to start; no custom domain is needed, `*.pages.dev` survives adding one later, and the 19 MB tarball fits under the per-file cap for now (R2 when it grows). **The name is permanent in practice**, because the URL is baked into every release and installed copies poll it forever. Remaining sequence once the name is chosen: wire both places in one commit, run `THROUGHLINE_RELEASE_KEY=~/throughline-release-key.pem ./.venv/bin/python scripts/manage.py release` and confirm it prints *signed*, `TL_RELEASES=https://<host> node frontend/assemble.mjs`, upload `dist/` plus `Main.dc.html`, then fetch a download link unauthenticated and confirm it is not a 404 — that last check is what actually closes this. **Update, 2026-08-26 (later the same day).** The name is chosen and wired: **`throughline-research.pages.dev`**, hyphenated to match `throughline-os` and because the run-together form misreads. Both spellings were free; the choice was made before the Pages project existed, which is the only window in which it is free to make. `throughline.pages.dev` was checked first and is **taken by an unrelated product** (*Throughline Health*, prior-authorization software) — verified by probing a name that certainly does not exist, which returns NXDOMAIN, against `throughline` which resolves and serves HTTP 200. `throughline-app` is taken too. Baking the requested name in would have pointed every installed copy's update check at a stranger's server, permanently. **Two things this row got wrong.** First, it called the host name the only thing blocking distribution; it was not — `install.sh` and `Throughline.bat` both still reached into the private repository, so hosting alone would have produced a download that 404s nowhere and then fails at the clone. That is now **T087**. Second, it named two places that must agree; there are **five** (the page, the updater, `install.py`, `install.sh`, and the `.bat`), and nothing tested that any of them agreed — the comment in `updates.py` claiming the host “lives in one place” was false. `test_every_front_door_names_the_same_host` now holds all five together and is mutation-checked against each drifting on its own. **What is left is hosting only, and it is not mine to do**: create the Cloudflare Pages project named `throughline-research`, upload `dist/` plus the landing page as `index.html` at the site root, and run the unauthenticated 404 check above. **R2 was considered and declined for now, deliberately.** The question was whether to move to R2 immediately rather than wait for the tarball to outgrow Cloudflare Pages' 25 MiB per-file limit (it is at 18.2 MiB, ~73%). Declined because R2 without a custom domain means `pub-<hash>.r2.dev`, which Cloudflare's own documentation calls *"rate-limited and should only be used for development purposes"*, *"intended for non-production traffic"*, with CNAMEs to it *"an unsupported access path"* — a worse thing to bake into every release forever than the cap it avoids. **Pages does not trap us**, which is the load-bearing fact: adding a custom domain later does not retire the `.pages.dev` address, so copies installed today keep updating while new releases advertise a domain, and R2 can then sit behind that same domain through a Pages Function with an R2 binding. Going to `r2.dev` now is the option that forces a hard hostname break later. **`throughline.research.pages.dev` is not available as a shape** — `*.pages.dev` grants one label; the nested form is reserved for preview deployments under a project you own, and it 404s. **The trigger for revisiting is now measured rather than remembered**: `release.build` reports remaining headroom past 85% of the limit and says plainly when an archive cannot be uploaded at all, so the wall is met with warning rather than by a failed upload weeks after the commit that caused it. The real fix whenever it comes is a **custom domain**, not R2 by itself. |

Nothing needed unblocking; the option was there the whole time and nobody looked. **What that verified, all of it previously unrun anywhere:** the `.bat` launcher parses and executes on real Windows, finds Python through `py -3`, resolves its install directory and invokes `manage.py start` — T071's row said it "cannot be verified from WSL" and that was true only of running it *inside* WSL. T070's **wrong-version re-exec**, which had no test anywhere: Windows has Python 3.14, so bootstrap reported "This is Python 3.14, and 3.12 is required", fetched `cpython-3.12.14+20260814-x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`, verified it against the committed digest and re-executed under it. The **Windows CPython pin** downloads and unpacks. **pgserver boots on Windows** and applied all 39 migrations. And the **release tarball works as an install medium** — a stranger's install path, driven for the first time. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | Unpacked `throughline-895feed.tar.gz` into `C:\\Users\\Fenil\\tl-win`, bootstrapped with the Windows `py -3`, and confirmed the result imports: 139 routes, and `version.current()` reporting `source: "release"` from the shipped VERSION file. **macOS remains genuinely untested** — there is no Mac here and no interop trick for one, so D034 still blocks that half. |
| D052 | **The landing page went live as a blank black screen, and every network request returned 200.** `frontend/Main.dc.html` is a Claude Design *canvas artboard*, not a web page: it opens with `<script src="./support.js">` and wraps its content in `<x-dc>` and `<helmet>`, all of which resolve only inside the canvas editor. Uploaded to Cloudflare Pages it renders nothing. **The reason it is hard to diagnose is the 200s.** Pages falls back to `index.html` for unknown paths, so the request for the missing `support.js` is answered with HTML; the browser fails on a syntax error rather than a 404, and a network tab shows success throughout. Nothing 404s, nothing logs, the page is simply empty. **Not a new defect so much as an unverified instruction.** `frontend/README.md` said *"Upload `Main.dc.html` as the page"* and D050's closing sequence said *"upload `dist/` plus `Main.dc.html`"*. Both were followed. `frontend/tools/mkharness.mjs` already produced the correct standalone page and was described as a screenshot rig, so nobody read it as the deploy step — it lifts `<helmet>` into a real `<head>`, `<x-dc>` into `<body>`, and supplies the component base class and props the canvas runtime would have. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | Diagnosed against the live host: `curl` of `/` returns the artboard, and `/support.js` returns **HTTP 200 carrying HTML**. Fixed by publishing the port instead, and the deploy note rewritten to name it. Evidence: `tests/test_landing_downloads.py` +4, **mutation-checked** — making `mkharness` emit the artboard unchanged fails `test_the_published_page_stands_on_its_own`, and the old README sentence fails `test_the_deploy_note_names_the_page_that_works`. The built page is also asserted to keep every download link, so the port cannot silently drop the thing the page is for. **Verified visually rather than only structurally**: rendered headless through the repository's own Playwright rig at two scroll positions — the shader, the headline, the badges and *Skip to installer* all paint. That check existed (`frontend/tools/shot.mjs`) and had never been pointed at the question of whether the *published* artifact renders. |
| D053 | **The only Download button on the landing page was on screen for about two wheel notches.** The finale's `content` value — the name, the tagline and the Download button — was `ss(0.73, 0.78, p) * (1 - ss(0.86, 0.9, p))`: full opacity from p=0.78 to p=0.86, roughly **300px of a 3,800px runway**. It began fading while a reader was still reaching for it, which is a landing page failing at the one thing it exists for. **The comment directly above it claimed the hold was 0.70-0.86 and the code did not agree**, which is how the window got short without anyone noticing — the same defect class as a column written by one part of the system and read by none, in prose instead of SQL. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | **Measured, not estimated.** A Playwright rig samples the button's computed opacity across the runway: the hold was 302px and is now **842px, 2.8x longer** — `content` widened to `ss(0.71, 0.76) * (1 - ss(0.945, 0.975))` and the runway lengthened 520vh → 620vh. Every part of the reverse (`back`, `expand`, `pull`, and the two un-draw tails) moved back with it, because a hold the mark expands away through is not a hold. Rendered at both ends of the window to confirm the composition is identical rather than merely non-zero. Evidence: `tests/test_landing_downloads.py` +3, **mutation-checked** — restoring the old window fails 2, letting `expand` creep back under the hold fails 1, and drifting the comment from the code fails 1. The floor is set below the shipped value on purpose: it pins the property, not the design, so the finale can be retimed but not quietly halved. **Separately, and not a defect:** the page falls back to `tl-static` when WebGL2 is unavailable or `prefers-reduced-motion` is set, which collapses the runway from 4,680px to 1,384px and reads as *the animation is gone*. Confirmed by driving both paths headlessly. Worth knowing because it looks like breakage and is the accessibility fallback working. |
| D054 | **The landing page's animation ran slower on slower machines — not fewer frames of the same motion, but genuinely reduced motion.** The render loop advanced its clock with `this.t += 0.016 * speed`: sixty frames a second written as a constant. Any machine that could not sustain 60fps ran the animation clock slow in exact proportion, so the black hole really did spin slower and the page really did look more static. Reported as *"somehow the frontend degraded, it is not smooth, the motion is reduced and it appears static"* — three descriptions of one bug. The same fixed-per-frame assumption was in the pointer easing and the rebirth decay. **Invisible to whoever writes it**, because it only appears on hardware weaker than theirs — the same shape as `bootstrap.sh` installing four of nine packages and passing on the machine it was written on. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | **Measured before and after, same page, same viewport, in a browser.** At ~50fps the old clock advanced at **83% of real time**; the new one at **100%**. At 30fps the old one would have been 48%, at 20fps 32%. Fixed by advancing on real elapsed `dt`, clamped at 0.1s — generous on purpose, because the obvious 0.05 silently reimposes a 20fps ceiling on exactly the machines the fix is for, which was caught by measuring rather than by reading. Per-frame eases corrected with `1 - 0.94^(dt*60)`. **Also added: the shader now adapts its resolution to sustained frame time**, floor 0.4, climbing back when the machine can afford it — the honest lever for a ray-marched shader is pixels, not motion. The first version counted thirty consecutive slow frames, which at 2fps is fifteen seconds of stuttering before it reacts; counting frames to notice frames are slow is circular, so it is a timer now. Both faults found by instrumenting rather than by inspection. Evidence: `tests/test_landing_motion.py` — 6 tests, **mutation-checked**: restoring the 60fps constant fails 1, tightening the clamp fails 2, reverting to frame-counting fails 1, removing quality recovery fails 1, and disconnecting `renderGL` from the quality it computes fails 1. **`prefers-reduced-motion` and WebGL2 were ruled out first** — the reporter's console showed `static:false, reducedMotion:false, webgl2:true`, which is what redirected this from the accessibility fallback to the clock. |
| D055 | **The landing page called the product open source. It is not.** The hero carried an `Open Source` badge beside `Local First` and `Cross-Platform`. There is **no `LICENSE` file**, `gh repo view` reports `isPrivate: true`, and `README.md` never claims it — the badge existed only on the landing page, which is the one surface strangers actually read. This project's own named defect class in marketing clothes: a displayed claim nothing can support, exactly like the Contradictions meter in D011. **Also fixed in the same pass:** the beats were positioned at 62% with a headline tall enough to run up into the accretion disc, so the words were printed over the brightest part of the image and were hard to read. Lowered to 71% with a smaller headline, which puts both beats on sky. And the copy was rewritten to lead on what the product actually is — local-first — replacing metaphor with the README's own claims. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Badge removed; the row is now two badges, both plainly true. Copy grounded line by line in `README.md`: *"The database, the queue and the sandbox all run here"* against *"The database, the job queue and the scientific sandbox all run locally"*, and *"Connect something and it says so first"* against *"where one can be connected, the interface says so before it is used"*. Guarded by `test_the_page_does_not_claim_to_be_open_source_while_it_is_not`, **mutation-checked** by putting the badge back. The test is deliberately conditional on a `LICENSE` existing rather than absolute — it refuses the claim arriving before the thing it describes, not the claim itself. Layout verified by rendering both beats headlessly at 1440x900. **Caveat worth stating: the hero now reads *"Your research never leaves this machine"* without qualification.** It is true by default and the very next beat names the exception, but a reader who does not scroll sees an absolute. Left as chosen rather than softened unilaterally, and recorded here so it is a decision rather than an oversight. |
| D056 | **Cloudflare answers Python's default User-Agent with 403, which made the whole product undeliverable — and only the live host could show it.** `curl` of a release URL returns 200; `urllib.request.urlopen` of the *same URL* returns **403 Forbidden**. Any named agent returns 200. Nothing local reproduces it, because nothing local sits behind a bot filter, so every test in this repository passed while the product could not be installed by anybody. **Four call sites, covering every path:** `install.sh` could not fetch `install.py`, so the advertised one-liner died at its last step; `install.py` could not read `latest.json` or the tarball, so nothing installed; `runtimes.download` fetches the release tarball during an update, so applying one would have failed; and worst, `updates.check_releases` could not reach the manifest, so **every installed copy would have reported *could not check* forever** — not an error anybody reports, just silence where updates should be. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | **Found by running step 5 against the live host**, which is the only place it exists. Fixed by naming the product in the agent on all four callers — honestly, not by impersonating a browser, and there is a test refusing a `Mozilla/...` string. Verified against live Cloudflare, not a fixture: the fixed `install.py` fetched the manifest, downloaded the 19 MB tarball, verified the checksum and installed `b2dd184`; `updates.check_releases` verified the signature and answered *update available* to an older version and *up to date* to the current one. Evidence: `tests/test_release_requests.py` — 11 tests, **mutation-checked**: a bare `urlopen` fails 1, an impersonating agent fails 1, and an apostrophe in the embedded Python fails 2. **That last one is its own small lesson.** `install.sh` embeds Python inside a single-quoted shell string, so an apostrophe anywhere in it — including in the English comment *"Python's default agent"*, which is what happened — ends the string and breaks the file. Invisible on inspection, immediate when run. `sh -n` is now a test. |
| D057 | **Clicking Download printed the launcher into the browser instead of downloading it.** Cloudflare Pages serves `Throughline.bat` and `Throughline.command` with **no content type at all** and `throughline.sh` as `text/x-sh`, and no `Content-Disposition` on any of them — so the browser rendered each one as a page of text. Every download button on the site appeared to work and handed back a wall of script: a dead download wearing a working link, which is worse than a 404 because nothing looks wrong. **The page had been correct about this all along** — it links the launchers, the release publishes them, and `test_every_file_the_page_links_to_is_published` proved the file was there. What nothing checked was what the host *does* with a file once it is there, which is a different question from whether it exists. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | Measured with `curl -I` against the live host: no `Content-Disposition` on any of the seven published files. Fixed with a Cloudflare `_headers` file giving the three launchers `Content-Disposition: attachment`, and **`_publish_launchers` now ships it with every release** rather than it being an upload step — a checklist step is one somebody eventually skips. **`install.sh` is deliberately exempt** and pinned to `text/plain` instead: the page offers it as *Read install.sh first — trust, then pipe*, which is the one honest thing a page suggesting `curl | sh` can do, and forcing it to save would break exactly that. Evidence: `tests/test_landing_downloads.py` +4, **mutation-checked**: dropping a launcher's attachment rule fails 1, forcing `install.sh` to download fails 1, and removing `_headers` from the publish list fails 1. That last mutation **passed on the first attempt** — `published_names()` was matching every quoted path in the function, including the table of reasons beside the list, so a file could be dropped from the publish tuple while its explanation kept the test green. It now parses only the tuple. |
| D058 | **A `.command` file can never be made Gatekeeper-clean, so the macOS download is a dead end by construction.** Apple DTS, on the developer forums: *"Notarization does not apply to shell scripts — it is technically not possible to notarize a shell script."* `codesign` can attach a signature to a script, but it lives in an extended attribute, is described as brittle, and earns no notarization ticket. **Stapling only works on `.app`, `.pkg` or `.dmg`.** So no amount of paying Apple fixes `Throughline.command` — the artifact itself has to change. **Why it blocks now:** since Catalina, Gatekeeper checks any *quarantined* executable, scripts included, when it is launched from Finder. A browser download sets `com.apple.quarantine`; double-clicking then triggers the check. The same script run from Terminal is not checked at all — which is exactly why the one-liner works. **macOS 15 made it worse**, from Apple's own release note: *"users will no longer be able to Control-click to override Gatekeeper... They will need to visit System Settings > Privacy & Security."* The page's old claim that macOS *"asks once"* was written for a bypass that no longer exists. **A `.dmg` does not help** — quarantine propagates to the contents, and the signature of what is executed is what gets checked. Confirmed, and it was worth confirming because `.dmg` is the intuitive answer. | high | open | — | **The route that works today is the one-liner**, because `curl` does not set the quarantine attribute. That is corroborated by every security source but **never stated by Apple in writing**, so it is an implementation detail that could close in any release — it is a working route, not a durable one. **What proper macOS costs:** Apple Developer Program at **$99/year**, which an individual can join with no D-U-N-S number; a *Developer ID Installer* certificate; and a **`.pkg`** rather than a `.command` — the conventional artifact for a CLI/local-server tool, where `.app`-in-`.dmg` is the GUI convention. Then `pkgbuild --sign`, `xcrun notarytool submit --wait`, `xcrun stapler staple`. Automatable in CI with an App Store Connect API key. **Homebrew is not the free way round it any more:** Homebrew 5.0.0 deprecated unsigned casks and disables casks failing Gatekeeper in September 2026 — next month. A plain *formula* may still need only ad-hoc signing, which is the one avenue left worth checking before paying. **This is a product decision and not mine**: $99/year, or ship the one-liner as the macOS route and be honest on the page that the double-click download asks for a trip to System Settings. The page now says exactly that. **Update — a third option is confirmed and it is free: a Homebrew tap.** The September 2026 enforcement applies to **casks only**; Homebrew's own supply-chain doc says it *"applies macOS quarantine attributes to Cask downloads so Gatekeeper performs these checks"*, and there is no formula equivalent — quarantine handling lives in a `Cask::Quarantine` namespace with no counterpart. **A formula install is never quarantined, so nothing it puts on disk meets Gatekeeper at all.** No Developer ID, no notarisation, no fee. A tap needs no review and no notability — homebrew-core's *30 forks / 75 stars* bar is core's rule, not a tap's. The requirements are a **public** repository named `homebrew-throughline` holding `Formula/throughline.rb`, and nothing else; the recipe points at the tarball already published on the release host, so no source is exposed. A Python app installed into a virtualenv is the officially documented formula shape (`Language::Python::Virtualenv`), and a tap — unlike core — may fetch a prebuilt tarball rather than build from source. **`--no-quarantine` is not available to us and never was**: it was a cask-only flag typed by the *installing user*, not something a publisher sets, and it was deprecated in 5.0.0 and removed in 5.1.0 because Homebrew *"does not wish to easily provide circumvention to macOS security features"*. It is also moot, since formulae are not quarantined in the first place. `manage.py release` now generates `dist/throughline.rb` with this build's URL and digest, for the same reason `_headers` is published rather than remembered — a formula carrying last release's sha256 fails on the user's machine with a checksum mismatch. **Correction, same day: a tap is NOT recommended, and the case for it was overstated above.** Asked why a separate repository was needed, three things came back that change the answer. **(a) There is no way to avoid one.** `brew install <https URL to a .rb>` is hard-blocked — `Formulary::FromURILoader` sets `ALLOWED_URL_SCHEMES = %w[file]` and raises *"Non-checksummed download of formula file from an arbitrary URL is unsupported"*. And `Tap#install` runs a **full `git clone`** with no `--depth` and no sparse checkout, so a tap cannot be an existing large repo either; it must be a small dedicated one. **(b) Homebrew 6.0 (June 2026) added Tap Trust**: taps *"must be explicitly trusted before their code is evaluated or run"*, and auto-tapping of untrusted taps is gone. So the real user journey is `brew tap` → `brew trust` → `brew install` — **more ceremony than the one-liner, not less** — and Homebrew's own docs warn users against casually trusting third-party taps, which undercuts the familiarity argument that was the main reason to want it. **(c) It solves nothing about Gatekeeper.** The one-liner already installs on macOS with no prompt. What a tap actually adds is `brew upgrade` and `brew uninstall` semantics, against a recurring cost of a second repository and a `sha256` to keep in sync every release. **Better value for the same goal:** `manage.py` already has `update`, so self-update exists; it has **no `uninstall`**, and adding one would deliver the real half of what a tap offers to every platform at once, without Homebrew's trust machinery. Recorded rather than done. The formula generator is kept because it is written, tested and costs nothing — it makes a tap a short job if anybody ever asks for `brew install`. **Nobody has, so the repository is not being created.** |
| D061 | **The macOS download button was removed rather than explained.** It was the only route on the page whose first impression was a *"could not verify... free of malware"* dialog, and unlike every other fault fixed around it, that one cannot be repaired: a shell script can never be notarised (D058), so it was a permanent state and not a temporary one. Four steps to System Settings were written for it first; deleting the button was better than documenting the wall. **macOS is not left without a route** — the one-liner installs with no warning at all, because `curl` fetches it rather than the browser and nothing is quarantined. **Confirmed that the same line also opens it**: on a second run `install.sh` prints *already installed*, downloads nothing and hands straight to `manage.py start`, which is the launchers' own contract from T081. The PowerShell half behaves identically. So macOS gets one line that installs the first time and launches every time after, which is what the double-click was for. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `Throughline.command` is still published for anyone who wants it — it is simply not what a stranger is pointed at. The four Gatekeeper steps came back out again in the same pass, since nothing on the page now leads there, leaving the menu shorter than before any of this started. Evidence: `test_every_platform_has_a_route_on_the_page` replaces the old *one door per platform* assertion — it now requires a **route** rather than a file, asserts macOS is still named and the one-liner is still present, and **fails if the `.command` link returns**. Re-running the one-liner over an existing install was driven against the live host rather than reasoned about. |
| D062 | **The download menu was carrying text that belonged somewhere else.** Three rows and a three-line footnote, where the page's job is to get somebody installed. Cut in one pass: the *Read install.sh first* row went (**the URL is already in the copyable command below it**, and `_headers` pins the script to `text/plain`, so the affordance survives without a row restating a link the reader already has); the **macOS row** went with it, since macOS has no download and is named in the one-liner's own label; and the footnote lost everything the read me now carries. The menu is three rows shorter than it was this morning and says more. | low | done | Claude (Fenil) | **`README.txt` is the overflow** — published with every release, linked from the page as a small *read me*, and served as `text/plain` so a click reads it rather than saving it. It carries what the menu cannot: both one-liners, what the first run actually does and how long it takes, where the install and the database live, how updating verifies a signature, and `manage.py doctor`. **A terms-of-use draft was written first and then withdrawn** — asked for terms, what was actually wanted was instructions. Worth recording rather than quietly deleting: there is still **no LICENSE and no terms** in this repository, so the legal position of the software is unstated, and a line saying *by installing you accept our terms* would have pointed at a document that did not exist — the Open Source badge from D055 wearing a suit. If terms are ever wanted, they need a real jurisdiction and a real entity, neither of which can be invented. Evidence: `tests/test_landing_downloads.py` — `test_install_sh_can_still_be_read_before_it_is_piped` now asserts the *capability* rather than the row (the URL is on the page and the host serves it as text), `test_the_page_links_a_read_me_that_the_release_publishes`, `test_the_read_me_is_shown_rather_than_downloaded`, and `test_the_read_me_covers_both_one_liners`. |
| D063 | **A release install could not run at all on any machine that had Node — it started the stack and then shut itself down.** `manage.py start` asked `if node:` and ran `npm run dev` inside `apps/web`. A release ships the **exported** interface and no npm project, so npm exited immediately with `ENOENT ... apps/web/package.json`; the supervisor exits as soon as any child does — deliberately, because a dead worker beside a live API looks like a working stack that never finishes anything — so it took down the API and worker that had just started correctly. **The branch asked the wrong question.** Whether Node is installed on the machine says nothing about whether *this installation* has a project to build; most machines carry Node for some unrelated reason. The design was already right and already documented — `_ensure_interface` says an already-built interface needs no Node at all, *"which is the case a release should arrive in"* — and only this one branch disagreed with it. **Invisible everywhere it was tested.** On a source checkout `package.json` exists and the dev server is correct, so the defect lives only on the installation nobody develops on. Every earlier verification (Linux, Windows, macOS) stopped at *installed*, because the next step takes minutes — so three platforms confirmed the install and none confirmed the run. | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | **Found by the first person to let a release run to the end**, on macOS.
