Then **reproduced on Linux against the shipped `367bc72`** — identical `ENOENT` and teardown — and fixed on that same install, before and after in one sitting: after the change it prints *Served by the API itself — no Node process is involved*, `/` and `/docs` both return **200**, the page served is the real Next export, there are **zero** npm errors, and it is still running twenty seconds later. Fixed by branching on `_can_run_dev_server()` — the presence of `apps/web/package.json` — rather than on Node. Evidence: `tests/test_release_runs.py` — 4 tests, **mutation-checked** by restoring `if node:`. **Second defect, fixed as a consequence:** the *Hand tracking model not installed* hint told a release install to run `npm --prefix apps/web run vendor:hand-model`, which cannot work there for the same missing-`package.json` reason. It now prints only on a checkout. **That note was wrong and is corrected in D069:** hand tracking is *not* unavailable on a release install. It works, and the code said otherwise. |
| D064 | **A broken release cannot heal itself, and the read me had the data directory wrong.** Two things, both surfaced by the same attempt to recover from D063. **(a) The one-liner does not update.** Re-running `curl ... | sh` over an existing install prints *already installed*, downloads nothing and runs the copy that is there — correct behaviour for a launcher (T081: install or open, never update on its own), but it means somebody holding a broken build re-runs the advertised line and gets the identical failure, which reads as *the fix did not work*. Worse, D063's failure kills the interface, so **the update button in the UI is unreachable on exactly the install that needs it**. The only way out is `manage.py update` from a terminal, or deleting the program directory. **Now fixed.** Both installers compare the installed `VERSION` against the live manifest, say what they found, and hand over to `manage.py update` — T073's job, which knows how to back up, verify the signature and roll back. The installer only *notices*; it does not do the swap itself. A failed update is **not fatal**: the copy already present starts anyway with the reinstall command printed, because refusing to run on account of a newer version existing would be a worse failure than the one being fixed. Driven against the live manifest in both directions, update succeeding and update failing. **(b) `README.txt` said the database lives inside the program directory. It does not** — `db.py` resolves the data root to `~/.throughline-os` (note the dot) from `THROUGHLINE_HOME`, while the program is `~/throughline-os`. Getting that backwards tells a researcher either that reinstalling destroys their work, or that backing up the program directory preserves it. Both are false and only one of them is discovered safely. Written by me the same day, in the file published as the read me. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | The read me now names both directories, points out the dot, and says plainly that removing the program directory is safe. Guarded by `test_the_read_me_names_the_data_directory_correctly`, which asserts the claim **against `db.py` itself** rather than against a second copy of the path written into the test — so if the data root ever moves, the read me is what fails. |
| D065 | **When the stack died on startup it said nothing, so the person it happened to had no way in.** The supervisor exits as soon as any child does — correct, because a dead worker beside a live API looks like a working stack that never finishes anything — but it returned in **silence**. A child that failed at startup produced a wall of its own error output and then the stack tearing itself down with no explanation. In D063 that was raw `npm error ENOENT` lines followed by nothing: something started, something stopped, and there was nothing to act on. **A supervisor that knows why it is exiting and does not say so is throwing away the one piece of information nobody else has.** | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Each child is now labelled — *the API*, *the background worker*, *the web dev server* — and its death reported by name and status. A death **within twenty seconds** is treated as a startup failure rather than a crash under load and says so: that is almost always the environment rather than the code. It prints both `manage.py doctor` and `manage.py update` **in full, with absolute paths**, because by definition the reader is on an installation that does not work and cannot be assumed to know either command. Evidence: `tests/test_release_runs.py` +4, asserting the labels exist, that a startup death is still distinguished from a later one, and that both commands are named. Recorded beside D064's installer fix because they are the same problem from two ends — one lets a stranger get current, the other tells them why they need to. |
| D066 | **A busy port told a stranger to run a file that was not there, under a name they had never heard.** With port 8080 held by an earlier run that had not shut down, `start` refused — correctly, before touching a database or spawning a child — and then advised: *`PORT=8081 WEB_PORT=3001 ./scripts/dev.sh`*. **Three things wrong with that one line.** It is a **relative path**, and somebody who installed with the one-liner is standing in their home directory, where `./scripts/` does not exist. It names **`dev.sh`**, a developer script nobody who used the advertised install has ever seen. And it never says **how to stop the process it just named** — even though `port_owner` had the pid in its hand and threw it away, having gone to the trouble of looking it up precisely so the message would be actionable. The same defect as the hand-tracking hint in D063: advice a release install cannot follow, which reads as the product being broken rather than the port being busy. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `port_owner` now returns the pid alongside the description, and both `start` and `doctor` use it. A blocked port prints *Most often that is an earlier Throughline that did not shut down*, then `kill <pid>`, then a **fully absolute** `manage.py start --api-port N --web-port N` — runnable from anywhere, naming the same entry point the person already used. Evidence: `tests/test_startup_checks.py`, whose assertions were themselves pinning the bad advice — they asserted `WEB_PORT` and `PORT=` appeared, so the unfollowable form was *held in place by a test*. They now require the pid, an absolute `manage.py start`, and that `./scripts/dev.sh` is **absent**. Verified by driving `dev` against a genuinely held port and reading what it printed. |
| D067 | **An install cut halfway through left a tree that every later branch trusted.** A download interrupted after `scripts/manage.py` lands, but before the rest does, is taken by the *already installed* branch — so `update` runs out of a partial tree and `start` imports from one, and both fail in ways that read as the product being broken rather than the install having been stopped. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Both installers now judge completeness **positively**, against what the release builder actually writes: a `VERSION` file, the `packages/` tree bootstrap installs from, and the exported `apps/web/out/index.html`. Anything short of that is treated as unfinished and the install is redone. **Moved, not deleted — and that was the whole design question.** The suggestion was an automatic `rm -rf`. That is not something an installer may do: `$DEST` comes from `THROUGHLINE_INSTALL_DIR`, so one bad value removes a directory nobody asked it to touch, and the failure is unrecoverable at exactly the moment somebody is already having a bad time. Renaming to `<dest>.broken-<timestamp>` reaches the same outcome, costs a folder nobody has to keep, and can be undone by somebody who disagrees with the diagnosis. A second guard refuses to move `$HOME`, `/` or an empty path at all, so it only ever touches something it has identified. Driven against the live host: a tree with `manage.py` and nothing else was detected, moved to `/tmp/tl-half.broken-20260826-174128`, reinstalled clean to `0bd64d6`, **and the moved copy was still there afterwards**. The guard was driven too, with `THROUGHLINE_INSTALL_DIR=$HOME`, and refused by name. Evidence: `tests/test_release_runs.py` +3, including one that fails if `rm -rf "$DEST"` ever appears in `install.sh`. |
| D068 | **Windows double-click was the one door that skipped the installer, so it missed both of the day's recovery fixes.** Asked whether the work covered all three platforms, and the honest answer was no. `throughline.sh` and `Throughline.command` hand to `install.sh`; both one-liners are installers themselves; **`Throughline.bat` fetched `install.py` directly**. That worked — but `install.py` only installs. The *installer* is where *already here, so update it* (D064) and *half-installed, so redo it* (D067) live, so the Windows launcher reached neither. Two lists in two languages, in a third language. The audit that found it took one command and should have been run before claiming the fixes were platform-wide. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | `Throughline.bat` now hands to `install.ps1` — PowerShell rather than more batch, because it is always present and `irm | iex` runs from a string, so no execution-policy prompt applies. `install.ps1` gained `THROUGHLINE_NO_START`, set only by the launcher, so the installer can install without starting and the launcher keeps ownership of the window the app runs in. **Driven on real Windows**, where it installed `0bd64d6`, detected Python 3.14, fetched the pinned 3.12 and bootstrapped. That run also caught a defect introduced in the same change: a `|| echo "the release server could not be reached"` fires on **any** non-zero exit from PowerShell, so a port already in use during startup was reported as a network failure — sending somebody to check their connection over a busy port. Removed; the existing *is it installed now* check answers the only question the file can actually answer. Naming a cause you have not established is worse than naming none. Evidence: `tests/test_launchers.py` +2 — one asserting **every** door installs through an installer, mapping each launcher to the one it uses, and one refusing the invented diagnosis. `Throughline.bat` also left `test_release_requests.py`'s caller list, since it no longer makes HTTP requests; a new test pins that it delegates and fails if it starts fetching again, so the removal is a file leaving the list by not making requests rather than by going unchecked. |
| D069 | **Hand tracking was reported missing on every release install while it was working.** The release ships the model at `apps/web/out/mediapipe/hand_landmarker.task` — inside the exported interface, which is exactly what the API serves — and both checks looked only in `apps/web/public/mediapipe/`, the *source* location, which is not in the archive at all. So `doctor` said *not installed*, startup said *not installed*, and the browser could load it perfectly well the whole time. **A false negative rather than a false positive**, which is the rarer direction and no better: it is still a claim that does not match what the machine can do, and it sends somebody to fix something that is not broken. **I recorded the opposite of the truth in D063** — that hand tracking was unavailable on a release and nothing said so — having reasoned about it instead of looking in the tarball. Corrected there. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Both checks now look in `out/` as well as `public/`. Confirmed by listing the published archive: the model and its four WASM files are all under `out/mediapipe/`. **And the advice was wrong even when the model really is missing:** it said `npm --prefix apps/web run vendor:hand-model`, which needs an npm project a release does not have — the same unfollowable-advice defect as D063's hint and D066's port message. A checkout is now told the vendor command; a release is told to reinstall, because a release should have shipped it. Evidence: `tests/test_startup_checks.py` — one test asserting the model is found where a release keeps it, and one asserting each kind of installation is given advice it can actually carry out. |
| D070 | **Thirty-seven server routes cannot be reached from the interface, and the suite was green on every one of them.** This project's named recurring defect — finished, tested, unreachable — measured for the first time rather than noticed one instance at a time: of 115 `/api` routes, 80 are called by `apps/web` and 35 are called by nothing a person can operate. Whole subsystems are inside that number. **The visuals subsystem has four routes and no page at all.** Workflow nodes wait for an approval nothing can give, which is the step this product is built around. Graph analytics have five routes and no interface. The board can add a card and bring one to the front but has no way to delete one, and `DELETE .../board/{id}` is the half nobody calls. **A backend test is not a client, and that is why this stayed invisible**: `test_every_route_answers.py` proves a route does not fall over when called, which says nothing about whether anyone can call it — so the more thoroughly an unreachable route is tested, the healthier it looks. | high | recorded | Claude (Sarthak) | `tests/test_routes_are_reachable.py`, 48 tests. Routes are discovered from the app and callers scanned from interface *sources only*; every one of the 37 now carries a written reason, so a new route with no client and no reason fails. The ledger is checked in both directions — an entry for a route that has since gained a client fails too, which is the direction these lists normally rot in. **Mutation-checked, 9 of 9 killed, and the first pass killed only 4**: the orphan, stale and deleted checks could each be deleted outright with the suite still green, because against a healthy system all three find nothing. Fixed by making the comparison a pure function and testing it against a fabricated broken system. The rule that every entry gives a reason had the same defect and the same fix. |
| D071 | **LAW 4 was recorded as enforced while being enforceable by nothing.** The approval gate — "an irreversible step is released by a visible human decision" — has been in the engine since the first migration, and no part of the system could produce one or give one. Nothing outside `workflow.py` ever called `start_node`, no `enqueue()` call in the codebase passed `nodes=`, so **no run has ever had a single node**, and the approve route needed a run id that nothing handed out. `docs/PHASE_0.md` named the law satisfied by `start_node` throughout. The same absence made two further claims false: `workflow.py`'s "a restart resumes after the last completed node" and `runner.py`'s "no work is abandoned mid-node" described machinery no run used. **And `approve_node` was unguarded** — it approved any node of the run by name, so approving a step that had already completed reset it to `queued` and un-finished work that was done, while approving a name no node had matched nothing and answered as though it had. Both were unreachable only because nothing could call it. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by D070's reachability guard, which is what it was built for. `workflow.gate()` stops a run and **requires a description of what it is asking to release** — an approval screen saying "approve step 3" is a rubber stamp, so the sentence is the control and a gate without one is refused. `workflow.once()` makes a gate affordable: a resumed handler re-enters from the top, so without it every sandboxed analysis before the gate is paid for twice. The runner catches `AwaitingApproval` **inside** the transaction — letting it escape rolls back both the completed steps and the record that the run is waiting, so it would retry forever and never appear on the screen. `GET /api/projects/{id}/workflows/awaiting-approval` is new; without it an approval could only be given by someone who already knew the run id. Applied to discovery, where Rule 10 ("AI does not secretly mutate important research state") bites: the sweep tests every pair, then waits before recording connections and promoting survivors. **Asked for per run, not imposed** — gating every sweep would stop the seeded worked example on a fresh install, and whether an unattended sweep may record is the operator's decision. +34 tests (24 backend, 11 web, one for the inline-subroutine call the worked example alone caught). Mutation-checked 24 of 24 after two survivors were fixed: `pytest.raises(WorkflowError)` passed either way because `AwaitingApproval` subclasses it, and a `Math.max(0, …)` guarding clock skew was dead code, since every negative age is already below the first threshold. |
| D072 | **The Figures screen exported a figure by cloning the live `<svg>` out of the page, and the entire server-side visuals subsystem had no caller at all.** Four routes, a critic, a publication renderer and a lineage edge — none of it reachable. A file came out either way, which is why nobody noticed what the DOM route skips. **LAW 5 was the casualty**: `create_visual` writes a `VISUALIZES` edge from the analysis run to the figure, which is what makes a figure on a slide resolve back to the computation and the dataset underneath — a file assembled in the browser is related to nothing. **The critic was skipped too**: a figure with an unfixed blocking problem must not be published, and cloning the DOM asks nobody, so a truncated axis or a caption claiming causation left through the side door. Also lost: PDF, EPS and TIFF at a stated pixel height, which is what journals ask for, and a filename carrying the figure id rather than `consumption-resistance.svg`, which nobody can trace back a month later. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by D070's reachability guard. The on-screen chart stays as it is — it is good for reading — and *export* now goes through the server: `components/publish.tsx` creates the figure, shows what the critic said, refuses to offer a download for a figure the critic blocked (the server returns 409, so the button would only ever fail), and asks for the format warning **before** fetching the file rather than with it. A pixel height is offered only where one means something; the server refuses one for a vector rather than ignoring it. +17 tests: 12 web, and 5 over HTTP because **the domain was never the problem** — `test_visuals.py` covered the critic and the renderers thoroughly while the four routes themselves had only 404 and 401 cases, so the seam the interface uses was the one nothing exercised. The new ones assert the PDF really starts `%PDF`, the PNG carries its magic bytes and `-600px` in the filename, and the `visualizes` edge exists in `artifact_lineage_edges`. Mutation-checked, 7 of 7 killed. |
| D073 | **The reachability guard was itself blind to nineteen routes, and so was the guard that checks every route answers.** `app.include_router()` does not flatten: this FastAPI version stores the included router as a single `_IncludedRouter` in `app.routes`, with the real routes behind its `original_router`. That object has no `.path`, so both guards — which discover routes by walking `app.routes` and reading `.path` — skipped every route in `interpretation.py`: contradictions, preregistration deviations, the exploration ledger, harvest, exports, library notes, challenges and analysis lineage. **D070's count of 115 routes was wrong; it is 134.** The consequences ran both ways: the audit reported thirteen paths the interface calls as calling nothing, exempted an entire feature area, and `test_every_route_answers.py` never asked those nineteen whether they answer. **Its own guard against silent emptiness passed the whole time** — it asserts the route count is *large*, and sixty is large whether or not nineteen are missing. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found while picking the next thing to build: the interface appeared to call `/projects/{id}/analyses/{id}/lineage`, which no route seemed to serve. It does serve it — 401, not 404. Discovery moved to `tests/apiroutes.py`, which descends into included routers, and both guards use it. Vacuity checks now assert the nested routes are *present* rather than that the count is big. Re-auditing surfaced 5 genuinely unreachable routes nothing had ever seen, and the newly-covered GET routes were all found to answer (83 cases, up from 72). Mutation-checked, 2 of 2 killed. |
| D074 | **Nothing could register a hypothesis, so every result in the system was exploratory by construction.** `POST /projects/{id}/preregistrations` had no caller anywhere, while the interface *did* read `/deviations` and `/deviations/narrative` — a screen reporting departures from registrations that could never be created, whose empty state read "Nothing registered in this project" and was permanent. This is not a cosmetic gap: a pre-registration is what earns a result its exemption from multiple-comparison correction, and the exploration ledger corrects every look at the data together precisely because the one legitimate exemption is a hypothesis registered before anybody looked. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by D073's corrected audit. `components/preregister.tsx`, on the deviations screen and in its empty state. **The predicted direction is required by the form because it is required by the domain** — a prediction with no direction cannot be wrong, and exempting a description would turn the mechanism into a way of laundering exploratory work. The form distinguishes an *unstated* plan from an empty one, because the domain does: `covariates: null` cannot be deviated from, `[]` can. It warns while the form is open that a registration with no method, design or covariates cannot be checked against the analysis that runs — "not comparable" is not "matched" — and it reports the server's own note about what the registration bought rather than restating it. +11 web tests, 8 of 8 mutations killed. |
| D075 | **A sensitivity analysis could be displayed but not performed.** `components/forklineage.tsx` showed where a run came from and what was tried from it, opening with the line that a sensitivity analysis is the relationship between runs rather than any one of them — and that relationship had no screen. `POST /analyses/{id}/fork` had no caller, so the history could not be added to; `GET /projects/{id}/analyses/compare` had no caller, so branches could never be put side by side. **`compare_runs` already answers the only question a set of branches raises** — *"branches disagree on significance, the conclusion depends on an analytical choice and should be reported as such"* — and no researcher could reach that sentence. The panel also returned `null` for a run with no branches, which is right for a description and wrong for an action: it hid the button that creates the first branch exactly where a researcher needs it first. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `components/sensitivity.tsx`. **A fork with nothing changed is a re-run, not a sensitivity test** — it gives an identical result and a comparison of two identical runs reports a stable conclusion, which is flattery rather than evidence, so the form says so. The change offered is the rank-based counterpart (pearson↔spearman, t_test↔mann_whitney, anova↔kruskal_wallis): a fork copies the parent's variables, so only a method taking the same roles can replace another, and role compatibility is necessary but not sufficient — `chi_square` also takes an x and a y and is a test for categorical data, so it is not offered. +21 tests. The HTTP seam had no coverage at all: the domain test built a fork by calling `create_spec` and `create_run` directly, which is how the domain was proved and is not how anybody uses it. **Two things were learned by trying**: fork answers 202, not 200; and a completed run is immutable at the database level — a trigger refuses the update with *"fork it instead of editing it"* — so the disagreement case could not be fabricated. It is now produced by data instead: one outlier gives Pearson p≈8e-15 and Spearman p≈0.55, which is a better test than the one that was refused. Mutation-checked, 10 of 10 killed. |
| D076 | **Nothing could move a finding along its lifecycle, and nothing could challenge one.** `POST /findings/{id}/transition` had no caller, so every finding stayed at whatever state it was created in for ever — while the findings screen opened by saying "a finding must link to supporting and contradicting evidence before it can be promoted past candidate", describing a rule nothing could exercise, and `docs/PHASE_0.md` recorded LAW 3 as enforced by `findings.transition`. It was, and nobody could reach it. **This is LAW 4 again one law along.** `POST /findings/{id}/challenge` had no caller either, so the Challenges panel's empty state — *"No critic has run against it yet. That is not the same as it having survived one."* — was permanently true and unchangeable: the screen displayed an adversarial process nobody could start. `GET /findings/{id}` was unreached too, which is why neither the evidence counts nor the lifecycle history were on screen. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `components/lifecycle.tsx`. **The three states of a robustness check are the design.** The domain distinguishes a check that was never run from one that ran and failed, and refuses validation differently for each — *"cannot validate without these checks"* against *"these checks did not pass"*. A checkbox has two states and would collapse that, defaulting to passed or reporting not-run as failed, so each check is asked as three options and unanswered ones are not sent at all. The screen offers only legal transitions rather than six buttons of which four return 422, which means it holds a copy of `FINDING_PROMOTION` — `tests/test_findings_lifecycle_ui.py` parses the TypeScript and fails when the copies diverge, since that drift is silent in both directions. **Unlike the last three, the backend seam here was already well covered** (`test_api.py`, `test_mvp_end_to_end.py`), so nothing was duplicated. +18 web tests, +9 backend, 9 of 9 mutations killed. The drift guard's own vacuity test earned its place immediately: the parser read `string[]` out of `Record<string, string[]>` and returned an empty map, which is a subset of everything and would have passed every comparison. |
| D077 | **Variable harmonisation was a chain dead at both ends, so every chart in this system is titled `resistance_pct`.** Six routes; the interface called one. It read `/projects/{id}/variables` — approved labels only — and nothing could propose a label or approve one, so the approved set was empty in every project and stayed that way. The figures screen asks for those labels to title its axes (`variables.data?.labels ?? {}`) and always got none. The vocabulary half was worse: a whole subsystem for what a project learns to call things, with a pending queue nothing could add to or decide. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `components/variables.tsx`, a new Variables section beside Sources. Two queues because they are two decisions: a **label** says what a column of this dataset means, an **alias** says a phrase refers to a variable this project already knows — and approving an alias silently resolves that term in everything afterwards, so the panel reports how often it has been used, letting "it improves as you use it" be checked rather than believed. The two decide routes have different shapes (`{approve: bool}` against `{status: str}`) and the tests hold them apart. Review order is the server's — least confident first, which is where a person is actually needed — and a test fails if the screen resorts it. Proposing needs a model and legitimately 503s without one; the server's reason is shown, because "no model provider is configured" is actionable and "could not propose labels" is not. +15 web tests, 10 of 10 mutations killed — **after five of them silently failed to apply**: the shell mangled escaped quotes in the patterns, the substitution never happened, and the green results meant nothing until they were re-run from a file. |
| D078 | **Closed. The reachability audit compared paths, not methods, and eleven routes were hiding behind that.** Callers were found by scanning the interface for `/api/...` strings, and a string carries no verb — so a path whose GET was called and whose POST was not counted as fully reached. The audit now compares `"METHOD /api/path"`. **Most of what it found is one shape: the writing half of a screen that can only read.** `librarynote.tsx` displays a library note and posts nothing, so a note cannot be written; the consistency screen reads the latest sweep and nothing starts one; the board can add a card and not delete one. The mirror image exists too — the compare screen posts a pair and shows the answer while nothing reads a stored assessment back, and the same for locating claims and extracting from a source. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Verbs are read from the call itself (`useApi`, `api.<verb>`, `fetch` with its `method` option), which attributes all but three real call sites. **The three matter, and the design turns on them**: `views.tsx` builds its search path in a `const` with a ternary and passes the variable, so no verb can be read there — guessing one would invent an orphan out of a scan that could not read the call. Those paths are `unresolved` and treated as reached by any method, which is exactly what this file did for every path before, so the change is strictly more detection and no new false positives. Bases built as `const base = \`/api/…\`` are expanded, which is what found the **first wrong ledger entry**: it claimed no journal view existed while `NodeJournal.tsx` both reads and writes the per-object journal through a computed base. Ledger rewritten to 31 method-qualified entries. Mutation-checked, 8 of 8 killed, including the two that matter most — collapsing every route to GET, and collapsing every call to GET. |
| D079 | **Three charts had hover wired to nothing, and the isosurface ignored half the command architecture.** `SetRegions` mounted a tooltip with `const hit = null` above it and `rows={[]}` inside: hovering dimmed the other combinations and reported nothing, on a chart whose entire content is how many items fall in each combination. **The highlight worked, which is what made it look finished**, and the unused `readable` import in both it and `Ribbon` was the evidence of what had been intended. `Ribbon` reported `id: cohort_a` — a raw identifier its own file calls the one thing a label must never be. `Isosurface3D` had `selectRegion` and `withinPolygon` returning `[]` whatever they were given, `focus` and `deselect` as empty functions, and a `select` that returned a target and recorded nothing — so a hand that could rotate it could pick nothing on it while every other chart in the same gallery answered. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Tooltips now report the intersection and its count, saying *none in common* for the empty combinations that are enumerated on purpose, since a bare `0` reads as a missing value rather than as the finding it is; `Ribbon` reports throughput, taking the larger of in and out so a middle node does not double itself. The isosurface selects, and a region keeps facets on the far side while a point does not — a click names one visible place, a lasso encloses an area. **The first fix was the same defect one layer down**: it added a `selected` state, wired every command to it, and painted nothing, so the command stopped being ignored and started being answered invisibly. Caught by mutation testing, not by review. +18 tests where there had been none. |
| D080 | **`insidePolygon` had six copies.** Every 3D chart carried its own point-in-polygon test — five byte-identical, the sixth the same formula rearranged. Nothing had diverged, which is the reason to move it rather than a reason not to: a lasso is one gesture in one command architecture, and the moment two charts answer it differently it reaches a researcher as a chart that feels wrong rather than as a failing test. It also had no test anywhere. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | One copy in `lib/charts/scene3d.ts`, used by all seven, with the first tests it has ever had — concave lassos, self-crossing ones, degenerate ones, and a point on a seam shared by two lassos belonging to exactly one of them. Lint is clean for the first time: the unused imports were the symptom, and the last warning was a stale dependency array in the isosurface's handle that would have had a lasso testing against the shell from before the level moved. |
| D081 | **The interface has been calling a route that was never registered.** `ResultCard` asks for `/api/analyses/{id}/plain-summary` whenever a connection is opened, and no such route existed. Every request fell through to the catch-all that serves the interface — 503 in development, and in a release an HTML page, which the client then parsed as JSON. **`throughline_domain.interpret`, the 194-line module that answers it, was imported by nothing at all**: not the API, not a worker, not a single test. So the plain-language reading beside every result — the whole answer to "a researcher who does not know statistics can still use this platform" — has never worked. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by asking which domain modules nothing imports, then noticing the web called a path the route table did not contain. **Every reachability check in this repository asked whether a route has a caller; none asked whether a caller has a route**, which is why this survived four rounds of exactly that audit. `test_the_interface_calls_nothing_the_server_does_not_serve` now closes that direction, and fails if the route is removed again. The route answers 409 rather than 404 for an unfinished run — the run exists and has no result yet, which is a different thing from one nobody has heard of — and 502 when the model writes a figure into prose that must not carry one. **That last check is the reason the summary is safe to show, and nothing had ever exercised it**: a paraphrase restating a number can round it or drift from the value printed beside it. +12 backend tests, 4 of 4 mutations killed, verified against the running server: 401 JSON where it was 503 HTML. |
| D082 | **Two other modules nothing imports, both correct.** `digitise` (337 lines) says so itself — *"the engine is here, the surface is not... recorded here because unreached code is otherwise indistinguishable from dead code"* — which is the right call and already stated. `preflight` (114 lines) is invoked by `scripts/serve.sh` as `python -m`, which an import-graph scan cannot see. | low | recorded | Claude (Sarthak) | Checked rather than assumed: the temptation with a list of unimported modules is to treat the list as the finding. One of the three was a real defect and two were the tool's blind spots. |
| D083 | **The consistency screen described a picker it did not have.** Its opening comment says the sweep comes first "and the manual picker is there for when they already have a suspicion" — there was no picker. `POST /projects/{id}/consistency`, which answers whether two *named* results agree and what would explain it if they do not, had no caller anywhere, so only the automatic pairing was reachable: a researcher who already knew which two results bothered them could not ask about them. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The picker, and the two verdicts now render through **one** component. The sweep had its own copy of that markup; two copies drift, and then the same disagreement reads differently depending on which question surfaced it, which is the one thing a verdict must not do. A result is trivially consistent with itself and the domain refuses that comparison, so the chosen result is left out of the other list — impossible to ask rather than reported back after asking. +7 tests where the screen had none, 6 of 6 mutations killed. |
| D084 | **An untyped test fixture rendered the whole screen blank and reported only a missing element.** The verdict fixture omitted `still_possible`; `VerdictCard` reads `.length` on it, so React unmounted the tree and seven tests failed with *unable to find role combobox* — a message pointing at the picker, which was not the problem. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Fixture typed as `VerdictBody`, so the compiler holds it to the contract. **The component was not changed**: every one of those fields is a dataclass list with a default server-side, so the arrays are always sent, and guarding them with `?? []` would be defending against a contract that holds while making the real failure quieter. Worth recording because the diagnosis cost more than the fix: an empty render reports itself as whatever the first query happened to look for. |
| D085 | **The workboard could only accumulate.** §4 calls it "the central operating surface of the product": a card could be added and brought to the front, and never taken off. `DELETE /projects/{id}/board/{object_id}` had no caller, and that route is careful — it distinguishes *removed* from *was never there*, because "it is off the board now" and "it was never on it" are different answers to somebody who believes they just removed something. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | **Removal with an immediate undo rather than a confirmation**, which is what §96 asks for and what this case deserves: the object is untouched — a position goes, not a piece of research — and the same PUT that placed it puts it back where it was. A modal would ask a researcher to confirm something that costs one press to reverse. The card leaves the screen before the server answers, because a card still sitting under a press that plainly did something reads as a board that ignores you; a refusal puts it back and says so. The control stops the pointer reaching the surface, which would otherwise read the press as the start of a drag on a card that is going away — **and it is the first thing on this board a keyboard can reach**, since the cards themselves are pointer-only, so it is shown on focus as well as on hover. +6 tests, 6 of 6 mutations killed. |
| D086 | **A test I wrote last commit was order-dependent and passed alone.** The consistency screen makes two requests — the sweep, and the list of results the picker offers — and the picker does not exist until the second lands. One test reached for the selects straight after the sweep text arrived, which held when that file ran by itself and failed inside the full suite, where the second request had not resolved. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Waited for rather than assumed. Confirmed by running the pair three times over. Recorded because the failure named the picker — *unable to find role combobox* — which is the second time in two commits that a timing problem on this screen has reported itself as a missing element. |
| D087 | **A card could be arranged and never looked into.** `Board.tsx` recorded the reason: *"there is nowhere in this workspace that shows a research object on its own yet, and a callback the host cannot satisfy is dead surface"* — correct at the time, and it left the product's central operating surface as a place for moving things around. Two routes had been waiting for that view with no caller: `/objects/{id}/impact` and `/objects/{id}/mentions`. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | A panel beside the board — beside rather than over, because covering the arrangement hides the reason somebody is on this screen. A press still raises (cards overlap; the one pressed is the one meant) and now also selects, which is what raising always implied. **Impact is shown forwards, not as a deletion.** That route's own summary is "what a deletion would destroy, before it is destroyed" and nothing in this product deletes a research object, so borrowing the framing would describe an operation that does not exist; it is put as what has been built on this, which is what matters before revising something. Findings resting on it as evidence are counted separately from artifacts derived from it — an artifact can be remade, and a finding that loses its evidence is a claim with nothing left under it. +18 tests, 7 of 7 mutations killed. An existing test's comment asserted "a press raises rather than opens" and was corrected rather than left contradicting the behaviour. |
| D088 | **A defensive line that turned out to be dead, and the one case where it is not.** Clearing the opened card on removal looked redundant: the panel is derived from the cards on the board, so removing the card closes it with no code at all, and deleting the line changed nothing the tests could see. It matters on **undo** — putting the card back would otherwise resurrect a panel the researcher had already dismissed by taking the card off. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by mutation testing, which is the only thing that would have found it: the line reads as obviously necessary. Kept, with the undo path tested, rather than deleted as dead or left as an untested guess. |
| D089 | **Twelve routes never checked who owned the project, and one account could read another's research.** Confirmed against a running app with a second signed-in account, not inferred: `GET /projects/{id}/board`, `/board/available`, `/artifacts`, `/citations/verify` and `/excerpts` all answered **200** with the other account's contents — the whole board, every unplaced object, every report, every excerpt taken from a paper. `POST /artifacts/{id}/presentation` answered **201**: somebody else's report re-cut as a talk, with the slides created inside their project. **The domain looked like it was guarding that one.** `draft_presentation_from_report` refuses a report belonging to a different project — and the route handed it the report's *own* project_id, so the comparison was against itself and could never fail. A check that cannot fail reads exactly like a check. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found while evaluating the presentation route as a feature to build: every neighbouring handler called `scoped_project` and that one did not. A static scan for handlers that take a `project_id` and never scope it returned sixteen; **testing them from a second account is what separated the ten real ones from the six that were fine**, and it also showed which of the failures were leaks (200) and which were only invalid ids (400/422) — those last had to be re-probed with bodies that validate, because a 422 means the ownership check never ran. `tests/test_project_isolation.py` asks every project-taking route the same question and reads the answer as a status code, because a route can be scoped by a dependency, a helper or the domain and only the answer settles it. Removing the twelve checks fails nine of its cases. |
| D090 | **A test that expected the weaker refusal.** `test_a_connection_from_another_project_is_refused` posted to a project that does not exist and asserted 400, which came from the domain noticing the connection belonged elsewhere. With the ownership check in front it is 404 — and its own comment argued for that: *"an identifier is not an authorisation"*. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Updated to 404, and split: the case it used to cover — a connection from another project the caller **does** own, where ownership is not the question — is now its own test, so the domain's refusal stays covered rather than being replaced by the earlier one. |
| D091 | **Five more routes leaked, keyed by an artifact, a source or an alias rather than a project.** The natural completion of D089, which only covered handlers taking a `project_id`: these carry an id that names a *row*, so the check has to look up which project the row belongs to before it can ask anything, and none of them did. Confirmed from a second account: `GET /artifacts/{id}` **200** — the whole report; `POST /artifacts/{id}/render` **200** — somebody else's report written to a file; `POST /artifacts/{id}/check-citations` **200**; `GET /sources/{id}/marks` **200** — everything drawn on their paper; `POST /vocabulary/{id}/decide` **200** — a term approved into their vocabulary, which from then on silently resolves in every paper of that project. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | One helper, `_owning_project`, since all five need the same lookup and answering 404 for a row you do not own is the same rule as `scoped_project`. Each leak proved before the fix and re-proved after by removing the check. |
| D092 | **A guard so this class cannot come back, and the reason it cannot be a scan alone.** `tests/test_routes_are_scoped.py` requires every route to establish ownership by one of the known means or to be written down with what it reaches instead — 32 entries, all of them auth, health, system settings or external search. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | **The entry that makes the point is `DELETE /api/projects/{id}`**, the most destructive route in the product. It calls none of the scoping helpers and is correct: it filters on `owner_user_id` in its own query and finds nothing for anybody else. A scan alone would have called it a hole; the behavioural test in `test_project_isolation.py` proves it is not. That is why the two files exist together — one asks what the code says, the other asks what the server answers, and only the second settles it. `POST /speech/transcribe` is recorded as the one route with no session at all, a documented choice made on the grounds that the API binds to localhost, flagged for re-reading if that ever changes — the product does support more than one account. Mutation-checked, 4 of 4 killed after two survived: the unexplained and stale checks passed because both lists are empty against a healthy API, the same hole the reachability guard had, fixed the same way. |
| D093 | **A finished report could not become slides.** `POST /artifacts/{id}/presentation` had no caller, so a researcher with a report had no way to get a talk out of it except by writing one somewhere else — which is the thing the route exists to prevent. §75: a presentation is the same evidence at a different length, derived from the report so both reference the same analysis runs; re-running an analysis then moves both, rather than leaving two accounts of one result that drift apart. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | On the report screen, and **it opens the talk rather than only making it** — creating a second document and leaving the reader on the first is how a button comes to look broken: the work happened and nothing on screen changed. `onOpenArtifact` is required rather than optional, because an optional callback nobody supplies is the dead surface this codebase keeps producing. Not offered on a talk, since a presentation re-cut from a presentation is a copy. +7 tests where the screen had none, 5 of 5 mutations killed — one only after being retargeted, because `setFailure(err)` appears twice in that file and the first occurrence is a different function. |
| D094 | **A second untyped fixture, and the same two hours of the wrong diagnosis.** The report fixture carried `problems` and no `warnings`; the integrity panel iterates both, so the screen rendered nothing and all seven tests reported that they could not find the button — which was never the problem. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Typed as `Artifact`. Recorded because it is the second time in three commits: an object literal standing in for an API response is a second copy of the contract, and only the compiler keeps the two together. The tell is uniform — every test in the file failing on a *missing element* rather than a wrong value means the render died, not the assertion. |
| D095 | **Four tests were passing over a render that had crashed.** React catches an error thrown during render, unmounts the tree and logs it — and an empty DOM satisfies every `queryBy…` expecting nothing, every `not.toContain`, and every assertion about a call made before the crash. Two board tests and two notebook tests were green over components that had fallen over. **This had already cost four debugging sessions in this repository**, each starting in the wrong place, because a dead render reports itself as whatever the first query asked for: *unable to find role combobox* when the picker was fine, *unable to find the button* when the button was fine. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by instrumenting `console.error` across the whole suite and listing every **passing** test that had logged a render-time TypeError. `tests/setup.ts` now turns that into a failure, with an opt-out list for tests that mean to provoke one — verified against a component written to throw, which passes its own assertion and is failed by the guard. The two board tests broke because pressing a card now opens a panel that fetches two more things; the two notebook tests because creating a note opens it, and the mock answered `{}` for the note. **The second one was hiding behaviour worth asserting** — that a created note is opened rather than left for the researcher to find — which could not have been tested while the step that does it was throwing. |
| D096 | **The cause behind all four: an object literal standing in for an API response is a second copy of the contract.** `problems` where the type says `warnings`; a note with no `body`; an impact payload answered with the board's. Nothing keeps the copy and the contract together unless the fixture is typed. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The fixtures in the files touched here are typed now, and the render guard catches the rest wherever they are — which is the better half of the fix, since it needs nobody to remember. |
| D097 | **The project's journal could only be read one object at a time.** `GET /projects/{id}/journal` — the stream of everything written, newest first — had no view. `NodeJournal` shows the notes attached to one object, so a researcher returning after a fortnight could check objects one by one and had to remember which ones to check. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | A Journal section beside the Notebook, **reusing `NodeJournal`'s marks and class names rather than inventing a second appearance for the same rows**: they are one table, and two screens styling it differently would teach a reader that the human/model distinction is decorative. Grouped by day, since that is how somebody looks for a thought they half remember. A note attached to nothing says *about the project* rather than leaving the line blank, which reads as a missing value. +14 tests, 6 of 6 mutations killed — the last only after asserting the ◇/◆ mark itself, since `data-kind` is invisible and the mark is what a reader sees before they read anything. |
| D098 | **A test that would have passed or failed depending on the machine's timezone.** The journal groups by the reader's own day, and the fixtures were written as UTC strings: `2026-08-27T22:00Z` is the 27th in London and the 28th in India, so "two days" was one day here and two in CI. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Timestamps are built from local components now, so the fixture means the same thing wherever it runs. Caught on the first run rather than by luck, because the failure was a count that did not match — but it would have been a *green* suite on a UTC machine and a red one on this laptop, which is the worse way round to find it. |
| D099 | **The synthesis screen built the table and stopped.** `POST /projects/{id}/synthesis/key-points` had no caller, so the reading across a set of papers — how many state a design at all, which pairs cannot be compared, where the papers are not independent evidence — was left to the researcher to work out from the matrix. That reading is the part the route exists to do, and it does it **without writing a sentence about the findings**: its own summary is that *"a generated synthesis of five papers is precisely the artifact nobody can check"*, so every point is a count over verified quotations. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Above the table, following this codebase's habit of the reading first and the evidence under it — the matrix is what the counts are counted from. **Two requests, and the second failing must not take the first away**: a researcher who has the comparison keeps it, and is told the reading could not be worked out rather than left with a blank space above a full table, which reads as "these papers had nothing in common". The same care for a genuinely empty result: it says nothing stands out, rather than rendering an empty section under a heading. +6 tests where the screen had none, 5 of 5 mutations killed. |
| D100 | **Figures were saved and then unreachable.** Exporting one creates a record — the spec, the critic's report, and a lineage edge back to the analysis it draws — and nothing listed those records or opened one. The interface downloaded the file and moved on, so a saved figure could be reached only by somebody who had kept its id. `GET /visuals/{id}` and `PATCH /visuals/{id}` both had no caller, **and `visuals` has been indexed on `(project_id, created_at DESC)` since the table was written — for a query nobody had made.** | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Added the missing listing route and a saved-figures list under the live chart, since the list is a record of what has been made rather than the thing a researcher came to that screen to do. **The edit is deliberately narrow**: a title and a caption are how a figure reads, its rows and variables are what it claims, and the domain refuses the second — *"a redraw would leave the old statistics on new data"*. Editing re-runs the critic and reports what it blocked, which is the reason to edit here rather than in the exported file: a caption that overstates the result is exactly what the critic stops, and a caption fixed in a PNG is fixed nowhere. The editor stays open while the critic is still refusing, rather than closing over a change that did not take. +12 tests, 6 of 6 mutations killed. `GET /visuals/{id}` stays in the ledger with a corrected reason: the list carries what the list and its editor need, and nothing yet opens one figure on its own. |
| D101 | **Any unmatched URL answered 500 with an errno.** Found by checking the new listing route against the running server and getting `500 {"error": "PermissionError", "message": "[Errno 1] Operation not permitted"}` — then getting the identical answer from a path that has never existed, which is what showed the route was fine and the **catch-all** was not. `Path.is_file()` swallows the errors that mean *not there* — ENOENT, ENOTDIR — and lets everything else through, so a bundle directory the process cannot traverse raised out of `installed()`, a question that reads like a yes-or-no. A researcher who mistyped a URL got an errno. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | An interface the API cannot read is one it cannot serve, which is the same answer as one that is not installed — and that answer already existed and explains itself: 503, saying so. Guarded in both places, because they fail at different levels: `installed()` on the file, `resolve()` one directory up, where `is_dir()` raises only when the directory *containing* the bundle cannot be traversed. Reachable without a sandbox: a bundle owned by another user, a restrictive umask, a mounted volume with the wrong permissions. Mutation-checked, 3 of 3 killed — the third only after writing the test for the containing directory, since guarding the file left the second call to raise and a guard nothing exercises is a guess. |
| D102 | **The Reports list showed a status that could never say a document had drifted.** `status` and `stale_reason` are columns on `communication_artifacts`, both the reports table and the report header render that status, and **nothing had ever written it** — `POST /projects/{id}/exports/recheck` is the only route that does, and it had no caller. The exports screen was never wrong, which is what hid this: it recomputes on every read, so the one place that could prove drift was the one place that did not need the stored flag. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | A button on the exports screen that records the verdict onto the documents, **not a side effect of loading** — the route is a POST because it writes, and a read that quietly changed a document's status would make opening a report a modification of it. The line beside it says where the recorded verdict shows up, because without that the button looks like it did nothing: the screen it is pressed on already said the same thing, live. +3 tests, 4 of 4 mutations killed. |
| D103 | **The vocabulary queue could be answered and never added to.** `POST /projects/{id}/vocabulary` had no caller, so a project's vocabulary grew only from what the system proposed while reading papers — a researcher who knew their own field's word for something had no way to say so. The decide half was wired up in D077 and this half was not, which left a queue with one end open. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | **The blocker was that no id ever left the server**: an alias points at a canonical variable by id, and `labels()` and `equivalent_columns()` both project the name and drop it while `learned()` only counts them. `vocabulary.variables()` returns them, on the response the vocabulary screen already fetches rather than as a new route. The variable is **chosen, never typed** — a term attached to a variable this project does not have resolves in no paper and looks, from outside, exactly like one that works. A phrase somebody typed is recorded as `researcher` rather than `paper`, because the queue shows where a term came from and those are not the same claim. +6 tests, 5 of 5 mutations killed. |
| D104 | **A project accumulated compatibility verdicts that nobody could see.** Every assessment is stored — verdict, reasoning, what blocked it, what would need harmonising — and `GET /projects/{id}/compatibility` returned them to nobody. A researcher with six datasets asked the same question about the same pair as often as they happened to select it, and never saw that they had asked before. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | **Dated, and described as the answer at that time.** A version id is immutable so the datasets have not moved underneath it, but the verdict depends on harmonisation too, and approving a variable label since then can turn a blocking difference into a shared dimension — presenting an old verdict as current would be the screen asserting something nobody rechecked. The label now travels with the verdict from the server rather than being mapped again in the client: a second copy of that vocabulary is a second thing to keep in step, and the one place this repository did copy such a map needed a drift test to hold the two together. +8 tests, 5 of 5 mutations killed. |
| D105 | **Two of those tests passed against the mutations they were written for.** `waitFor` on an *absence* passes on its first evaluation — before the fetch resolves — so the empty-state test reported success against a screen that had not yet had the chance to render the thing it was checking was not there. And the fallback for a missing dataset was asserted on one side of the pair while the two sides are separate expressions. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Flushed rather than waited for, and both sides asserted. Worth recording as a rule: **`waitFor` proves a thing appeared; it proves nothing about a thing that has not appeared yet.** |
| D106 | **The synthesis screen told a researcher to press a button that was not there yet.** Above Compare it said *"Papers must be read first — use 'Read this paper' if one is missing"*, and that control only appears **after** a comparison has been built and reported the paper missing. So the instruction named something invisible at the moment it was given, and the only way to find an unread paper was to press Compare and be refused. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The chosen papers are now asked about before comparing, which is what `GET /sources/{id}/extract` exists for — *"what was already read out of this paper. Never runs a model"* — and it had no caller, so a previous reading could not be seen and a paper could be read twice at a model call each. Bounded by the **selection** rather than the project: asking about every paper on arrival would be a query nobody made. Reading one forgets the cached answer for it, or the screen would go on calling it unread. The hint now says what is true without pointing at a control. +5 tests, 4 of 4 mutations killed. |
| D107 | **Choosing a paper re-read it, every time.** The claim test posted to locate claims on every selection — a model call per click, and, the part that matters, a reading that can quietly replace the claims an earlier comparison rested on. `GET /sources/{id}/claims` exists for precisely this and had no caller; its own summary says why the two are separate routes: *"reading the record and re-reading the paper are different acts, and only one of them can change what every downstream comparison rests on."* | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The record is read first, and a re-read is a visible choice with its consequence beside it — **not that it costs money, but that anything already tested against those claims rested on that reading**. The recorded claims carry the model and prompt version that produced them, and the screen names them, because two readings of one paper can disagree and the difference has to be attributable rather than argued about. A paper nobody has read is still read on selection: there is nothing to show, and reading it is plainly what choosing it meant. +7 tests, 4 of 5 mutations killed — the fifth is the initial value of a flag that no path can observe, since every route to a rendered reading sets it first. |
| D108 | **Reconciling two papers re-read both of them, every time.** `reconcile_papers` called `locate_claims` per side, which always asks a model — two readings per comparison, and the same pair reconciled twice could come back different. That is not only cost: `stored_claims` says so itself — two readings of one paper can disagree, and when they do *"the disagreement has to be attributable rather than argued about"* — so the verdicts could change under a researcher with nothing on screen saying the inputs had. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | `claim_test.claims_for` reads the record and only reads the paper when there is none. **Comparing two papers is not a request to re-read them**; re-reading stays deliberate and stays where it belongs, as its own act on the claim-test screen. A recorded reading carries the model and prompt that produced *those* claims rather than the model configured today, and the response now says which sides had to be read. +4 tests, 4 of 4 mutations killed. |
| D109 | **`POST /projects/{id}/reconcile` is not a gap.** It reconciles two *claims*; the screen reconciles two papers, and `/reconcile-papers` already returns every pair of their claims — so the directed question is answered inside the broad one. | low | recorded | Claude (Sarthak) | The ledger reason said only that the interface calls the other route, which reads as an oversight. Building a control for it would recompute something the reader already has, so the entry now says why it stays unbuilt rather than implying somebody forgot. |
| D110 | **A test run killed part-way poisoned the next one.** Most tests sit inside a transaction that is rolled back, but the HTTP ones go through `TestClient` and commit real rows, tidying up in fixtures that only run when a session ends gracefully. So an interrupted run leaves accounts, projects and analyses behind, and the next run inherits them. **Not hypothetical:** an interruption in this session left rows that failed six tests in the file that happened to sign in as the same address, and every one of them pointed at the tests rather than at the wreckage — the usual shape of contaminated state. `conftest` already recovered from a *half-initialised cluster* with some care; nothing handled a database that was merely full. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Every session now truncates the data tables after migrating. **It refuses to run anywhere but the test database**: this clears every table there is, and pointed at a researcher's project directory that is not a cleanup but data loss — so it checks `THROUGHLINE_HOME` against the temp path set at import and raises rather than guessing. One `TRUNCATE ... CASCADE`, so foreign keys never see a half-emptied database, and `schema_migrations` is kept or every migration would re-run against a schema that already has them. +3 tests, 3 of 3 mutations killed — **and the third one proved the point by damaging the test database**: truncating the migration record left the schema in place but unrecorded, and the next run failed on a column that already existed. Repaired by discarding the temp directory, which is exactly what that safety check exists to make unthinkable anywhere else. |
| D111 | **The product did not agree with itself about how many comparison verbs it has.** `compare.tsx` said *"Part I has six verbs. Three counts of one thing, none of them right. | low | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Both corrected against what the screen actually renders: all six of Part I's verbs, plus two this product added — figures, and scan ↔ scan. The ordinary fate of a count kept in prose beside the thing it counts, which is why the comment now says so. |

| D113 | **An Ollama cloud model would have been chosen as though it ran here.** Ollama serves models with a `:cloud` tag that execute on ollama.com, and reports them from `/api/tags` beside the local ones. `installed()` listed them identically and the picker offered them identically, so selecting one looked exactly like selecting a local model — while every passage of every paper the system reads would leave the machine. **The README's guarantee is the whole point of the product**: *"nothing leaves the machine unless the researcher connects an external service — and where one can be connected, the interface says so before it is used."* The hosted provider already confirms before use, on the grounds that it is "the one action here that changes where data goes"; an Ollama cloud model changes it just as completely and asked nothing, **because the check was written against the provider rather than against where the model actually runs**. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found while connecting this installation to `glm-5.3-flash:cloud` — the request that would have exercised the gap. The tag is the signal rather than asking Ollama, because `/api/tags` does not say where a model runs and a wrong guess here only shows up as data already sent. +4 tests, 3 of 3 mutations killed, including the one that matters in the other direction: a false positive would put a warning on a model that never leaves the machine and teach a researcher to dismiss it. |
| D114 | **Ollama answering with an error was reported as Ollama being down.** `urllib.error.HTTPError` is a subclass of `URLError`, so a response carrying a status code was caught by the same branch as a dead socket and reported as *"Ollama is not reachable — start it with `ollama serve`"*. That is false whenever the server answered, and it sends a researcher to fix the one thing that is not wrong. | high | done | Claude (Sarthak) | Found by pointing this installation at `glm-5.3-flash:cloud`, which answered **402 Payment Required** — and was reported as a stopped server that had in fact been running throughout. §104's rule is the server's own words, never "something went wrong", and Ollama puts its reason in the body: the message now reads *"Ollama answered 402 for 'glm-5.3-flash:cloud': this model requires a subscription or extra usage, upgrade at ..."*, which is something a person can act on. A `:cloud` model adds that the account lives at ollama.com, because a 402 on a local model is a different problem. +5 tests, 4 of 4 mutations killed — **the last only after testing the routing rather than the wording**: every test called the message function directly, which proves nothing about which branch an HTTP error actually lands in, and that branch was the bug. |
| D115 | **A discovery sweep recorded its own denominator and showed none of it.** `discovery_runs` holds how many pairs were considered, how many were dropped and why, how many were tested, and the correction the survivors were judged against — and `GET /discoveries/{id}` returned it to nobody. The screen listed the connections that came out and said nothing about the search that produced them. **In this product that is not cosmetic**: a q-value means nothing without the number of tests it was corrected across, and the same p-value is worth less after forty comparisons than after four. A reader shown only the survivors cannot tell which they are looking at. | medium | done | Claude (Sarthak) | The run id came back from the POST and was being discarded; it is kept, and the sweep now accounts for itself under the results. A run still going says so instead of showing a half-written count, which would read as a search that tested very little. +11 tests, 5 of 5 mutations killed. |

| D117 | **The exploration ledger's correction family was identified by a browser tab, and the record it kept was unreachable the moment that tab closed.** `lib/session.ts` minted a UUID into `sessionStorage`. The half that was wrong is that a *tab* is not the alternative. It is invisible, so a researcher could not see what was being corrected together and could not tell us it was wrong; two windows split one afternoon into two families, each reporting its looks with more confidence than they had earned; and — the part that decided it — `exploration_tests` is a durable table, so closing the tab discarded only the *identifier* and left every past look in the database with nothing able to reach it. | D123 | **The command bar asserted `aria-modal="true"` on a `<div>` that trapped nothing.** `role="dialog"` and `aria-modal="true"` tell assistive technology that content outside the dialog is inert. | D128 | **The resizable panels stored their layout and never restored it.** `react-resizable-panels` reports layouts as **percentage shares** — `{"rail": 27.143, "workspace": 47.143, "inspector": 25.714}` for a 1400px window — and the validator was checking each value against the *pixel* bounds the panels are configured with (180–380 for the rail). Every layout the library actually wrote failed validation and was silently discarded, so dragging a divider stored a value and the next reload went back to the defaults. **The unit tests could not have caught it**: they built layouts with `writeLayout` in pixels and read them back, which round-trips perfectly while agreeing with nothing the library produces — the suite was green throughout. It took opening the page. | medium | fixed | — | Found while verifying T091, by reading `localStorage` after a keyboard resize and seeing `27.143` where the validator wanted 180–380. `sane()` now validates shape rather than size — every share a finite positive percentage, and the set summing to 100 — because the pixel limits are enforced by `minSize`/`maxSize` on the panels, which is the only place that knows the viewport. Wrapping rather than `overflow-x: auto`: a scrolling strip hides choices behind an affordance people miss, and these are the screen's verbs — the set should be readable at a glance. That passed a check comparing only the leading column and was caught by comparing all seven. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T096 by scrolling the *wrapper* and leaving the table a table, reached with `:has(> table)` since those wrappers carry no class. | D132 | **`brew audit --strict` advises deleting the formula's version line, and following that advice would ship releases under a nonsense version.** Homebrew reads a version out of the archive name when the formula does not state one. `version_name` is `git describe --tags --always`, so between releases the archive is `throughline-v1.0.0-3-gabc1234.tar.gz` — and Homebrew reads that as version **1234**, the trailing digits of the commit hash. `brew upgrade` would then compare the wrong numbers. Measured with `brew info` on exactly that URL with the line removed, not inferred. The audit's advice is correct for projects whose archives are named `name-1.2.3` and wrong here, which is what makes it dangerous: it looks authoritative and the formula still installs. | medium | fixed | — | The line stays, with a comment saying why and a test that fails if it is removed. | D133 | **Building the interface silently breaks a running dev server, including when a release does it.** `next build` writes its manifests through `.next` regardless of `NEXT_DIST_DIR`, and `next dev` keeps its working files there — so a build pulls them out from under a running server and every route it serves answers 500 with `ENOENT: .next/static/development/_buildManifest.js.tmp.*`. The build reports success; the process that breaks is a different one; and the recovery (`rm -rf apps/web/.next`, restart) is obvious only once you already know. Found the hard way — I ran `build-interface` to size the release tarball while the dev server was up, and the researcher hit the 500 on `/gesture-check`. **`release` inherits it**, because it builds the interface when no export exists, so this is a way of shipping that breaks your own machine. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T098.
