Noticed while running the ML-sweep experiment (T103), where the sweep had to pass `verb="discovery"` because the vocabulary is research-bound — which prompted looking at how that vocabulary is defined, and finding it defined twice. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T104. The lists did in fact agree; what was missing was anything holding them there. Mutation-tested in both directions, and the guard reads the live `pg_constraint` rather than the migrations, because `0040` rebuilt what `0026` created. |
| D143 | **Two Homebrew formulas, and the more careful-looking one was wrong about the product's own architecture.** An untracked `packaging/homebrew/Formula/throughline.rb` sat beside the one `release.py` generates, declaring `depends_on "postgresql@16"` and instructing users to start that service before first use. The product bundles PostgreSQL through `pgserver`; `db.py` states the principle in its opening lines — *"a desktop researcher must never be asked to install one."* So the formula asked for software the product never talks to, and for a step that does nothing. It also pointed at a GitHub archive rather than the release host, contradicting a passing test, and ran `bootstrap` inside `brew install`, which Homebrew sandboxes without network. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T105. Its one genuinely better idea — pinning `python@3.12` rather than trusting `/usr/bin/env python3` — is folded into the generator, and the rest is documented as deliberate refusals rather than omissions. The hand-written file is left in place, untracked, for its author to remove. |
| D144 | **A drafted report quoted a corrected q-value and never said what it was corrected against.** The report is otherwise exemplary — every number a `{{ref:…}}` resolved from the record at render time, "the sentence carries no digits at all" — and it carried `corrected q` from the connection. But nowhere did it say the family had 200 tests in it, or 4. `resolve_block`'s own docstring explains why that matters: the same run in a family of five and a family of five hundred yields different q. So the document put its most important number in front of a reviewer with no way to weigh it, which is the failure the ledger exists to prevent, one step further downstream than anyone had looked. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T106. Found by asking what a researcher actually hands over, rather than what the screens show — the rigour chain (preregister → ledger → deviations → narrative) turned out to be built and reachable, and it stopped at the edge of the artefact. |
| D145 | **Three catalogued globes were drawn as flat grids.** "3D globe", "Population globe" and "Climate globe" were filed under the `surface` primitive, which draws a height field on a rectangular grid — so the catalogue's own measurement showed 253 entries resolving to 15 distinct pictures, and three of them were the wrong *shape* rather than merely undifferentiated styling. A globe drawn as a plane misrepresents the thing it is named after, and no amount of restyling fixes a shape. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T099. New `globe` primitive and `places` data shape, with `lib/charts3d/globe.ts` holding the geometry as testable arithmetic: great-circle densification so a long border follows the surface instead of cutting through it, per-feature back-face culling, and separate rings so Sumatra is not joined to Java. `GENERATORS.places` is deliberately `null` — a value invented for a real country reads as a fact about the world in a way "node 7" never does — so the gallery draws the globe with no data at all, which is what a reader has to judge anyway. |
| D146 | **The globe's ocean was drawn a third of its true size, with the coastlines outside it.** The disc's radius was taken by projecting `{x:1,y:0,z:0}` and measuring back to the centre. That point lies on the silhouette only when the camera looks straight down z; at any other yaw it is foreshortened, so the sea came out at 74px on a 620px canvas while the continents drawn on the sphere spilled well past its edge. **Every geometry test passed** — the number was self-consistent and simply wrong — and one screenshot showed it. Same shape as D128 and D134: arithmetic that round-trips with itself and agrees with nothing on screen. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T099. `silhouetteRadius` derives the tangent-cone radius `d/√(d²−1)`, which depends on no rotation at all — and that rotation-independence is what the new test asserts, together with the property the formula exists to have: no visible point of the globe may fall outside the outline drawn around it, swept over 25 camera angles. Mutation-tested. Measured after: an 843×843 circle filling 92% of the canvas height. |
| D147 | **A CSS token read from JavaScript could name nothing and fail silently, and the guard against that counted the read as a definition.** `css-tokens.test.ts` treated every `"--x"` string literal in a component as *defining* `--x`, so a `getPropertyValue("--surface")` — a token this stylesheet has never had — registered as its own definition. The read returns `""`, lands in whatever `|| fallback` the caller wrote, and paints a plausible wrong colour; it is quieter than the `var()` case the file was written for, which at least drops the declaration. Found by writing the bug: a globe shading gradient whose two stops silently became the same colour, producing exactly the flat fill it was meant to replace. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T099. Reads are now stripped before the definition scan and checked against it separately, so a read can never excuse itself. Mutation-tested by reintroducing `--surface`. |
| D148 | **The workspace had one address for twenty-one screens.** The section lived in `useState<Section>("overview")` with no router, no query parameter and no storage, so four ordinary things did not work: a researcher could not send a colleague the Connections view, a reload returned them to Overview however deep they were, reopening the app did the same, and the browser's Back gesture **left the product** rather than going back one section — the only history entry was the landing page. This is **D128 one level up**: the panel layout was persisted because "dragging a divider stored a value and the next reload went back to the defaults", and the same reload discarded the researcher's *place*, which is the larger half of that complaint. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T108. The URL, not `localStorage` — storage fixes the reload and none of the rest, cannot be linked to or gone back through, and is actively wrong on a second tab where two windows fight over one key. `pushState` per navigation so Back moves between sections, a `popstate` listener so the address bar and the screen cannot disagree, and the section read inside the `useState` initialiser so a deep link paints its own section first rather than flashing Overview. **Partly unverified**: the parsing, the fallbacks and the round trip are tested and mutation-tested, and a deep link was confirmed live to survive without rewrite, but clicking the rail and pressing Back need the API running, and this machine has no PostgreSQL. |
| D149 | **Six of the seven spatial charts could be turned only with a mouse.** All seven listened for `pointerdown`; one listened for a key. Six had no `tabIndex`, so Tab could not reach them, and five had no label, so a screen reader met an unannotated canvas — a blank. This is **D127 one family over**: that defect was lists selecting a row from `onClick` with nothing focusable inside, and its guard asks every component the question rather than the five known files — but it asks about `onClick`, and a drag is not a click, so the whole spatial family sat outside it. **Worse here than it looks**: rotation is not a convenience on these charts, it is the depth cue. Motion parallax is the strongest signal a flat screen has for which mark is in front, and §10 tolerates a third dimension only where depth carries information — so a reader who cannot rotate is looking at one fixed projection of a tangle for ever, which is the picture §10 exists to forbid. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T109. One `useSpatialKeys` hook rather than six copies of the globe's handler, since six copies are six chances for the step sizes to drift and a reader who learns Left turns the network expects Left to turn the volume by as much. Verified live on all seven by pixel hash — the picture changes on the key press, not merely the attribute. |
| D150 | **Adding `tabIndex` made six canvases focusable with no visible focus ring — a defect I introduced while fixing D149.** A focusable element with no focus style is worse than an unfocusable one: a keyboard reader tabs into a chart and nothing says they are there (WCAG 2.4.7). The grab cursor and focus ring existed on `.chart-canvas`, and none of the six carried the class. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T109 in the same pass, with the class supplied by the hook so a rotatable canvas cannot get the keys without the affordance. A related claim of mine was **wrong and is worth recording**: I read `.chart-canvas.volume` as matching zero elements and nearly filed it as a dead rule — it matches `Surface.tsx` and `Volume.tsx`, which simply do not appear on `/charts-3d`, the one page I had measured. |
| D151 | **The network's only depth cue was paint order, which says nothing where marks do not overlap.** A node six units away and one two units away were drawn identically, so a spatial graph read as a flat tangle until it was moved — and `project` returns a `scale` factor documented "for scaling a mark by its distance" that nothing used. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T109 **without** binding depth to size: radius already carries node weight, and a size that means two things means neither. The recorded reason for declining was itself slightly wrong and is corrected in place — it said depth "is already carried by the projection", and the projection carries depth into a mark's *position*, not its size; the conclusion stands on the weight argument alone. Depth went on two channels carrying nothing: aerial perspective with a floor, so no mark fades out of existence, and a halo in the page colour drawn behind each node, which is what actually makes the sort visible — two circles of one colour overlapping read as a figure-of-eight, not as one in front of the other. |
| D152 | **The network layout claimed a frame budget it had never been measured against.** `DEFAULT_LAYOUT` said its pass count was "few enough to run inside a frame budget" for "a few hundred nodes", and that "a layout that took a second would be computed on a background thread". Measured, the all-pairs repulsion is exactly quadratic: 42ms at 300 nodes against a 16.7ms budget, 121ms at 500, and **1.93 seconds at 2000** — synchronous, with the interface frozen throughout, well past the one-second mark the same note said would call for a worker. A claim the data does not support, in a docstring that reads as authoritative reasoning. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T110. The claim is replaced by the measured table. Note the *neighbouring* comment was honest and was right at the time — "quadratic and fine at this scale... a graph large enough for that to hurt needs a spatial index, and building one before anything renders would be optimising a picture nobody has seen". The picture has since been seen. |
| D153 | **Barnes-Hut made the layout slower at every size a person is likely to open.** The first working version allocated thirteen typed arrays per pass — 120 passes over a thousand nodes is tens of megabytes of short-lived garbage — so the asymptotically better algorithm lost to the loop it replaced at 100 (2.8x slower), 300, 500 and 800 nodes. The tests were green throughout; only the benchmark showed it. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T110 by reusing the tree and the force vector across passes, and then by **keeping both algorithms** behind a measured crossover rather than pretending one wins everywhere. A first attempt to find that crossover was wrong and was thrown away: it compared the tree against a stripped all-pairs loop that only accumulated a scalar, which the engine optimised into something the layout never runs, and it reported "pairs win" at every size including 5000. |
| D154 | **The octree lost a body whenever a cell split.** A cell holding one body must push it down into a child when it subdivides; the first version left it recorded in the parent's totals and present in no child, so any traversal that opened that parent walked straight past it. The node repelled nothing, was repelled normally, and drifted into its neighbours — producing a completely plausible layout, which is why it is worth naming: a layout has no right answer to check by eye. | high | fixed | — | Caught before shipping by reading the insertion back, and now guarded by checking the traversal against brute force at theta 0, where the two must agree to nine decimal places. Mutation-tested: restoring the bug fails four tests. |
| D155 | **The catalogue told a reader there was no renderer for the globe, three sections below a working globe.** `standing()` fell through to "no renderer for it yet" whenever `isDrawable` was false — but `isDrawable` asks whether an *example* can be generated, not whether a renderer exists. `GENERATORS.places` is `null` deliberately, because a fabricated choropleth of the world reads as an epidemiological finding rather than as synthetic data, so all four globes reported the strongest promise as the weakest. `builtWithoutAnExample()` already named this third category and the browser did not use it — in a file whose own docstring opens by insisting that "this product can do X", "this product could do X" and "somebody has written X" are three different promises. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T111. Found by the researcher reading the rendered list, not by the suite. |
| D156 | **"Interactive globe" claimed a library the repository ships, and was the fourth globe filed under `surface`.** Its note read "a basemap and a projection, neither of which is here" — `world-atlas` is a bundled dependency, `d3-geo` projects for `Geographic`, and `Globe3D` had been drawing on the same page for two commits. T107 moved three globes off `surface` and missed this one, which is the argument for asking the question of every entry rather than of the three that were noticed. `needs-library` is the most expensive claim in the catalogue to get wrong: a reader deciding whether the tool fits their work takes it at its word and stops looking. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T111 and guarded by `catalogue-tells-the-truth.test.ts`, which checks negative claims against `package.json` rather than against the catalogue's internal consistency — the existing registry tests passed throughout, because neither entry contradicted anything *inside* the catalogue. |
| D157 | **"Molecular dynamics trajectory" said the codebase lacks Mol*, in a note reading "Mol* draws the frames".** Mol* is a dependency at ^5.11.0 and seven sibling entries already name it as their viewer. Filed under `surface` as well — a trajectory of atoms is not a height field over a grid, the same mis-primitive as the globes. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T111 to `specialist` / `mesh` / `viewer: molstar`, and the status was **checked rather than assumed**: the viewer reads `.xyz` and `.gro`, both multi-frame, and Mol* parses a multi-model file as a real trajectory. It cannot read the binary formats, so the note now names `.xtc`, `.dcd` and `.trr` explicitly instead of claiming the whole capability. |
| D158 | **All 24 remaining `needs-library` entries gave no reason at all, so the guard written one commit earlier passed over every one of them vacuously.** `catalogue-tells-the-truth` checks a `needs-library` note against `package.json` — and an entry with no note has nothing to check. The strongest negative claim the catalogue makes was being asserted 24 times without saying which library or why, which is unfalsifiable by a test, a reviewer, or the researcher deciding whether the tool does their work. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T112. Every one now states its reason, and the guard requires it — the structural fix, because a claim nobody can check is how the other two got in. Mutation-tested by stripping a note. |
| D159 | **Fifteen entries said "needs a library this codebase does not have" while the library sat in `package.json`.** `@kitware/vtk.js` is a dependency and already draws "CAD model" from `.stl`, `.obj`, `.ply` and `.vtp`. For stress on a mesh the real gap is here, not upstream: the mesh path is `mapper.setInputData(data)` with no scalar range and no legend, so the field would not be readable. Two different admissions were being made as one, and only one of them is a decision somebody else made for the reader. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T112 using `primitive-missing`, a status that already existed, was documented as "the primitive itself is not written yet", and **no entry used**. Rendered as "the renderer for this is not written yet". The page now shows 4 genuine `needs-library` rows — the physics-engine cases — where it showed 24. |
| D160 | **Five entries claimed a missing library for a plain mesh the installed viewer draws.** Urban 3D map, Human anatomy, Organ model, Brain model and Cellular model are cases where the geometry *is* the content, so the objection recorded in `specialist-vtk.test.tsx` — that an uncoloured mesh shown for "Stress visualization" would be "a placebo with a viewer around it" — does not apply: there is no solution field for the shape to be mistaken for. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T112 to `specialist` / `vtk`. The geomap test that pinned Urban 3D map was **read before being changed** and its reasoning was about *geomap* ("flipping it would be the claim this viewer cannot make"); its title, "leaves the city model to whoever wires a mesh reader", describes vtk.js exactly. It now asserts the guarantee it is entitled to — that geomap does not draw it. |
| D161 | **Every `.vtp` failed to open, with the suite green.** The three geometry readers were called through one branch — `reader.parse(bytes)` — and they do not share an interface: `vtkSTLReader` and `vtkPLYReader` expose `parse`, while `vtkXMLPolyDataReader` inherits `parseAsArrayBuffer` from `XMLReader` and has no `parse` at all. So `.vtp` died on `reader.parse is not a function` while sitting in the viewer's own accepted list, and the `.vti` branch four lines above already called `parseAsArrayBuffer` correctly. **The seam tests could not see it**: they hand the viewer a stand-in reader, and a stand-in has whatever methods it is written with. | high | fixed | — | Found by building a `.vtp` and opening it in the browser, which is the only thing that had not been tried. Guarded now against the real package's module surface rather than against a double — the base `XMLReader` defines `parseAsArrayBuffer` and no `parse` — with a companion test that STL and PLY still take `parse`, so the fix cannot over-reach. |
| D162 | **A `.vtp` carrying a field was coloured by it, wrongly, with no legend.** vtk.js leaves `scalarVisibility` on and defaults a mapper's scalar range to [0, 1]. A stress array running 0 to 240 MPa therefore clamped almost everywhere: a smooth radial gradient rendered as a flat blue sheet with one small hot spot — verified in a browser and screenshotted. It reads exactly like a solved field, and there was nothing to check it against; the caption said only "625 points, 1,152 polygons". **Worse than an uncoloured mesh, which was the assumption this rested on.** | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T113. `setScalarVisibility(false)`, so the viewer draws the shape and only the shape, and the caption now names the array and its range and says it is not mapped. `specialist-vtk` already argued the principle for the catalogue — an uncoloured mesh offered as "Stress visualization" would be "a placebo with a viewer around it" — and colouring it wrongly is that same placebo with a coat of paint. |
| D163 | **Two of the four specialist viewers never checked that the modules they name exist, and niivue checked nothing at all.** `specialist-vtk` had made the argument already — "a misspelled vtk.js path is invisible until a researcher opens the viewer: it type-checks, it bundles, and it rejects at run time inside a dynamic import nobody executes in the suite" — and the reasoning was never carried across to the other three. This is the same shape as D161: a failure that only appears when somebody opens a file. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T114 with one guard over all four viewers, mutation-tested on a plausible-but-wrong vtk path and on a renamed niivue package. It deliberately resolves **modules, not methods**: vtk.js generates most setters through macros — `setScalarVisibility` appears nowhere in `Mapper.js`, only `scalarVisibility: true` among its defaults — so a name-grepping guard would report working code as broken, and a guard that is wrong about a correct thing is the kind that gets deleted. |
| D164 | **The "flaky" tests were my own dev server.** Four timing-sensitive tests failed in one run, one taking 27 seconds against a normal 5. The machine was at a load average of 23.8 with `next-server` alone consuming 172% CPU — it had been left running and recompiling on every file edit for the whole session. With it stopped, all 2,488 pass. | low | fixed | — | The tests are genuinely wall-clock sensitive and the task to remove those assertions still stands, but the diagnosis in the earlier note was incomplete: it named the tests and not the load that triggered them. |
| D165 | **Five catalogue entries were unreachable for want of one renderer.** "Finite element analysis", "Stress visualization", "Strain visualization", "Thermal analysis" and "Pressure distribution" are not five things — they are one: a value per point on a mesh. The viewer read the `.vtp` and drew the geometry, and the field went unshown, so all five sat at "the renderer for this is not written yet". | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T115. The array is painted over **its own** range through a shared ramp, with a legend in the DOM. `useLookupTableScalarRange` is the load-bearing line: without it the mapper keeps its default window and samples the transfer function through the wrong one, which is the original [0, 1] defect wearing a lookup table. |
| D166 | **The legend contradicted the sentence directly above it.** The caption read `0 to 240` and the bar read `0` and `239.96400451660156` — one number, formatted in two places, disagreeing in the one spot a reader compares them, and claiming seventeen digits of precision the measurement does not have. Found in the browser; every test passed. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T115. The legend carries written strings rather than raw floats, produced by the same `measured()` the caption uses, so the two cannot drift. Same principle as the ramp: the bar's colours are `FIELD_RAMP` itself, not a second copy of it. |
| D167 | **A file with no field said nothing about it, under a heading that promises one.** `specialist-vtk` had named this risk precisely — a researcher who picks "Stress visualization", opens an STL of a bracket and sees an uncoloured shape has been handed "a placebo with a viewer around it" — and painting the field answers it only for files that carry one. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T115: a mesh with no per-point array now says so, and says what a field would arrive as. The objection was answered rather than waived, which is why the five entries above could move. |
| D168 | **Thirty-five network entries drew one picture.** Every entry resolving to the `network` primitive got the same 27-node blob, so "Transformer architecture", "Hierarchical network" and "Protein interaction network" were one image with three labels — the flat-globe error again, where the name denotes a definite structure and the picture does not have it. `SHAPES` had solved this for surfaces and voxels (20 named objects); nothing had been done for graphs. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T116 with `GRAPHS`, the counterpart of `SHAPES`: layered architectures, rooted trees, and preferential-attachment hubs. Tests assert the **defining property** rather than that the pictures differ — three different random blobs would also differ and be just as wrong: every architecture edge runs forward exactly one layer, every tree node but the root has one parent and there are n−1 edges, and the busiest tenth of an interaction network carries over a quarter of the connections. |
| D169 | **The layered data reached a force-directed layout, so the layers vanished from the picture.** My own fix, half-done: the architecture entries got layered data and `layoutGraph` relaxed it into the same blob, with the structure surviving in the node count and nowhere on screen. The caption read "19 nodes, 78 edges" over a tangle. **`Network3D` already accepted a `depthOf` and already called `layoutLayered` when given one** — the catalogue never passed it. | medium | fixed | — | Found by looking at the screenshot after the tests were green, on the page that exists to stop built-and-unreachable code. One line, plus a guard asserting the prop is actually handed over, since the data and the renderer were both already right and only the wiring between them was missing. |
| D170 | **The page said "The three below" above six named primitives and seven figures.** A count typed into prose beside a list that is derived — so the list grew and the word did not. This product refuses a literal number in a drafted report outright (`LiteralNumberRejected`), and its own landing page carried one in the first paragraph. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T117: the word is derived from the same array that produces the list, so the two cannot disagree. |
| D171 | **"A further 26 are drawn by a specialist library" double-counted seven entries.** "A further" asserts the sets are disjoint and they are not: a CT volume draws as voxels here *and* properly in niivue from a scan; likewise MRI, PET, Point map, Molecular surface and the two reconstructions. 216 + 26 + 4 came to 260 against a catalogue of 253. **Nothing was wrong with the data** — each number was separately correct, which is why no test saw it. The wrong thing was a word implying a partition. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T117 by dropping the claim, and guarded by asserting the property the sentence asserts rather than the numbers it prints. The guard also records the overlap as a *fact*, so the next person cannot restore the word believing the sets are disjoint. |
| D172 | **A surface with no observations described observations anyway.** One branch said "No observations are drawn on this surface", and then, unconditionally, "Faint cells have no observation near them — the smoothest part of a fitted surface is usually the part with no data under it." Vacuously true of every cell, and it reads as though some cells had data under them; worse, it describes a *fit* on a height field that was never fitted to anything. All 52 generated surfaces in the catalogue draw this way, so it was the common case rather than an edge. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T117. The sentence moved inside the branch that earns it; **no test covered it** — making it unconditional again passed all 35 conformance tests — so both branches are now asserted. |
| D173 | **Filtering the catalogue left a chart from another family on screen.** Choosing "Plane" and then filtering to "Volume" drew a Mathematical entry above thirteen volume entries that did not include it, with nothing saying they were unrelated. Two `useState` calls that never consulted each other. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T117, clearing only when the chart falls outside the chosen family — narrowing to its *own* family is a reader looking closer, and blanking it there would punish the gesture. Mutation-tested in both directions, since "always clear" passes the first test and fails the reader. |
| D174 | **Two tests failed intermittently, and my diagnosis of them was wrong twice.** I reported them as "asserting on elapsed milliseconds". Neither asserts anything about time: one thins 100,000 points and checks no `RangeError` escapes (a claim about the stack), the other restores a camera and compares positions. They were exceeding **vitest's 5s default timeout** while the suite competed with a dev server — 942ms and 367ms respectively on an idle machine, seen failing at 5.4s, 6.3s and 27s. | low | fixed | — | Fixed by T118 with explicit 30s timeouts and a note saying what the number is not. Shrinking the input was rejected: the point of a hundred thousand points is that a recursive implementation cannot survive them, so a shorter path is a weaker claim. Verified by running the full suite twice **with the stack up and load climbing to 9.5** — the condition that produced the failures. |
| D175 | **A `.vtp` with its field on the cells was told it had no values.** Only `getPointData` was read, and stress per element is the ordinary way a solver writes an FEA result — so the field was present, the picture plain, and the caption agreed with the picture rather than with the file. The mapper was also left to choose its own scalar mode, which searches for point scalars. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T118: both arrays are read, the mapper is told explicitly which to use, and the caption names whether a value is per point or per cell. Points win when a file carries both — a nodal field interpolates across each cell and an element field paints each facet flat — and the reader is told which they are looking at. Verified with a real cell-data file: 800 elements, flat per-facet shading, "one value per cell". |
| D176 | **The layout froze the interface, and the docstring had said so for weeks.** `DEFAULT_LAYOUT` promised "a layout that took a second would be computed on a background thread"; after Barnes-Hut it still took 1.4s at two thousand nodes and 6s at five thousand, synchronously — unable to scroll, resize or answer a key throughout. Half of a frozen interface is a frozen interface. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T119. A worker above 400 nodes (161ms at five hundred is where a freeze stops being a stutter); below that the round trip costs more than the work. The synchronous path is also the fallback, and it is load-bearing in two ordinary places — the test environment has no `Worker` and neither does a server render. **Verified in the browser** by instrumenting the constructor: Turbopack ships the worker as a `blob:` URL, which is why it never appeared in resource timing and why "no chunk loaded" was the wrong inference. |
| D177 | **My own hook looped for ever against the real caller.** `CatalogueChart` built its example inside render, so every pass handed down a fresh `graph` describing exactly the same thing. Keyed on that reference, the effect re-fired, set state, re-rendered and made another — an infinite loop in the browser, not merely the hung test that exposed it. The `useMemo` it replaced survived only because a memo recomputes where an effect re-fires. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T119 by keying on a rolling content signature rather than identity, and by memoizing the example — which was rebuilding a 24³ voxel grid and a whole force relaxation on every render for a result that depends only on the entry. |
| D178 | **A hook that decided during render whether a `Worker` exists broke hydration.** The server said no and the browser said yes for the same graph, so the markup disagreed and React reported a hydration failure on a page that otherwise looked fine. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T119: *what* goes across is decided by the data alone, *where* it is computed is decided after mounting. Found by reading the browser console while checking something else. |
| D179 | **An import placed above `"use client"` 500'd the page while every test passed.** A directive must be the first statement in the file; vitest does not enforce that and Turbopack does. | low | fixed | — | Fixed by T119 within a minute of making it, and recorded because it is the session's pattern in miniature: 2,512 green tests and a page that would not load. |
| D180 | **The ledger recorded work as unassessed while it was finished and tested — the §74 failure, repeating.** §54 WORKSPACE LAYOUT and §56 COMMAND PALETTE sat at `unreviewed` with eleven tests apiece behind them, and §81 REDUCED MOTION sat there with sixteen `prefers-reduced-motion` blocks in the stylesheet. The spatial-charts page **exists because of this exact defect** — its docstring says so: *"§74 and §75 were recorded as unreviewed while their code was complete and unreachable."* Every guard added this session checks the product against reality; nothing checked the *ledger* against the product. | high | fixed | — | Fixed by T120. `test_requirements_ledger` had eleven checks for the overstating direction and none for the understating one; it now refuses a row that names an implementation and still says nobody has looked. `MAX_UNREVIEWED` ratchets 144 → 137. |
| D181 | **§81 was not only unrecorded, it was incompletely implemented.** Writing the missing test found five animated classes with no reduced-motion cover, three of which genuinely moved — a treemap tile sliding to its new rectangle, a progress bar, a state label animating `transform`. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T120. **Reduce, not remove**: each keeps its change and loses its travel, and the state label keeps its colour fade. My first guard was too blunt and flagged twenty-two — every colour transition — which would have put fades on a list of accessibility defects and got the whole guard turned off. It measures motion properties now, with two exemptions declared and reasoned. |
| D182 | **§73 asked for bibliography export in one line and nothing did it.** Citations were built and *better* than the section asked — entailment checked per claim rather than asserted — while the sentence "Allow bibliography export" had no implementation anywhere. A researcher who cannot get references out of a tool retypes them, which is exactly where a citation acquires a wrong year. Recorded as the gap in T121, closed here. | medium | fixed | — | Fixed by T122. BibTeX over the papers *actually cited*, never the whole corpus — exporting everything read would pad a reference list in a way a reviewer notices. |
| D183 | **I recorded §54 WORKSPACE LAYOUT as `built` in error, while fixing this very ledger.** The row cited the resizable shell panels. §54 asks for objects to be arranged on a canvas — alignment guides, magnetic snapping, grouping, frames, layers, spatial zones, *"organize this workspace by experiment"* — and none of it exists: the board is `Board.tsx` plus `CardDetail.tsx`, with no snapping or grouping anywhere. Dragging the rail wider is not arranging a workspace. | high | fixed | — | Corrected to `not-built` by T123. Found by a reader asking whether the navigation rail has requirements behind it, which is a question nothing in the suite asks. The lesson is narrow and sharp: I matched a section by its *title* rather than reading what it demanded, two sessions after writing a guard against exactly that class of mistake. |
| D184 | **My own audit of the navigation rail was wrong in three of its findings, and I published it.** I reported Overview, Sources and Patterns as having no requirement behind them, and suggested trimming two of them. All three are specified — the spec names "Overview mode", and carries 18 mentions of import and 23 of cluster. The method was keyword probing over a run-together specification, and the probes were guesses (`project overview` instead of `overview mode`). | high | fixed | — | Corrected in T123 and here. There is a **category error** underneath the wrong details: a specification describing *capabilities* need not name a *navigation entry*, so "does this rail item have a section" was close to the wrong question. The answerable ones are: is any capability behind the rail unrequested, and is any requested capability unreachable? |
| D059 | **The Windows launcher opened a window and closed it again, with no message.** `Throughline.bat` ran and vanished — the hardest failure to report, and the cause was a comment. `rem` in cmd is **not** an inert comment: the parser still expands `%` substitutions on the line. A `rem` explaining that *"`%~dp` on a URL yields a filesystem path"* contained a bare `%~dp` — a parameter operator with no argument number — which is a **fatal syntax error**, not an ignored one. `%~dp0` is fine; `%~dp` ends the script. **Written by me while fixing D050**, and it survived because no test runs a batch file and CI cannot reach Windows (D034). | high | done | Claude (Fenil) | Diagnosed by running the downloaded `.bat` on real Windows through WSL interop, which printed *"The following usage of the path operator in batch-parameter substitution is invalid"* — invisible on a double-click because the window closes on the error. After the fix, the same file downloaded from the live host installed `1caf2a0`, verified the checksum, applied all 39 migrations and reached package installation. Evidence: `test_no_comment_in_the_batch_file_contains_an_invalid_substitution`, **mutation-checked** by restoring the comment. It requires every `%~` to be followed by an argument number, anywhere in the file, comments included. |
| D060 | **The one-liner cannot be universal, and the page implied it was.** Pasted into PowerShell it fails with *The term 'sh' is not recognized* — a fair thing to try, since the page offered one line and never said which shells it was for. There is no `sh` on a stock Windows and no single literal line can span both families. | medium | done | Claude (Fenil) | Added `scripts/install.ps1`, so the shape is universal even though the line is not: `curl ... | sh` for macOS/Linux/WSL, `irm ... | iex` for PowerShell — the pair rustup, deno and uv all ship. It is thin by design: it finds a Python and hands over to `install.py`, the one implementation of download-verify-unpack, rather than becoming a third copy of the install sequence. **Driven on real Windows against the live host**: fetched the installer, verified the checksum, installed `1caf2a0`. That run also caught a genuine PowerShell bug — `& $Python` where `$Python` was the array `@('py','-3')` makes PowerShell look for a command literally named *"py -3"*; the arguments have to be splatted. Guessed wrong, measured right. Evidence: `test_both_shell_families_get_a_one_liner`, `test_the_powershell_installer_is_published`, and the User-Agent guard extended to PowerShell's `-UserAgent` idiom. |
